Classification of marked elliptic root systems with non-reduced quotient Thanks: This research was partly supported through the program "Oberwolfach Research Fellows" by the Mathematisches Forschungsinstitut Oberwolfach in 2023. Y .S. is partially supported by JSPS KAKENHI Grant Numbers JP20K03568 and JP25K06942.
A. Fialowski, K. Iohara and Y. Saito

Abstract
. K. Saito (Publ. RIMS 21 (1), 1985, 75-179) has introduced a class of root systems called elliptic root systems which lie in the real vector space F F with a metric I I whose signature is of type ( l , 2 , 0 ) (l,2,0) . He also classified the pair ( R , G ) (R,G) of elliptic root systems R R with one dimensional subspaces G G of the radical of I I , under the assumption that the quotient root system R / G R/G is reduced. Here, we classify the pairs ( R , G ) (R,G) where R / G R/G is non-reduced.



[name=notations, title=List of Notations]
[name=index, title=Index]

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 17B22; Secondary 08A35, 20F55

To the memory of Professor Kenji Iohara
(
1970-2025
)
1
1

1

We are
deeply shocked that our good friend and collaborator, a bright and extremely productive
mathematician and wonderful person, Kenji Iohara passed away on October 1, 2025
at the age of 55.
.

Introduction

The notion of root system of a Lie algebra has a long history. Originally it was introduced by W. Killing
[
Kil88
,
Kil89a
,
Kil89b
,
Kil90
]
in 1888, in order to classify all simple finite dimensional Lie algebras over
\mathbb{C}
. His work was continued and completed by É. Cartan
[
Car94
]
in 1894. In the early 1930s, H.S.M. Coxeter
[
Cox34
]
initiated the study of discrete groups generated by reflections, which are now called Coxeter groups. Then comes a miraculous year 1934-35
2
2

2

See, e.g., MacTutor:
https://mathshistory.st-andrews.ac.uk
.
; Coxeter visited Princeton and attended lectures by H. Weyl. They soon realized a surprising connection between Coxeter’s finite groups and Weyl’s infinite groups, i.e., Coxeter’s groups show up as the Weyl group of root systems. At the same time, P. DuVal had been visiting Princeton and studied isolated rational surface singularities
[
DV34a
,
DV34b
,
DV34c
]
. They found a curious resemblance between Coxeter graphs and what are today called simple singularities. Hence, a fabulous discovery of connections between Lie groups, Coxeter groups and simple singularities happened in 1934 !
In 1970 at the ICM Congress in Nice, E. Brieskorn
[
Bri71
]
showed,
that simple singularities of type A, D, E and their semi-universal deformations can be realized purely in terms of simple algebraic groups of type A, D, E over an algebraically closed field with good characteristic. Later P. Slodowy
[
Slo80a
,
Slo80b
]
gave an accessible account of the Brieskorn theory and slightly generalized it, in particular, he also described them in terms of simple Lie algebras.

H. Coxeter studied properties of a product of generators (cf.
[
Cox51
]
), which plays a prominent role in invariant theory. Any such product is now called a Coxeter transformation. For a simple finite dimensional Lie algebra, B. Kostant
[
Kos59
]
established a direct relationship between its principal simple 3-dimensional subalgebra, its adjoint representation, and the Coxeter transformation. For more historical account for finite root systems and Coxeter transformations see
[
Bou81
]
. We note that the finiteness of the order of a Coxeter transformation plays an important role in the geometry of isolated surface singularities.

There is a natural extension of a finite Weyl group, which is a semi-direct product of
some lattice and a finite Weyl group. Such a group is called an affine Weyl group.
In 1965, N. Iwahori and H. Matsumoto
[
IM65
]
found that an affine
Weyl group can be realized as the Weyl group of a Chevalley group over a
p
-adic field.
A few years later, V. Kac
[
Kac69
]
and R. Moody
[
Moo69
]
invented a new
class of infinite dimensional Lie algebras, called affine Lie algebras, whose Weyl group is
an affine Weyl group. An affine Lie algebra can also be defined with a generalized Cartan
matrix, introduced by R. Moody
[
Moo68
]
, and their complete classification was
achieved by E.B.Vinberg
[
Vin71
]
. A. J. Coleman
[
Col89
]
studied
Coxeter transformations for a wider class of Lie algebras, including the affine ones.
Note that for an affine Weyl group, the Coxeter transformation is of infinite order.
There are also other approaches to the classification of affine root systems, like one in
relation with the classification of reductive groups over a local field by F. Bruhat and J. Tits

[
BT72
]
, another in relation with the denominator identity and
powers of the Dedekind
\eta
-function by I. Macdonald
[
Mac72
]
.
Both classifications give the same result, and the complete classification was given in
Tits’ paper
[
Tit79
]
. A remarkable feature of affine Lie algebras is that the
character of their integrable highest weight representations can be described in terms of
the Dedekind
\eta
-function and
\theta
-functions, as was studied by V. Kac and D.
Peterson
[
KP84
]
.
This phenomenon was one of the key ingredients in the development
of theoretical physics such as conformal field theory.

In 1974 K. Saito
[
Sai74
]
studied and classified simple elliptic singularities.
Inspired by Brieskorn’s theory,he
initiated
a research on elliptic root systems in a series of papers from the middle of 1980s (cf.
[
Sai85
]
).
An elliptic root system (called extended affine root system) is a generalized root system realized in
a real vector space of finite dimension equipped with a symmetric bilinear form
I
of signature

(l,2,0)
. He gave the classification of marked elliptic root systems
(R,G)
, which are pairs of
an elliptic root system
R
and a
1
-dimensional subspace
G
of the radical of
I
,
under the condition that the quotient root system
R/G
is a
reduced affine root system. To each pair
(R,G)
, he attached a finite oriented diagram,
called an elliptic diagram (first called an elliptic Dynkin diagram), and defined an element of the Weyl group, called
a Coxeter element.

The important part of this theory is that a Coxeter element here is an element of the Weyl group of finite order. Since then Saito has been added new ideas to his theory in a series of papers, like for example the structure of Weyl groups
[
ST97
]
or
\eta
-product
[
Sai01
]
. For each marked elliptic root system
(R,G)
, U. Pollmann, in her master thesis
[
Pol94
]
, showed that there exists a Lie algebra whose real root system is isomorphic to
R
. Indeed, she constructed such a Lie algebra via the twisted construction of the pair
(\mathfrak{g},\sigma)
of an affine Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
with its finite order diagram automorphism
\sigma
admitting a fixed point. She also examined
6
other cases when the finite order diagram automorphisms do not have a fixed point; there are
2
cases, whose real root systems do not correspond to those in the list of K. Saito
[
Sai85
]
. Here, we shall mention that Allison et al.
[
ABP14
]
studied the extended affine Lie algebras (cf.
[
AAB+97
]
) with nullity
2
in 2014, which are naturally related to the above Lie algebras. Apparently, such Lie algebras contain certain class of Lie algebras graded by a finite root system, among which
BC_{l}
-graded cases were studied in
[
ABG02
]
for
l\geq 2
and in
[
BS03
]
for
l=1
.
However, it is not so clear whether these Lie algebras and the root systems considered in this memoir are related or not.

Beside these works, we mention 3 other directions of elliptic root systems.
Firstly, Y. Billig
[
Bil99
]
and K. Iohara, Y. Saito and M. Wakimoto

[
ISW99a
,
ISW99b
]
in 1999 obtained
soliton equations arriving from a representation of the Lie algebras of type

A_{l}^{(1,1)},D_{l}^{(1,1)},E_{6}^{(1,1)},E_{7}^{(1,1)}
and
E_{8}^{(1,1)}
.
The other direction is the work of Helmke and P. Slodowy in 2004

[
HS04
]
in which they show the connections of loop groups and
elliptic Lie algebras via principle bundles over an elliptic curve, in view of simply elliptic
singularities (see
[
HS05
]
for an overview).
The third direction is elliptic Hecke algebras by Y. Saito and M. Shiota in 2009

[
SS09
]
.
They studied the connection with the double affine Hecke algebra, which plays an
important role to analyze Macdonald’s polynomials (cf.
[
Mac03
]
).

In this article, we complete the classification of marked elliptic root systems
(R,G)
, namely, we classify the so far missing cases where the quotient root system
R/G
is a non-reduced affine root system. We found 6 series of new reduced marked elliptic root systems (see Theorem
4.1
, and Section
10.1
for their root data), among which 4 have been already found by K. Saito
[
Sai85
]
, namely
BC_{l}^{(1,2)},BC_{l}^{(4,2)},BC_{l}^{(2,2)\sigma}(1)
and
BC_{l}^{(2,2)\sigma}(2)
, but with different marking (see §
6.1
) and 34 series and 1 exceptional new non-reduced marked elliptic root systems (see Theorem
4.2
and Sections
10.2
,
10.3
,
10.4
, and
10.5
for their root data). It turns out that the
2
missing cases in Pollmann’s construction mentioned above, correspond to the
2

newly found reduced marked elliptic root systems
(R,G)
with non-reduced affine quotient
R/G
!

The logical dependencies of the main results of this memoir can be described as follows.
Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
provide a list of marked elliptic root systems with non-reduced affine quotient, whose proofs are given by gluing K. Saito’s marked elliptic root systems, thus their explicit construction is given. At this moment, we don’t state whether any two marked elliptic root systems in the above are non-isomorphic as marked root systems or not. In Theorems
6.1
and
6.2
, we classify the isomorphism classes of the marked elliptic root systems presented in the above two theorems, as root systems.
These last theorems allow us to compare the list of our elliptic root systems with those obtained in
[
Aza02
]
and
[
AKY05
]
, as stated in Remark
6.5
.
After the introduction of the notion of elliptic diagram in §
8.1
, where its well-definedness is assured in Proposition
8.2
, we again classify the marked elliptic root systems with non-reduced affine quotient (cf. Corollary
8.13
) via their diagrams in Theorem
8.12
. This latter theorem states possible marked elliptic root systems up to isomorphism, as marked root systems, but their existence is not discussed.
Comparing this last theorem with Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
, where an explicit construction of each marked elliptic root system is given, we obtain the classification of marked elliptic root systems with non-reduced affine quotient. Thus, our results together with Theorem
2.9
, due to K. Saito
[
Sai85
]
,
allow us to conclude the complete classification of marked elliptic root systems. The explicit root data are given in Chapter 4.

The structure of the paper is the following.
In Chapter
I
, we provide a brief review of the known theory on elliptic root systems.
In Section
1
we recall the basic definitions. In Section
2
we recall the technical tools necessary for the classification of reduced marked elliptic root systems from K.Saito. In particular, we present his classification with details of the proof to show how it works, and obtain the classification of marked elliptic root systems
(R,G)
for which its quotient
R/G
is reduced. For later use, we describe the structure of the group of automorphisms of a marked elliptic root systems
(R,G)
in Section
3
.

In Chapter
II
, we provide the list of all marked elliptic root systems
(R,G)
whose affine quotient
R/G
is non-reduced.
In Section
4
we describe all the possible marked elliptic root systems
(R,G)
with non-reduced affine quotient
R/G
,
and state a weak classification theorem, namely, a theorem which states that any such
(R,G)
is isomorphic to one of those presented in this section. Section
5
is devoted to the proof of these theorems. In Section
6
,
we discuss the isomorphism classes of the elliptic root systems, as root systems. For each marked elliptic root system
(R,G)
,
possible affine quotients of
R
(not necessarily by
G
) are also given.

In Chapter
III
, we prove that any two of the marked elliptic root systems
(R,G)
presented in Chapter
II
are non isomorphic.
For this purpose, another proof of the classification theorem of the marked elliptic root systems, that is completely independent of the previous one, is given, which allows us to associate each marked elliptic root system to a unique diagram. Section
7
is a preparation for the proof of these theorems and in Section
8
, a strong classification theorem is given.
Chapter
10
is the summary of the root systems we obtained in this
article, namely, we collect the root data of the newly obtained marked elliptic root
systems in Sections
10.1
-
10.5
. We
conclude this article with three Appendices
A
,

B
and
C
, where we collect
root data for the finite, affine root systems and marked elliptic root systems with reduced
affine quotient.

The present article is a first step in the study of (marked) elliptic root systems with
non-reduced affine quotients. Therefore, there are many problems that need to be
studied. For example, the following topics should be considered:

(i)

Coxeter elements for a (marked) elliptic root system
(R,G)
with
non-reduced affine quotients;

(ii)

detailed structure of the Weyl group
W(R)
and the group
\mathrm{Aut}(R)

of automorphisms for such an
(R,G)
;

(iii)

invariant theory for such an
(R,G)
.

For (i) and (ii), the results will appear in our forthcoming paper (cf. Remark

8.16
below). For (iii), we have to discuss the relationship between
our results and those of Looijenga
and van der Lek.
Motivated by his work on semi-universal deformations of simple
elliptic singularities, Looijenga introduced an
extended Coxeter group

\widetilde{W}
which is a semidirect product of a Coxeter group
W
and
a root lattice
Q
, and a certain tube domain
\Omega
which has a natural action of

\widetilde{W}
(
[
Loo76
]
,
[
Loo80
]
).
When
W
is an affine Weyl group, the orbit space
\Omega/\widetilde{W}

appears also in K. Saito’s theory by using the language of marked elliptic root
systems with reduced affine quotients (see
[
Sai90
]
)
3
3

3

In
[
Sai90
]
,
this orbit space is denoted by
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}/\widetilde{W}_{R}
.
The method for identifying these two spaces is not
necessarily obvious. However, since it is not relevant to the results of this paper,
we will omit to give it in detail.
. After Looijenga’s work, H. van der Lek
computed the fundamental group of the regular
\widetilde{W}
-orbit space of

\Omega
(see
[
Lek83
]
).

Based on our results in this article, the invariant theory and the study on Artin group for

(R,G)
should be extended to the case when
(R,G)
has non-reduced affine
quotient. We believe that this is an important problem in this area, and deserves
future research.

Acknowledgement
.

The authors are grateful to the referees for their valuable comments.

Part I
Marked elliptic root systems with reduced quotient

In 1985, K. Saito
[
Sai85
]
introduced what one calls now elliptic root systems (
né
extended affine root systems).
The main purpose of this chapter is to provide a brief review of elliptic root systems and some of the key concepts, among which marking, tier numbers, counting sets and counting numbers play a key role in the main part of this article. In particular, the classification theorem of marked elliptic root systems with reduced affine quotient is given, to show how the concepts like counting numbers work in the classification.

In Section
1
, we recall the notion of generalized root system, state the relations between finite, affine and elliptic root systems, and present the finite root system of type
BC_{l}
, since this is one of the main objects in this article. The Dynkin diagrams of finite and affine root systems are also reviewed. Section
2
is devoted to several concepts in relation with marking such as tier numbers, countings and elliptic diagrams. In particular, we state the classification theorem of marked elliptic root systems with reduced affine quotient and give a proof of it.
In Section
3
, we determine
the structure of the automorphism groups of marked elliptic root systems with reduced
affine quotient. This results will be used in Part 2, for studying marked elliptic root
systems with non-reduced affine quotient.

1.
Generalized Root Systems

1.1.
General framework

Recall some important notions from K Saito
[
Sai85
]
.

Let
(F,I)

be a pair of a vector space
F
over
\mathbb{R}
and a symmetric bilinear form of finite rank
I:F\times F\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with signature
(\mu_{+},\mu_{0},\mu_{-})
. For a nonisotropic vector
\alpha\in F
(i.e.
I(\alpha,\alpha)\neq 0
), define its
dual
by

\alpha^{\vee}=\frac{2}{I(\alpha,\alpha)}\alpha

and the
reflection

r_{\alpha}\in\mathrm{O}(F,I)
by

r_{\alpha}(\lambda)=\lambda-I(\alpha^{\vee},\lambda)\alpha

where
\mathrm{O}(F,I):={\{\,g\in GL(F)\,|\,I\circ g=I\,\}}
is the orthogonal group of
the metric
I
. In this article, we use the word
dual
to distinguish from the
preposition
co
which indicates an object in the dual vector space.

Definition 1.1
.

A subset
R\subset F\setminus\{0\}
is called a (generalized)

root system
belonging to
(F,I)
if it satisfies the following:

(R1)

the subgroup of
F
generated by
R
,
Q(R):={\mathbb{Z}}R
(the root lattice of
R
) is a full lattice in
F
, i.e.,
\mathbb{R}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Q(R)\cong F
,

(R2)

for any
\alpha\in R
,
I(\alpha,\alpha)\neq 0
,

(R3)

for any
\alpha\in R
,
r_{\alpha}(R)=R
,

(R4)

for any
\alpha,\beta\in R
,
I(\alpha^{\vee},\beta)\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(R5)

irreducibility: if there is a decomposition
R=R_{1}\amalg R_{2}
and
R_{1}\perp R_{2}
with respect to
I
for some subsets
R_{i}\subset R\;(i=1,2)
, then either
R_{1}=\emptyset
or
R_{2}=\emptyset
.

It is clear that if
R
is a root system belonging to
(F,I)
, then so does its
dual root system

R^{\vee}:=\{\alpha^{\vee}\,|\,\alpha\in R\}
.

The condition (R3) implies that
\alpha\in F
belongs to
R
iff so does

-\alpha
. From condition (R4) it follows that if
\alpha\in R
and
c\alpha\in R

for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
, then
c\in\{\pm\frac{1}{2},\pm 1,\pm 2\}
.
A root system
R
is said to be

reduced

if
\alpha\in R
and
c\alpha\in R
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
implies
c\in\{\pm 1\}
. Otherwise,
R
is said to be
non-reduced
.

The subgroup
W(R)
of
\mathrm{O}(F,I)
generated by the
reflections
r_{\alpha}
for

\alpha\in R
is called the
Weyl group
of the root
system
R
. The reflections are clearly the same for the dual root system.

Two root systems
R_{1}\subset(F_{1},I_{1})
and
R_{2}\subset(F_{2},I_{2})
are
isomorphic
iff there exists a linear isomorphism

\varphi:F_{1}\rightarrow F_{2}
such that
\varphi(R_{1})=R_{2}
.
For a root system
R\subset(F,I)
, a linear isomorphism

\varphi\in GL(F)
is called an
automorphism
of
R
, if

\varphi(R)=R
. The group of automorphisms of
R
is denoted by

\mathrm{Aut}(R)
.
The following lemma is due to K. Saito.

Lemma 1.1

(
[
Sai85
]
)
.

The group
\mathrm{Aut}(R)
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{O}(F,I)
.

In this article, we sometimes denote
I_{F}
in place of
I
to indicate the underlying vector space
F
on which the symmetric bilinear form
I
is defined.

We consider the case when
\mu_{-}=0
.

(1)

the case
\mu_{0}=0
corresponds to the
finite (classical) root systems
;
this means there are finitely many roots and the Weyl group is finite,

(2)

the case
\mu_{0}=1
corresponds to the
affine root systems
,

(3)

the case
\mu_{0}=2
corresponds to the so-called
elliptic root systems
.

In such a case, it follows that

I(\alpha^{\vee},\beta)\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[-4,4]\qquad\forall\;\alpha,\beta\in R.

1.2.
Dynkin diagrams: finite and affine cases

Let
R
be a finite or affine root system. There exists a minimal generating system
\Pi\subset R
of
F
such that for any root
\alpha=\sum_{\beta\in\Pi}c_{\beta}\beta\in R
, either
c_{\beta}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
for all
\beta\in\Pi
or
c_{\beta}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
for all
\beta\in\Pi
holds. For a finite root system, any such system
\Pi^{\prime}

is
W(R)
-conjugate to
\Pi
and for an affine root system
\Pi^{\prime}
is either in
W(R).\Pi
or in
W(R).(-\Pi)
. Fix such a system
\Pi
and call it a

simple system
of
R
. One can associate
\Pi
with
a graph, called the
Dynkin diagram

\Gamma
.

Definition 1.2
.

A
Dynkin diagram

\Gamma
of a (finite or affine) root system
R
is a graph whose set of nodes
|\Gamma|
consists of
\alpha\in\Pi
and is represented by

(Node1)

\alpha

if
\alpha\in R
but
2\alpha\not\in R
and

(Node2)

\alpha

if
\alpha\in R
and
2\alpha\in R
,

and for two vertices
\alpha,\beta\in\Pi
,
we connect them by a bond with arrows according to the following rules:
for
\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.04544pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#CCCCCC} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.94 0 C 3.94 2.17 2.17 3.94 0 3.94 C -2.17 3.94 -3.94 2.17 -3.94 0 C -3.94 -2.17 -2.17 -3.94 0 -3.94 C 2.17 -3.94 3.94 -2.17 3.94 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(Edge0)

\alpha
\beta

if
I(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad I(\beta,\alpha^{\vee})=0
,

(Edge1)

\alpha
\beta

if
I(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})=I(\beta,\alpha^{\vee})=-1
,

(Edge2)

\alpha
t
\beta

if
I(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})=-1
and
I(\beta,\alpha^{\vee})=-t
for
t=2,3,4
and

(Edge3)

\alpha
\infty
\beta

if
I(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})=I(\beta,\alpha^{\vee})=-2
.

Some necessary data about the finite and affine root systems are recalled in
the Appendices
A
and
B
, respectively.

For any root system
R
, let us denote the subset of short roots, middle length roots
and long roots by
R_{s}
,

R_{m}
and

R_{l}
, respectively.
For a non-reduced root system
R
, let
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and

R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
be the root subsystems of indivisible roots
and non-multiplicable roots of
R
, respectively (cf.
[
Mac72
]
), that is,

R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}:=\left\{\alpha\in R\,\left|\,\frac{1}{2}\alpha\not\in R\right\},\right.\qquad R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}:=\{\alpha\in R\,|\,2\alpha\not\in R\}.

We call the pair
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})

the
reduced pair
of
R
.
It can be shown that both
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
are root subsystems of
R
, and the
Weyl groups
W(R),W(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})
and
W(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
are the same.

1.3.
Root system of type
BC_{l}
(
l\geq 1
)

In this subsection, we present root data of the finite root system
R_{f}
of type
BC_{l}

for reader’s convenience. We realize
R_{f}
in the real vector space
F_{f}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{R}\varepsilon_{i}
equipped with the symmetric bilinear form
I_{F_{f}}
satisfying

(1)

I_{F_{f}}(\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j})=\delta_{i,j}\qquad 1\leq i,j\leq l.

As is given in §
A.2.1
, the root system
R_{f}
is the union of the set of short roots
(R_{f})_{s}
, middle length roots
(R_{f})_{m}
and long roots
(R_{f})_{l}
, given by

\displaystyle(R_{f})_{s}=

\displaystyle\{\,\pm\varepsilon_{i}\,|\,1\leq i\leq l\,\},

\displaystyle(R_{f})_{m}=

\displaystyle\{\,\pm(\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j})\,|\,1\leq i<j\leq l\,\},

\displaystyle(R_{f})_{l}=

\displaystyle\{\,\pm 2\varepsilon_{i}\,|\,1\leq i\leq l\,\}.

Notice that
(R_{f})_{m}=\emptyset
if
l=1
.
For
l>1
,
(R_{f})^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=(R_{f})_{s}\cup(R_{f})_{m}
is a root system of type
B_{l}
and
(R_{f})^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}=(R_{f})_{m}\cup(R_{f})_{l}
is a root system of type
C_{l}
. It should also be mentioned that
(R_{f})_{m}
is a root system of type
D_{l}
. Here and in the rest of this article, the root system of type
D_{l}
is defined for
l\geq 2
as follows:

R(D_{l})=\{\,\pm(\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j})\;(1\leq i<j\leq l)\,\}.

In particular, we choose its simple roots and its fundamental dual weights as usual:

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\;\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{i+1}\;,&1\leq i<l,\\
\;\varepsilon_{l-1}+\varepsilon_{l}\;&i=l,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\varpi_{i}^{\vee}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\;\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{i}\;&1\leq i\leq l-2,\\
\;\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{l-1}-\varepsilon_{l})\;&i=l-1,\\
\;\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{l-1}+\varepsilon_{l})\;&i=l.\end{cases}

The rank
l
of a root system of type
D_{l}
is usually assumed to be
l\geq 4
.
It is known that

R(D_{2})\cong R(A_{1})\times R(A_{1}),\qquad R(D_{3})\cong R(A_{3})

as abstract root systems. The Weyl group of type
D_{l}
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{S}_{l}\ltimes(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{l-1}
for
l\geq 4
. For
l=2
and
3
, we have

\displaystyle W(D_{2})\cong W(A_{1})\times W(A_{1})\cong\mathfrak{S}_{2}\times\mathfrak{S}_{2}\left(\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})\times(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})\right),

\displaystyle W(D_{3})\cong W(A_{3})\cong\mathfrak{S}_{4}.

It is clear that the Weyl group
W(R_{f})
is isomorphic to the Weyl group of type
B_{l}
and
C_{l}
, that is,
\mathfrak{S}_{l}\ltimes(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{l}
for
l\geq 2
, and for
l=1
it is isomorphic to the Weyl group of type
A_{1}
.

Set
\alpha_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{i+1}
for
1\leq i<l
and
\alpha_{l}=\varepsilon_{l}
. Then
\Pi_{f}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}:=\{\alpha_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq l}
is a simple system of
(R_{f})^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and
\Pi_{f}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}:=\{\alpha_{i}\}_{1\leq i<l}\cup\{2\alpha_{l}\}
is a simple system of
R_{f}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
. Notice that

(\Pi_{f}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})^{\vee}:=\{\,\alpha^{\vee}\,|\,\alpha\in\Pi_{f}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\,\}=\Pi_{f}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(\Pi_{f}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})^{\vee}:=\{\,\alpha^{\vee}\,|\,\alpha\in\Pi_{f}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\,\}=\Pi_{f}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}.

The simple system
\Pi_{f}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
is also a simple system of
R_{f}
, which we also denote by
\Pi_{f}
.
Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}=

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{l},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}=

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{j-1},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}=

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+1}\cdots+\alpha_{j-1}+2(\alpha_{j}+\alpha_{j+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{l}),

and the root lattice
Q(R_{f})
of
R_{f}
, which is generated by the elements of
R_{f}
, is indeed generated by
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq l}
:

Q(R_{f})=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Pi_{f}}\mathbb{Z}\alpha.

As for the dual roots, it follows that

(\varepsilon_{i})^{\vee}=2\varepsilon_{i},\qquad(\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j})^{\vee}=\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j},\qquad(2\varepsilon_{i})^{\vee}=\varepsilon_{i},

in particular, one has
R_{f}^{\vee}=R_{f}
. Hence, the dual root lattice
Q(R_{f}^{\vee})
and the root lattice
Q(R_{f})
are the same. Since,
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}=\alpha_{i}
(
1\leq i<l
) and
\alpha_{l}^{\vee}=2\alpha_{l}
, we have

Q(R_{f}^{\vee})=\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{1}^{\vee}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{2}^{\vee}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{l-1}^{\vee}\oplus\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{l}^{\vee},

i.e., the dual roots
\{\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\}_{1\leq i\leq l}

do not
generate
Q(R_{f}^{\vee})
.
The lattice generated by the dual roots
\{\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\}_{1\leq i\leq l}
is the root lattice of type
C_{l}
.

Recall that the fundamental weights
\varpi_{i}
(resp. dual weights
\varpi_{i}^{\vee}
) (
1\leq i\leq l
) are the dual base of simple dual roots
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
(resp. roots
\alpha_{i}
) (
1\leq i\leq i
). Explicitly, they are given by

\varpi_{i}=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{i}\qquad&1\leq i<l,\\
\frac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{l})\quad&i=l,\end{cases}\qquad\varpi_{i}^{\vee}=\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{i}\;(1\leq i\leq l).

In particular, the weight lattice
P(R_{f})
, which is the set of all
\lambda\in F_{f}
with
I_{F_{f}}(\lambda,R_{f}^{\vee})\subset\mathbb{Z}
, does not contain
\varpi_{l}
:

P(R_{f})=\mathbb{Z}\varpi_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\varpi_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}\varpi_{l-1}\oplus 2\mathbb{Z}\varpi_{l},

and the lattice generated by
\{\varpi_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq l}
is the weight lattice of type
B_{l}
.
The dual weight lattice
P(R_{f}^{\vee})
, which is the set of all
\lambda\in F_{f}
with
I_{F_{f}}(\lambda,R_{f})\subset\mathbb{Z}
, is indeed generated by
\{\varpi_{i}^{\vee}\}_{1\leq i\leq l}
:

P(R_{f}^{\vee})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{Z}\varpi_{i}^{\vee}.

This lattice is the weight lattice of type
C_{l}
.

Remark 1.2
.

(1)

With the normalization (
1
) of
I_{F_{f}}
in this section,
all the
4
lattices
Q(R_{f}),\,Q(R_{f}^{\vee}),P(R_{f})
and
P(R_{f}^{\vee})
coincide with

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{Z}\varepsilon_{i}.

(2)

In general, we only have

P(R_{f})=Q(R_{f})
,
P(R_{f}^{\vee})=Q(R_{f}^{\vee})
and
Q(R_{f}^{\vee})\cong Q(R_{f})
.

2.
Marked elliptic root systems

Let
(F,I_{F})
be an
\mathbb{R}
-vector space with a symmetric bilinear form
I_{F}
on it.

First we
introduce the notion of a marking
G
which is a certain linear subspace of
F
, and
then we shall restrict ourselves to the elliptic case, i.e., to those where the signature of

I_{F}
is
(l,2,0)
for some
l>0
.

2.1.
Marking

We say that a subspace
G
of
F
is defined over

{\mathbb{Z}}
if we have

\text{rank}_{\mathbb{R}}G=\text{rank}_{\mathbb{Z}}\left(G\cap Q(R)\right).

The radical

\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}):=\{x\in F\,|\,I_{F}(x,y)=0\quad\forall\;y\in F\}

is defined over
\mathbb{Z}
, i.e.,

\operatorname{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})=\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\cap Q(R)

is a full lattice of
\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
.
A subspace
G
of
\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
is called a
marking
of
R

if it is defined over

{\mathbb{Z}}
. Let
G
be a marking, and
\pi_{G}:F\rightarrow F/G
the canonical projection.
It induces a bilinear form
I_{F/G}
on
F/G

defined by
I_{F/G}(\pi_{G}(x),\pi_{G}(y))=I_{F}(x,y)
for

x,y\in F
. The image of
R
in
F/G
, denoted by

R/G
,
is a root system called the

quotient root system of
R
by
G
.
It is a root system belonging to
(F/G,I_{F/G})
.

The pair
(R,G)
of a root system and a subspace of
\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
defined over
{\mathbb{Z}}
is
called a
marked root system
. Two marked root systems
(R_{1},G_{1})
with

R_{1}\subset(F_{1},I_{1})
and
(R_{2},G_{2})
with
R_{2}\subset(F_{2},I_{2})
are

isomorphic
iff there exists a linear isomorphism
\varphi:F_{1}\rightarrow F_{2}

such that
\varphi(R_{1})=R_{2}
and
\varphi(G_{1})=G_{2}
.
In particular,
(R,G)
is called a

marked elliptic root system
if

\dim G=1
.

Remark 2.1
.

(1)

For a marked root system
(R,G)
, its quotient
R/G
is still a generalized root
system
(
cf. §1.8 in
[
Sai85
]
)
. In particular, for any marked elliptic root
system
(R,G)
, its quotient root system is an affine root system, if
G
is of rank

1
.

(2)

Note that, if the quotient
R/G
is reduced, then so is
R
. Indeed, assume
R

is non-reduced. Then there is a root

\alpha\in R
such that
r\alpha\in R
for
r=\pm 2,\pm 1/2
. Taking their
images under the canonical projection
\pi_{G}:F\to F/G
, it follows that
there exists a root
\pi_{G}(\alpha)\in R/G
such that

r(\pi_{G}(\alpha))=\pi_{G}(r\alpha)\in R/G
. That is,
R/G
is non-reduced.

(3)

Therefore, for any marked root system
(R,G)
, one of the
following three holds:

(i)

both
R
and
R/G
are reduced;

(ii)

R
is reduced and
R/G
is non-reduced;

(iii)

both
R
and
R/G
are non-reduced.

In this article we classify marked elliptic root systems

(R,G)
with non-reduced quotient
R/G
. K. Saito
[
Sai85
]
has already classified such root systems in the case
when the quotient root system
R/G
is reduced.
Hence, this gives a complete classification result. For short, we will abbreviate
marked elliptic root system
to
mERS
.
Here and after, for
\alpha\in R
,
we sometimes denote
\overline{\alpha}=\pi_{G}(\alpha)
for simplicity.

Let
(R,G)
be a mERS.

Definition 2.1
.

A set
\Pi\subset R
of roots
is called a
simple system
of a
marked elliptic root system
(R,G)
, if its image under the canonical projection

\pi_{G}:F\to F/G
is a simple system of the quotient affine root system
R/G
.

K. Saito
[
Sai85
]
showed, that if
R/G
is reduced, then for any two
simple systems
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
of
(R,G)
, there exists an automorphism
\varphi
of

(R,G)
such that
\varphi(\Pi_{1})=\Pi_{2}
(see
[
Sai85
]
, (6.2)
Corollary, for details). That is, a simple system
\Pi
of
(R,G)

is unique up to automorphism of
(R,G)
, if
R/G
is reduced. In
his proof, the reducedness of
R/G
is essentially used. Here and after,
we denote
\Pi_{G}:=\pi_{G}(\Pi)
.

On the other hand, if
R/G
is non-reduced, the situation is more
complicated. For a non-reduced mERS
(R,G)
belonging to
(F,I_{F})
,
let
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
be the reduced pair of
R
and
((R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
of the
quotient affine root system
R/G
. Since both
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},G)
and
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}},G)
are
(reduced) mERSs, their quotients
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G
and
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G
are
affine.
However, since
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G
and
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G
are
not necessarily reduced, they are again the union of their reduced parts:

R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G=(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}{/}G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cup(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}},\quad R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G=(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cup(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}.

Hence, there are six reduced affine root subsystems

(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\ (R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}},\ (R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\ (R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}},\ (R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\ (R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}

of
R/G
belonging to
(F/G,I_{F/G})
. A natural question now is the following:

Question 1
.

What are the explicit relationships between these six affine root systems ?

A partial answer for this question will be given in

\lx@sectionsign

7.1
(see Lemma
7.2
).

The uniqueness of a basis of a non-reduced
(R,G)
,
after preparing certain terminologies in the first half of Section
7
, will be treated in
Proposition
7.23
in
\lx@sectionsign

7.7
.

The relationship between a simple system of
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and
of
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
is described as follows.
Denote by
(\Pi_{G})^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
a simple system of
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
. By definition (cf.
[
Mac72
]
),
it is also a simple system of
R/G
.

Lemma 2.2
.

There exists a simple system
(\Pi_{G})^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
of
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
such that
(\Pi_{G})^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\setminus(\Pi_{G})^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}

contains only
1
root for
R/G
being of type
BCC_{l},C^{\vee}BC_{l}
and
BB_{l}^{\vee}
,
and
2
roots of type
C^{\vee}C_{l}
.

Proof.

Write
(\Pi_{G})^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\{\overline{\alpha_{i}}\}_{0\leq i\leq l}
and set

\overline{\beta_{i}}=\begin{cases}\overline{\alpha_{i}}\qquad&\text{if}\;2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\not\in R/G,\\
2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\qquad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

It can be shown that
\{\overline{\beta_{i}}\}_{0\leq i\leq l}
is a simple system of

(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
we are looking for. Indeed, by
[
Bou81
]
, there
exists an automorphism
\varphi
of
R/G
such that
\varphi((\Pi_{G})^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})
is the one
in §
B.2
. By case by case checking, we see that

\varphi((\Pi_{G})^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
satisfies this property.
∎

Here and after, we fix
a\in G
in such a way that

(2)

G\cap Q(R)=\mathbb{Z}a,

and
take
b\in\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
so that
\operatorname{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})=\mathbb{Z}b\oplus\mathbb{Z}a
.

Remark 2.3
.

For a mERS
(R,G)
, an element
a\in\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
is uniquely determined by
(
2
) up to sign. On the other hand, there are
many choices for
b\in\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
. More precisely,
only the class
b\,\mathrm{mod}\,(Q(R)\cap G)\in\operatorname{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})/(Q(R)\cap G)
is uniquely determined up to sign.

2.2.
Tier numbers

Let
R
be a root system belonging to
(F,I)
. It is shown in
[
Sai85
]

(cf. Lemma 1.9) that
there exists a non-zero scalar
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that the set

\{cI(\alpha,\alpha)\,|\,\alpha\in R\}
is a finite subset of
\mathbb{Z}
. Set

t(R)=\max\left\{\left.\frac{I(\alpha,\alpha)}{I(\beta,\beta)}\right|\,\alpha,\beta\in R\right\},

and call it the
total tier number
of
R
.
With such a constant
c
, we also
define

(3)

\begin{split}&I_{R}=\frac{2c}{\mathrm{gcd}\{cI(\alpha,\alpha)\,|\,\alpha\in R\}}I,\\
&I_{R^{\vee}}=\frac{\mathrm{lcm}\{cI(\alpha,\alpha)\,|\,\alpha\in R\}}{2c}I.\end{split}

Here is the list of total tier numbers:

Type of
R/\operatorname{rad}(I)

A_{l}

B_{l}

C_{l}

BC_{l}

D_{l}

E_{l}

F_{4}

G_{2}

t(R)

1

2

2

4

1

1

2

3

Let
(R,G)
be a marked elliptic root system and
(R^{\vee},G)
its dual. Let

a^{\vee}\in\mathbb{R}a
and
b^{\vee}\in\operatorname{rad}(I)
such that
Q(R^{\vee})\cap G=\mathbb{Z}a^{\vee}

and
Q(R^{\vee})\cap\operatorname{rad}(I)=\mathbb{Z}a^{\vee}\oplus\mathbb{Z}b^{\vee}
.
The
first and the second tier numbers

for
(R,G)
and

(R^{\vee},G)
are
defined by

\displaystyle t_{1}(R,G)=

\displaystyle|(b^{\vee}\;\mathrm{mod}\;a^{\vee}):(b\;\mathrm{mod}\;a)|\times(I_{R^{\vee}}:I),

\displaystyle t_{1}(R^{\vee},G)=

\displaystyle|(b\;\mathrm{mod}\;a):(b^{\vee}\;\mathrm{mod}\;a^{\vee})|\times(I_{R}:I),

\displaystyle t_{2}(R,G)=

\displaystyle|(a^{\vee}:a)|\times(I_{R^{\vee}}:I),

\displaystyle t_{2}(R^{\vee},G)=

\displaystyle|(a:a^{\vee})|\times(I_{R}:I),

where
A:B
signifies the constant
c
such that
A=cB
.
It is known that

t(R)=t_{1}(R,G)t_{1}(R^{\vee},G)=t_{2}(R,G)t_{2}(R^{\vee},G).

2.3.
Counting set

The classification of the mERSs
(R,G)
with reduced affine quotient
R/G
has been carried out
by K. Saito in
[
Sai85
]
. In that work, even if the result itself is clearly stated, the
details of its proof is omitted.
Here we briefly present a proof to show how the notion like counting works.

In the first half of this subsection, we return to the general setting.
Let
R
be an elliptic root system belonging to
(F,I)
and
G
be a
subspace of
\operatorname{rad}(I)
defined over
\mathbb{Z}
(marking). That is,
G
is
not

necessarily one dimensional, and
R/G
is
not
assumed to be reduced.

For each
\alpha\in R
, let us define the subset
K_{G}(\alpha)
of the lattice

Q(R)\cap G
by

(4)

K_{G}(\alpha)=\{x\in G\,|\,\alpha+x\in R\,\},

and call it the
counting set
of
\alpha\in R
.
It has the next nice properties
(cf. p. 97 in
[
Sai85
]
):

Lemma 2.4
.

(1)

0\in K_{G}(\alpha)
and
K_{G}(\alpha)=-K_{G}(\alpha)
for
\alpha\in R
.

(2)

K_{G}(\varphi(\alpha))=K_{G}(\alpha)
for an automorphism
\varphi
of
R
and

\alpha\in R
.

(3)

K_{G}(\alpha)
is closed under the reflection centered at each point of

K_{G}(\alpha)
,

i.e.
2x-y\in K_{G}(\alpha)
for any
x,y\in K_{G}(\alpha)
.

(4)

K_{G}(\alpha)
is closed under the translation by

I(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})K_{G}(\beta)
for
\alpha,\beta\in R
,

i.e.,
K_{G}(\alpha)\supset K_{G}(\alpha)+I(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})K_{G}(\beta)
.

(5)

\dfrac{2}{I(\alpha,\alpha)}K_{G}(\alpha)=K_{G}^{\vee}(\alpha^{\vee})
, where

K_{G}^{\vee}
is the counting set of
(R^{\vee},G)
.

In particular, the first statement implies that if
x\in K_{G}(\alpha)
, then
\mathbb{Z}x\subset K_{G}(\alpha)
. Indeed, as
\alpha,\alpha+x\in R
,
-x\in K_{G}(\alpha+x)
which implies

x\in K_{G}(\alpha+x)
, so
\alpha+2x\in R
. In the same way, one can show that

\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}x\subset K_{G}(\alpha)
, hence
\mathbb{Z}x\subset K_{G}(\alpha)
again by (1).
The statements (2) and (4) imply that if
\beta\in W(R).\alpha
and
I(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})\in\{\pm 1\}
, then
K_{G}(\alpha)=K_{G}(\beta)
is additively closed.

Lemma 2.5
.

Suppose that there exists a subspace
L\subset F
satisfying

(5)

\displaystyle F=L\oplus G,

(6)

\displaystyle\pi_{G}|_{R\cap L}:R\cap L\rightarrow R/G\quad\text{is surjective}.

Then

(1)

K_{G}(\alpha)
contains a full lattice of
G
for
\alpha\in R
.

(2)

R=\amalg_{\alpha\in R\cap L}\left(\alpha+K_{G}(\alpha)\right)
.

(3)

\bigcup_{\alpha\in R\cap L}K_{G}(\alpha)
generates the lattice
Q(R)\cap G
.

Remark 2.6
.

As we already remarked above, the subspace
G\subset\operatorname{rad}(I)
is not
necessarily one dimensional. That is, we may assume
G=\operatorname{rad}(I)
. In this setting,
there exists a mERS
(R,G)
such that the surjective map
(
6
) is not injective
(
see also below
)
.

Here and after, we assume that
G=\mathbb{R}a
. Recall that we already
fixed a generator
a
of the lattice
Q(R)\cap G
of rank 1:

Q(R)\cap G=\mathbb{Z}a
(see (
2
)), and define the

counting number
for

\alpha\in R
by

(7)

k(\alpha)=\mathrm{min}\,\{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\,|\,\alpha+ka\in R\}.

For
\alpha\in R
, set

(8)

\alpha^{*}=\alpha+k(\alpha)a.

Fix a simple system
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq l}
of the mERS
(R,G)
in the sense of
Definition
2.1
, and define an
(l+1)
-dimensional subspace
F_{a}

of
F
by

(9)

F_{a}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{R}\alpha_{i}.

The following lemma is a generalization of the result of K. Saito
(
[
Sai85
]
, (6.1), Assertion).

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\alpha\in R
.

(1)

One has
K_{G}(\alpha)=\mathbb{Z}k(\alpha)a
. That is,
the counting number
k(\alpha)
satisfies

(10)

\alpha+\mathbb{Z}k(\alpha)a=R\cap(\alpha+\mathbb{Z}a).

(2)

For any two roots
\alpha,\beta\in R
,

(11)

k(\beta)|I(\beta,\alpha^{\vee})k(\alpha).

In particular, if
I(\beta,\alpha^{\vee})\in\{\pm 1\}
, then

1|k(\alpha)k(\beta)^{-1}|I(\alpha,\beta^{\vee}).

(3)

For every automorphism
\varphi
of
R
, one has
k(\varphi(\alpha))=k(\alpha)
.

For the following two statements, we assume
R/G
is reduced.

(4)

For the
(l+1)
-dimensional subspace
F_{a}
of
F
introduced in
(
9
), one has

(12)

R=\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in R\cap F_{a}}(\alpha+\mathbb{Z}k(\alpha)a).

(5)

g.c.d.\{k(\alpha_{i})\,|\,0\leq i\leq l\}=1
.

Remark 2.8
.

(1)

If
R/G
is reduced,
it is known that the canonical projection
\pi_{G}:F\to F/G
induces a bijection

(13)

\pi_{G}|_{R\cap F_{a}}:R\cap F_{a}\xrightarrow{\sim}R/G

(
see
[
Sai85
]
,
(3.3), Note 2)
. Therefore, the subspace
F_{a}
satisfies the
conditions (
5
) and
(
6
), and the results in Lemma

2.5
hold for
F_{a}
. Indeed, in
[
Sai85
]
,
K. Saito proved the statements
((6.1), Assertion)
under the assumption that

R/G
is reduced, and used Lemma
2.5
.

(2)

Otherwise, the induced map

\pi_{G}|_{R\cap F_{a}}:R\cap F_{a}\longrightarrow R/G
is not surjective in general.
As a consequence, Lemma
2.5
does not
hold in general. Therefore, we have to prove the statements
(1), (2), (3)

in the lemma without using Lemma
2.5
.

Proof.

Let us prove statement (1). Note that the following equality holds:

(14)

k(\alpha)=k(\alpha^{\ast}).

Indeed, since
\alpha,\alpha^{\ast}=\alpha+k(\alpha)a\in R
, we have

-k(\alpha)a\in K_{G}(\alpha^{\ast})
. By Lemma
2.4
(1),
it follows that

k(\alpha)a\in K_{G}(\alpha^{\ast})
. This shows that
k(\alpha^{\ast})\leq k(\alpha)
.
By Lemma
2.4
(1),

-k(\alpha^{\ast})a\in K_{G}(\alpha^{\ast})
. Hence,

\alpha^{*}-k(\alpha^{\ast})a=\alpha+(k(\alpha)-k(\alpha^{\ast}))a\in R,

from which the minimality of
k(\alpha)
implies
k(\alpha^{\ast})\geq k(\alpha)
,
thus we have (
14
). By using (
14
) repeatedly, we obtain

k(\alpha)=k(\alpha+rk(\alpha)a)\quad\text{for every $r\in\mathbb{Z}$}.

Thus, statement (1) follows.

Statement (2) is an immediate consequence of statement (1) and Lemma

2.4
(4).
Statement (3) follows from Lemma
2.4
(2) and
statement (1).
∎

2.4.
Elliptic diagrams I: reduced affine quotient

Let
(R,G)
be a mERS belonging to
(F,I_{F})
whose quotient system
R/G
is

reduced
, and fix a simple system
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq l}
of
(R,G)
.
Recall that the canonical projection
\pi_{G}:F\to F/G
induces a bijection
(
13
).
Via this bijection, the set
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq l}
can be
regarded as the set of nodes of the Dynkin diagram of the affine root system
R/G
.
Let
\Gamma_{a}
be the Dynkin diagram of the affine root system

R/G
. Denote
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=\pi_{G}(\alpha_{i})
for
0\leq i\leq l
.
Let
n_{i}
’s be the positive coprime integers such that

\overline{b}:=\sum_{i=0}^{l}n_{i}\overline{\alpha}_{i}
generates the lattice

Q(R/G)\cap\operatorname{rad}(I_{F/G})
of rank
1
. Define

(15)

b=\sum_{i=0}^{l}n_{i}\alpha_{i}.

Then
(b,a)
is an integral basis of
\operatorname{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})
,
i.e.
,

\operatorname{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})=\mathbb{Z}b\oplus\mathbb{Z}a
.
Let
k(\alpha_{i})
be the counting of the root
\alpha_{i}
.
The
exponent

m_{\alpha_{i}}
is defined by

m_{\alpha_{i}}=\frac{I_{R}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})}{2k(\alpha_{i})}n_{i}.

We denote the set of
nodes by
|\Gamma_{a}|
.

Set
m_{\max}=\max\{m_{\alpha_{0}},\cdots,m_{\alpha_{l}}\}
. Let
\Gamma_{m}
be
the subdiagram of
\Gamma_{a}
consisting of nodes

|\Gamma_{m}|=\{\alpha_{\alpha_{i}}\in|\Gamma_{a}|\,|m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{\max}\}.

Define

\displaystyle|\Gamma_{m}^{\ast}|=\{\alpha^{\ast}\,|\,\alpha\in|\Gamma_{m}|\,\}.

Definition 2.2
.

The
elliptic diagram

\Gamma(R,G)

for a marked elliptic root
system
(R,G)

is the graph whose set of nodes is

|\Gamma(R,G)|:=|\Gamma_{a}|\cup|\Gamma_{m}^{\ast}|,

and any two nodes
\alpha,\beta\in|\Gamma(R,G)|
are connected by the rules

(
Edge0
)
-
(
Edge3
)
with the additional case as follows:

(Edge4)

\alpha
\beta

if
I_{F}(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})=I_{F}(\beta,\alpha^{\vee})=2
.

2.5.
Classification of mERSs with reduced quotient

In this subsection, we provide the classification theorem of the mERSs
(R,G)
with reduced affine quotient
R/G
. Assume that the root system
R
belongs to a real vector space
F
equipped with a symmetric bilinear form
I_{F}
whose signature is
(l,2,0)
.
Fix a basis
(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\cdots,\varepsilon_{l},b,a)
of
F
satisfying
G=\mathbb{R}a
and

(16)

I_{F}(\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j})=\delta_{i,j},\;I_{F}(F,b)=I_{F}(F,a)=0.

Here, we use freely the notations on affine root systems as is given in Appendix
B
. Fix an isometric section
F/G\hookrightarrow F
so that its image is spanned by
\{\varepsilon_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq l}\cup\{b\}
and that it induces
R/G\hookrightarrow R
. Via such a section, we identify
F/G
with its image and regard
R/G
as a root subsystem of
R
.

Depending on the type of the full quotient
R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
, we introduce some mERSs
(R,G)
as follows (classical type):

(1)

R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
: of type
A_{l}\;(l\geq 1)
,
D_{l}\;(l\geq 4)
,
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
or
E_{8}
.  Let
R(X_{l})
be one of such finite root systems. The mERS of type
X_{l}^{(1,1)}
is defined by

R(X_{l}^{(1,1)})=R(X_{l}^{(1)})+\mathbb{Z}a=R(X_{l})+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b.

(2)

R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
: of type
B_{l}\;(l\geq 3)
,
C_{l}\;(l\geq 2)
,
F_{4}
or
G_{2}
.  Let
R(X_{l})
be one of such finite root systems and let
t_{1},t_{2}\in\{1,2\}
for
X_{l}
of type
B_{l},C_{l}
or
F_{4}
and
t_{1},t_{2}\in\{1,3\}
for
X_{l}
of type
G_{2}
. The mERS of type
X_{l}^{(t_{1},t_{2})}
is defined by

\displaystyle R(X_{l}^{(t_{1},t_{2})})=

\displaystyle(R(X_{l}^{(t_{1})})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a)\cup(R(X_{l}^{(t_{1})})_{l}+t_{2}\mathbb{Z}a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(R(X_{l})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(X_{l})_{l}+t_{2}\mathbb{Z}a+t_{1}\mathbb{Z}b).

The defining range of the root system
R(X_{l}^{(t_{1},t_{2})})
with respect to
l
is the same as that of the affine root system
R(X_{l}^{(t_{1})})
.

(3)

R/\operatorname{rad}{I_{F}}
: of type
BC_{l}\;(l\geq 1)
.
By the assumption that
R/G
is reduced, it should be of type
BC_{l}^{(2)}
. That
is, the first tier number
t_{1}
is equal to
2
in this case. Recall that

R(BC_{l}^{(2)})=(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{m}+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{l}+(1+2\mathbb{Z})b).

Let
t_{2}\in\{1,2,4\}
be a positive integer.

i)

For
t_{2}=1
, the root system of type

BC_{l}^{(2,1)}
(
l\geq 1
) is defined by

R(BC_{l}^{(2,1)})=R(BC_{l}^{(2)})+\mathbb{Z}a.

ii)

For
t_{2}=2
, let
i\in\{1,2\}
be a positive integer.
The root system of type
BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(i)

(
l\geq 2
for
i=1
and
l\geq 1
for
i=2
) is defined by

R(BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(i))=(R(BC_{l}^{(2)})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a)\cup(R(BC_{l}^{(2)})_{m}+i\mathbb{Z}a)\cup(R(BC_{l}^{(2)})_{l}+2\mathbb{Z}a).

iii)

For
t_{2}=4
, the root system of type
BC_{l}^{(2,4)}

(
l\geq 1
) is defined by

R(BC_{l}^{(2,4)})=(R(BC_{l}^{(2)})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a)\cup(R(BC_{l}^{(2)})_{m}+2\mathbb{Z}a)\cup(R(BC_{l}^{(2)})_{l}+4\mathbb{Z}a).

Surprisingly, there are another type of mERSs, called
\ast
-type:

(1)

The root system of type
A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}
:

R(A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast})=R(A_{1})+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,mn\equiv 0[2]\,\}.

(2)

The root system of type
B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}\;(l\geq 2)
:

R(B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast})=(R(B_{l})_{s}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,mn\equiv 0[2]\,\})\cup(R(B_{l})_{l}+2\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b).

(3)

The root system of type
C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}\;(l\geq 2)
:

R(C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast})=(R(C_{l})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(C_{l})_{l}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,mn\equiv 0[2]\,\}).

Remark that the superscripts are the tier numbers. See Appendix
C
for detail.

K. Saito
[
Sai85
]
obtained the following classification theorem:

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
(R,G)
be a marked elliptic root system belonging to a real vector space
F=\mathbb{R}^{l+2}
equipped with a symmetric bilinear form
I
whose signature is
(l,2,0)
for some positive integer
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Suppose that the affine quotient
R/G
is reduced. Then
(R,G)
is isomorphic to one of the following marked elliptic root systems:

\displaystyle A_{l}^{(1,1)}\,(l\geq 1),\;D_{l}^{(1,1)}\,(l\geq 4),\;E_{6}^{(1,1)},\;E_{7}^{(1,1)},\;E_{8}^{(1,1)},

\displaystyle B_{l}^{(1,t)}\,(l\geq 3),\;C_{l}^{(1,t)}\,(l\geq 2),\;F_{4}^{(1,t)}\quad t\in\{1,2\},\qquad G_{2}^{(1,t)}\quad t\in\{1,3\},

\displaystyle B_{l}^{(2,t)}\,(l\geq 2),\;C_{l}^{(2,t)}\,(l\geq 3),\;F_{4}^{(2,t)}\quad t\in\{1,2\},\qquad G_{2}^{(3,t)}\quad t\in\{1,3\},

\displaystyle BC_{l}^{(2,1)},\;BC_{l}^{(2,4)}\quad(l\geq 1),

\displaystyle BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(1)\,(l\geq 2),\;BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(2)\,(l\geq 1),

\displaystyle A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast},\;B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast},\;C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}\quad(l\geq 2).

A sketchy proof of this theorem is given by K. Saito in
[
Sai85
]
. Here, we provide a simple complete proof as an application of Lemma
2.4
.
It should be noticed that none of the two mERSs in the above theorem are isomorphic, since two tier numbers are different, except for types
BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(i)

(i=1,2)
where the number of Weyl group orbits on the root systems are different.
Therefore, in practice, the above theorem also gives the classification of the isomorphism classes of mERS with reduced affine quotient.

2.6.
Proof of Theorem
2.9

A key idea of the proof comes from Lemma
2.4
, as the quotient root system
R/G
is a reduced affine root system. In view of this lemma, it is convenient to regroup the reduced affine root systems, depending on the type of the orbit decomposition of
R/G
with respect to the affine Weyl group
W(R/G)
, as follows:

(1)

A_{l}^{(1)}\,(l\geq 2)
,
D_{l}^{(1)}\,(l\geq 4)
,
E_{6}^{(1)}
,
E_{7}^{(1)}
and

E_{8}^{(1)}
.

(2)

B_{l}^{(1)}\,(l\geq 3),\;F_{4}^{(1)},\;G_{2}^{(1)},\;C_{l}^{(2)}\,(l\geq 3),\;F_{4}^{(2)}

and
G_{2}^{(3)}
.

(3)

A_{1}^{(1)},\;C_{l}^{(1)}\,(l\geq 2)
and
B_{l}^{(2)}\,(l\geq 2)
.

(4)

BC_{l}^{(2)}\,(l\geq 1)
.

See Appendix
B
for details on the affine root systems.

Remark 2.10
.

Here we use the nomenclature for the reduced affine root systems due to
K. Saito
[
Sai85
]
, since they reflect the structure of root systems
themselves more than those of V. Kac
[
Kac90
]
. For the sake of reader’s
convenience, we have recalled them in Appendix
B
.
Here is the correspondence
:

\begin{matrix}\text{\small V. Kac }\\
\text{\small$($1969, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Kac1969}{}{}]}$)$ }\end{matrix}

X_{l}^{(1)}

\begin{matrix}{\tiny D_{l+1}^{(2)}}\\
{\tiny l\geq 2}\end{matrix}

\begin{matrix}{\tiny A_{2l-1}^{(2)}}\\
{\tiny l\geq 3}\end{matrix}

E_{6}^{(2)}

D_{4}^{(3)}

A_{2l}^{(2)}

\begin{matrix}\text{\small R. Moody}\\
\text{\small$($1969, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Moody1969}{}{}]}$)$}\end{matrix}

X_{l,1}

B_{l,2}

C_{l,2}

F_{4,2}

G_{2,3}

\begin{matrix}{\tiny A_{1,2}}&\text{\tiny$l=1$}\\
{\tiny BC_{l,2}}&\text{\tiny$l>1$}\end{matrix}

\begin{matrix}\text{\small I. Macdonald}\\
\text{\small$($1972, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Macdonald1972}{}{}]}$)$}\end{matrix}

\begin{matrix}{\tiny X_{l}=X_{l}^{\vee}}&\text{\tiny$X=ADE$}\\
{\tiny X_{l}}&\text{\tiny$X=BCFG$}\end{matrix}

C_{l}^{\vee}

B_{l}^{\vee}

F_{4}^{\vee}

G_{2}^{\vee}

BC_{l}=BC_{l}^{\vee}

\begin{matrix}\text{\small K. Saito }\\
\text{\small$($1985, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Saito1985}{}{}]}$)$}\end{matrix}

X_{l}^{(1)}

B_{l}^{(2)}

C_{l}^{(2)}

F_{4}^{(2)}

G_{2}^{(3)}

BC_{l}^{(2)}

\begin{matrix}\text{\small R. Carter}\\
\text{\small$($2005, \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Carter2005}{}{}]}$)$}\end{matrix}

\widetilde{X}_{l}

\widetilde{C}_{l}^{\mathrm{t}}

\widetilde{B}_{l}^{\mathrm{t}}

\widetilde{F}_{4}^{\mathrm{t}}

\widetilde{G}_{2}^{\mathrm{t}}

\widetilde{C}_{l}^{\prime}

From now on, we fix a basis of the vector space
F=\mathbb{R}^{l+2}
with the symmetric
bilinear form
I_{F}
with signature
(l,2,0)
. Let
(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\cdots,\varepsilon_{l},b,a)
be a basis of
F
satisfying (
16
).

2.6.1.
R/G
of type
A_{l}^{(1)}\,(l\geq 2),\;D_{l}^{(1)}\,(l\geq 4),\;E_{6}^{(1)},\,E_{7}^{(1)},\,E_{8}^{(1)}

Let
X_{l}^{(1)}
be one of such root systems.
In this case, the real root system
R/G
has a single Weyl group orbit. Hence, by
Lemma
2.7
(3),
k(\alpha)=1
for any
\alpha\in R
. Thus, the
root system
R
can be described as follows:

R=R(X_{l}^{(1)})+\mathbb{Z}a=R(X_{l})+\mathbb{Z}b+\mathbb{Z}a.

This is exactly the root system of type
X_{l}^{(1,1)}
.

2.6.2.
R/G
of type
B_{l}^{(1)}\,(l\geq 3),\;F_{4}^{(1)},\;G_{2}^{(1)},\;C_{l}^{(2)}\,(l\geq 3),\;F_{4}^{(2)},\;G_{2}^{(3)}

Let
X_{l}^{(r)}
be one of such root systems. In this case, the decomposition

R(X_{l}^{(r)})=R(X_{l}^{(r)})_{s}\amalg R(X_{l}^{(r)})_{l}
gives the decomposition into the Weyl
group orbits. Hence the counting number only depends on the length of the roots.
Moreover, for a long root
\alpha
and short root
\beta
, one has

I_{F}(\beta,\alpha^{\vee})\in\{\pm 1\}
if it is not
0
. Thus, by Lemma

2.7
(2), we have
k(\beta)=1
.
For each
s:=k(\alpha)\in\left\{1,\frac{I_{F}(\alpha,\alpha)}{I_{F}(\beta,\beta)}\right\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(R(X_{l}^{(r)})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a)\cup(R(X_{l}^{(r)})_{l}+s\mathbb{Z}a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(R(X_{l})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}b+\mathbb{Z}a)\cup(R(X_{l})_{l}+r\mathbb{Z}b+s\mathbb{Z}a),

which is of type
X_{l}^{(r,s)}
. This gives us the next list:

Type of
R/G

B_{l}^{(1)}\;(l\geq 3)

F_{4}^{(1)}

G_{2}^{(1)}

C_{l}^{(2)}\;(l\geq 3)

F_{4}^{(2)}

G_{2}^{(3)}

s=1

B_{l}^{(1,1)}

F_{4}^{(1,1)}

G_{2}^{(1,1)}

C_{l}^{(2,1)}

F_{4}^{(2,1)}

G_{2}^{(3,1)}

s=\dfrac{I_{F}(\alpha,\alpha)}{I_{F}(\beta,\beta)}

B_{l}^{(1,2)}

F_{4}^{(1,2)}

G_{2}^{(1,3)}

C_{l}^{(2,2)}

F_{4}^{(2,2)}

G_{2}^{(3,3)}

2.6.3.
R/G
of type
A_{1}^{(1)},\;C_{l}^{(1)}\,(l\geq 2),\;B_{l}^{(2)}\,(l\geq 2)

In this case,
R/G
admits a diagram automorphism
\overline{\varphi}
, which is defined
by
\overline{\varphi}(\beta_{i})=\beta_{l-i}
(
0\leq i\leq l
) where
\{\beta_{0},\cdots,\beta_{l}\}
is a simple system of
R/G
. If

\overline{\varphi}
lifts up to an automorphism
\varphi
of
(R,G)
, then the counting
number depends only on the length of the roots, in which case it follows that

(1)

k(\alpha)=1
for any
\alpha\in R
if
R/G
is of type
A_{1}^{(1)}
and

(2)

k(\alpha)=1
for any
\alpha\in R_{s}
and
k(\alpha)\in\{1,2\}
for any

\alpha\in R_{l}

if
R/G
is either of type
B_{l}^{(2)}
or
C_{l}^{(1)}
.

Hence, we consider the case when the automorphism
\overline{\varphi}
of
R/G

cannot be lifted up to an automorphism of
(R,G)
. In this case, one should have

\{k(\alpha_{0}),\,k(\alpha_{l})\}=\{1,2\}
.

The case
(k(\alpha_{0}),k(\alpha_{l}))=(1,2)

(1)

R/G
: of type
A_{1}^{(1)}

R=\{\alpha+nb+ma\,|\,\alpha\in R(A_{1}),\;m(n-1)\equiv 0\;[2]\,\}.

(2)

R/G
: of type
B_{l}^{(2)}

R_{s}=\{\alpha+nb+ma\,|\,\alpha\in R(B_{l})_{s},\;m(n-1)\equiv 0\;[2]\,\}.

By Lemma
2.7
(2), one has
k(\alpha)=2
for any

\alpha\in R_{l}
.

(3)

R/G
: of type
C_{l}^{(1)}

R_{l}=\{\alpha+nb+ma\,|\,\alpha\in R(C_{l})_{l},\;m(n-1)\equiv 0\;[2]\,\}.

By Lemma
2.7
(2), we have
k(\alpha)=1
for any

\alpha\in R_{s}
.

The case
(k(\alpha_{0}),k(\alpha_{l}))=(2,1)

(1)

R/G
of type
A_{1}^{(1)}

R=\{\alpha+nb+ma\,|\,\alpha\in R(A_{1}),\;mn\equiv 0\;[2]\,\}.

(2)

R/G
of type
B_{l}^{(2)}

R_{s}=\{\alpha+nb+ma\,|\,\alpha\in R(B_{l})_{s},\;mn\equiv 0\;[2]\,\}.

By Lemma
2.7
(2), one has
k(\alpha)=2
for any

\alpha\in R_{l}
.

(3)

R/G
: of type
C_{l}^{(1)}
.

R_{l}=\{\alpha+nb+ma\,|\,\alpha\in R(C_{l})_{l},\;mn\equiv 0\;[2]\,\}.

By Lemma
2.7
(2), one has
k(\alpha)=1
for any

\alpha\in R_{s}
.

Let us examine whether the cases
(k(\alpha_{0}),k(\alpha_{l}))=(1,2)
and

(k(\alpha_{0}),k(\alpha_{l}))\\
=(2,1)
provide isomorphic marked root systems or not.

In the case when
R/G
is of type
A_{1}^{(1)}
, it can be checked that the linear map of

F
defined by

\alpha\,\mapsto\,\alpha+b,\qquad b\,\mapsto\,b,\qquad a\,\mapsto\,a,

with a fixed positive root
\alpha
of
R(A_{1})
,
induces an isomorphism of the two marked root systems which is of type
A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}
.

In the case when
R/G
is of type
B_{l}^{(2)}
, the linear map of
F
defined by

\varepsilon_{i}\,\mapsto\,\varepsilon_{i}+b,\qquad b\,\mapsto\,b,\qquad a\,\mapsto\,a

for
1\leq i\leq l
,
induces an isomorphism of the two marked root systems which is of type
B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}
.

In the case when
R/G
is of type
C_{l}^{(1)}
, the linear map of
F
defined by

\varepsilon_{i}\,\mapsto\,\varepsilon_{i}+\frac{1}{2}b,\qquad b\,\mapsto\,b,\qquad a\,\mapsto\,a

for
1\leq i\leq l
,
induces an isomorphism of the two marked root systems which is of type
C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}
.

Summarizing, we obtain the next table:

Type of
R/G

A_{1}^{(1)}

B_{l}^{(2)}\;(l\geq 2)

C_{l}^{(1)}\;(l\geq 2)

k(\alpha_{0})k(\alpha_{l})=1

A_{1}^{(1,1)}

B_{l}^{(2,1)}

C_{l}^{(1,1)}

k(\alpha_{0})k(\alpha_{l})=2

A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}

B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}

C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}

k(\alpha_{0})k(\alpha_{l})=4

B_{l}^{(2,2)}

C_{l}^{(1,2)}

2.6.4.
R/G
of type
BC_{l}^{(2)}

Denote the finite root system of type
BC_{l}
by
R_{f}
.
In this case, there can be
3
different values, say

k_{s}:=k(\varepsilon_{i}),\;k_{m}:=k(\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}),\;k_{l}:=k(2\varepsilon_{i})
with any
1\leq i\neq j\leq l
.
By Lemma
2.7
(2), we have

(17)

k_{s}|k_{l}|4k_{s},\qquad k_{s}|k_{m}|2k_{s},\qquad k_{m}|k_{l}|2k_{m}.

From Lemma
2.7
(5), it follows that
k_{s}=1
. Hence the
above equality implies
k_{m}\in\{1,2\}
.

(1)

If
k_{m}=1
, then
k_{l}\in\{1,2\}
by (
17
).

i)

If
k_{l}=1
, then

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle\{\alpha+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b\,|\,\alpha\in(R_{f})_{s}\cup(R_{f})_{m}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{\alpha+\mathbb{Z}a+(2\mathbb{Z}-1)b\,|\,\alpha\in(R_{f})_{l}\}

is of type
BC_{l}^{(2,1)}
, and

ii)

if
k_{l}=2
, then

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle\{\alpha+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b\,|\,\alpha\in(R_{f})_{s}\cup(R_{f})_{m}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{\alpha+2\mathbb{Z}a+(2\mathbb{Z}-1)b\,|\,\alpha\in(R_{f})_{l}\}

is of type
BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(1)
.

(2)

If
k_{m}=2
, then
k_{l}\in\{2,4\}
by (
17
).

i)

If
k_{l}=2
, then

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle\{\alpha+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b\,|\,\alpha\in(R_{f})_{s}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{\alpha+2\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b\,|\,\alpha\in(R_{f})_{m}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{\alpha+2\mathbb{Z}a+(2\mathbb{Z}-1)b\,|\,\alpha\in(R_{f})_{l}\}

is of type
BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(2)
, and

ii)

if
k_{l}=4
, then

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle\{\alpha+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b\,|\,\alpha\in(R_{f})_{s}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{\alpha+2\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b\,|\,\alpha\in(R_{f})_{m}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{\alpha+4\mathbb{Z}a+(2\mathbb{Z}-1)b\,|\,\alpha\in(R_{f})_{l}\}

is of type
BC_{l}^{(2,4)}
.

3.
Automorphisms of a mERS with reduced quotient

3.1.
Preliminaries

For the description of Weyl groups, we recall a notion of
the Eichler-Siegel map, following
[
Sai85
]
.
Let
V
be a finite-dimensional real vector space with a symmetric
bilinear form
J:V\times V\to\mathbb{R}
on
V
. Define a semigroup structure

\circ
on
V\otimes V/\mbox{rad}J
by

\Bigl(\sum_{i}u_{i}\otimes v_{i}\Bigr)\circ\Bigl(\sum_{j}w_{j}\otimes x_{j}\Bigr):=\sum_{i}u_{i}\otimes v_{i}+\sum_{j}w_{j}\otimes x_{j}-\sum_{i,j}J(v_{i},w_{j})u_{i}\otimes x_{j}.

Definition 3.1
.

The Eichler-Siegel map
E_{V}
for
(V,J)
is the map from

V\otimes V/\mathrm{rad}(J)
to
\mathrm{End}(V)
defined by

E_{V}(\sum_{i}u_{i}\otimes v_{i})(u)=u-\sum_{i}I(v_{i},u)u_{i}.

From the definition, it follows that

i)

the map
E_{V}:V\otimes V/\mathrm{rad}(J)\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(V)
is
a homomorphism of semi-groups;

ii)

it is injective, and is bijective if and only if
\mathrm{rad}{J}=0
;

iii)

for a non-isotropic
\alpha\in V
, one has
r_{\alpha}=E_{V}(\alpha\otimes\alpha^{\vee})
;

iv)

the inverse of Eichler-Siegel map on the Weyl group
W(R)
is well-defined;

v)

for
\xi,\eta\in\mathrm{rad}(J)\otimes V/\mathrm{rad}(J)
, one has

E_{V}(\xi+\eta)=E_{V}(\xi)E_{V}(\eta).

(See
[
Sai85
]
, for details.)

We return to the setting in §
1.1
. Let
(F,I_{F})
be a pair of
a finite-dimensional real vector space
F
and a symmetric bilinear form

I_{F}:F\times F\to\mathbb{R}
on
F
with signature
(\mu_{+},\mu_{0},\mu_{-})
and

R
a root system belonging to
(F,I_{F})
. Furthermore, let
G
be a subspace of

\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
defined over
\mathbb{Z}
(marking).

Since every element
w\in W(R)
preserves the subspace
G
of

\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})
, the
canonical projection
\pi_{G}:F\to F/G
induces the surjective group homomorphism

(\pi_{G})_{*}:W(R)\to W(R/G)
. Define a subgroup
T_{G}(R)
of
W(R)
by

(18)

T_{G}(R)=W(R)\cap E_{F}\big(G\otimes(F/\mathrm{rad}\,I_{F})\big),

where
E_{F}:F\otimes F/\mathrm{rad}\,I_{F}\to\mathrm{End}(F)
is the
Eichler-Siegel map. The following lemma is useful for describing the structure
of the Weyl group.

Lemma 3.1

(
[
Sai85
]
, (1.15), Assertion and Lemma)
.

(1)

There is an exact sequence

(19)

1\longrightarrow T_{G}(R)\longrightarrow W(R)\xrightarrow{(\pi_{G})_{*}}W(R/G)\longrightarrow 1.

(2)

Assume there exists a vector subspace
L
of
F
which has
the following properties
:

(i)

F=L\oplus G
;

(ii)

The restriction
(\pi_{G})_{*}\big|_{W(R\cap L)}:W(R\cap L)\to W(R/G)
is
surjective. Here,
W(R\cap L)
is the subgroup of
W(R)

generated by the reflections
r_{\alpha}\ (\alpha\in R\cap L)
.

Then, the map
(\pi_{G})_{*}\big|_{W(R\cap L)}
is an isomorphism.

(3)

Under the assumption that such an
L
exists,
the map
\big((\pi_{G})_{*}\big|_{W(R\cap L)}\big)^{-1}:W(R/G)\to W(R\cap L)

gives a section of (
19
). That is, this exact sequence splits, and

W(R)
can be described as a semidirect product
:

(20)

W(R)\cong T_{G}(R)\rtimes W(R\cap L).

(4)

Under the same assumption,
E_{F}^{-1}\big(T_{G}(R)\big)
is a lattice of

G\otimes F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})
, which is generated by

(21)

\alpha_{G}\otimes\big(\alpha_{L}^{\vee}\,\mathrm{mod}\,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})\big)\quad\text{for}\quad\alpha\in R.

Here,
\alpha=\alpha_{L}+\alpha_{G}
is the decomposition of
\alpha\in R
with
respect to the direct sum decomposition
F=L\oplus G
.

Remark 3.2
.

In the proof of the above lemma (see
[
Sai85
]
, (1.15),
Assertion and Lemma), both of the root systems
R
and
R/G

are not assumed to be reduced.

3.2.
Elliptic Weyl groups

In the following two subsections, for a mERS
(R,G)
belonging to
(F,I_{F})
,
the quotient affine root system
R/G
is assumed to be reduced.
In this subsection, under this assumption, we study
the explicit structure of the elliptic Weyl group
W(R)
. These results will be used in
Part 2 for studying the case that
R/G
is non-reduced.

Step 1
. First, we fix an integral basis
(b,a)
of
\operatorname{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})
. Indeed, take a basis
(\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{l})
of

(R,G)
as in Appendix
C
. Note that the element

\alpha_{0}
is chosen as the condition

n_{0}=1

is satisfied. The elements
a
and
b
are introduced in (
2
) and
(
15
), respectively.

Step 2
.
Second, recall the
(l+1)
-dimensional subspace
F_{a}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{R}\alpha_{i}

of
F
, introduced in (
9
). By (
13
), we see
that
L=F_{a}
satisfies the properties (i) and (ii)
of Lemma
3.1
(2).
Thus, the results of the lemma can be applied for such an
(R,G)
. Recall
the split exact sequence (
19
):

1\longrightarrow T_{G}(R)\longrightarrow W(R)\xrightarrow{(\pi_{G})_{*}}W(R/G)\longrightarrow 1.

By Lemma
2.7
(4) and Lemma

3.1
(4), the lattice
E_{F}^{-1}\big(T_{G}(R)\big)
is generated by

k(\alpha)a\otimes\big(\alpha^{\vee}\,\mathrm{mod}\,\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)=a\otimes\big(\alpha^{\dagger}\,\mathrm{mod}\,\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)\quad\text{for}\quad\alpha\in R\cap L,

where

(22)

\alpha^{\dagger}:=k(\alpha)\alpha^{\vee}.

The following is known:

(i)

The set
\{\alpha^{\dagger}\,|\,\alpha\in R\cap F_{a}\}

is an affine root system which is denoted by

(R,G)_{a}
,
and its ordered subset

\{\alpha_{i}^{\dagger}\}_{0\leq i\leq l}
is a simple system of
(R,G)_{a}

(see
[
ST97
]
),

and the next formula follows easily by the definition:

(ii)

Q((R,G)_{a})\equiv\bigoplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}^{\dagger}\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))
.

Define a lattice
Q_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),a}\subset F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
of rank
l
by

(23)

Q_{{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),a}}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}^{\dagger}\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})).

Combining the above results, we see that

(24)

{T_{G}(R)}=E_{F}(a\otimes Q_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),a}),

and there exists a semidirect product expression

(25)

W(R)\cong E_{F}(a\otimes Q_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),a})\rtimes W(R\cap F_{a}).

The explicit form of
Q_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),a}
is given in Table 1 below.

Step 3
. Third, study the group
W(R/G)\cong W(R\cap F_{a})
.
Since
R/G
is a reduced affine root system
belonging to
(F/G,I_{F/G})
, the structure of
W(R/G)
is well-known. There
exists a lattice
Q_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),b}\subset(F/G)/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F/G})

of rank
l
so that

(26)

W(R/G)\cong E_{F/G}\big(\overline{b}\otimes Q_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),b}\big)\rtimes W\big((R/G)/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F/G})\big),

where
E_{F/G}:(F/G)\otimes(F/G)/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F/G})\longrightarrow\mathrm{End}(F/G)
is the Eichler-Siegel map for
(F/G,I_{F/G})
.

By the canonical isomorphism
(F/G)/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F/G})\cong F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
, the lattice

Q_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),b}
is regarded as a sublattice in

F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
, and the root system

(R/G)/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F/G})
is identified with
R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
.
Note that
R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
is a finite root system belonging to

(F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),I_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})
,
which is not
necessarily reduced.

Hence, we have

(27)

W(R/G)\cong E_{F/G}\big(\overline{b}\otimes Q_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),b}\big)\rtimes W(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})).

The explicit form of
Q_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),b}
is given in Table 1 below.

Step 4
.
Finally, applying Lemma
3.1
for
the pair
(R,\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))
, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 3.3
.

(1)

Define a subspace
F_{f}
of
F
by

(28)

F_{f}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{R}\alpha_{i}.

Then, it satisfies properties
(i)
and
(ii)
of Lemma

3.1

(2)
for the pair
(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))
.
That is, the results of the lemma can be applied for such an
(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))
.
Especially,
there exists a split exact sequence

(29)

1\longrightarrow T_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}(R)\longrightarrow W(R)\xrightarrow{(\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}}W(R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))\longrightarrow 1.

(2)

Recall the lattices
Q_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),a},Q_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),b}\subset F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})
of
rank
l
introduced above. We have

(30)

T_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}(R)=E_{F}\big(a\otimes Q_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),a}+b\otimes Q_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),b}\big).

Therefore, we get a semidirect product expression

W(R)\cong E_{F}\big(a\otimes Q_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),a}+b\otimes Q_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),b}\big)\rtimes W(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})).

Table 1.

Here
X=A,B,C,D,E,F
or
G
, and
t=2,3
. In addition,

\widetilde{Q}\big((R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)
is the sub-lattice of
Q\big((R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)

defined by

\widetilde{Q}\big((R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)=\sum_{\gamma\in R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}\dfrac{I_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(\gamma,\gamma)}{I_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(\gamma_{s},\gamma_{s})}\mathbb{Z}\gamma^{\vee}

where
\gamma_{s}\in R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
is a short root.

3.3.
Modular part

In the following three subsections, we study the structure of
\mathrm{Aut}(R)
.
We use these results in the weak classification of mERSs with non-reduced
affine quotients (see
\lx@sectionsign

4.3
and Section
5

below).

Remark 3.4
.

In
[
Sat95
]
, Satake also gave a description of the group
\mathrm{Aut}(R)
.
However, he excluded the
mERSs
(R,G)
of types
BC_{l}^{(2,1)}
,

BC_{l}^{(2,4)}
,
BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(1)
,
BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(2)
,
A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}
,

B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}
and
C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}
as “exceptions”.
Some of these exceptions are necessary for the weak
classification of mERSs with non-reduced affine quotients in

\lx@sectionsign

4.3
and Section
5
.

By Lemma
1.1
,
an element
\psi\in\mathrm{Aut}(R)
preserves

\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})=\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\cap Q(R)
. In other words, the restriction

\psi|_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}
belongs to
GL(\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F}))
,
where

GL(\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})):=\big\{g\in GL(\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))\,\big|\,g(\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F}))=\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})\big\}.

The map defined by
\psi\mapsto\psi|_{\mbox{\scriptsize rad}(I_{F})}
is
denoted by
\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}:\mathrm{Aut}(R)\to GL(\mbox{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F}))
.
Its image

\Gamma(R):=\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}(\mathrm{Aut}(R))

is called the
modular part
of the automorphism group. Define the
elliptic modular group

\Gamma^{+}(R)
attached to
R
by

\Gamma^{+}(R)=\Gamma(R)\cap SL(\mbox{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})),

where

SL(\mbox{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})):=\big\{g\in GL(\mbox{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F}))\,\big|\,\det g=1\big\}.

Consider the integral basis
(b,a)
of
\operatorname{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})
. There is
an isomorphism

SL(\mbox{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F}))\xrightarrow{\sim}SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})

defined by

(31)

g\longmapsto\begin{pmatrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{where}\quad(g(b),g(a))=(b,a)\begin{pmatrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{pmatrix}.

Define the subgroup
\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)

of orientation preserving automorphisms by

\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)=\bigl\{\psi\in\mathrm{Aut}(R)\,\bigl|\,\psi|_{\mbox{\scriptsize rad}(I_{F})}\in SL(\mbox{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F}))\bigr\}.

Hence, we have the following commutative diagram

(32)

\begin{array}[]{l}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 290.28673pt\raise-31.51164pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.31248pt\raise-63.49829pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
