148.73375pt\raise-63.49829pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 148.73375pt\raise-63.49829pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{Aut}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 189.15237pt\raise-57.77705pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.00874pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 226.05394pt\raise-63.49829pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 166.69804pt\raise-87.49828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 226.05394pt\raise-63.49829pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Gamma(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 290.28673pt\raise-63.49829pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 239.07243pt\raise-87.49828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 290.28673pt\raise-63.49829pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-94.93579pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 72.42522pt\raise-94.93579pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 152.39256pt\raise-94.93579pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 196.85193pt\raise-90.7075pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.22829pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 224.76695pt\raise-94.93579pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 166.69804pt\raise-119.41078pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 224.76695pt\raise-94.93579pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 239.07243pt\raise-119.41078pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 292.59921pt\raise-94.93579pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-125.89828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 72.42522pt\raise-125.89828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 161.38556pt\raise-125.89828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 233.75995pt\raise-125.89828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 292.59921pt\raise-125.89828pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array},

where

\mathrm{Aut}(R,\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})):=\big\{\psi\in\mathrm{Aut}(R)\,\big|\,\psi|_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}=\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}\big\}.

Both the left and right vertical exact sequences in
(
32
)
split. Indeed,
recalling the subspace
F_{f}
of
F
introduced in (
28
),
we have a decomposition of
F
:

(33)

F=F_{f}\oplus\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})=F_{f}\oplus(\mathbb{R}b\oplus\mathbb{R}a).

Define an involutive automorphism
\iota_{F_{f}}\in\mathrm{Aut}(R)
by

(34)

\iota_{F_{f}}|_{F_{f}}=\mathrm{id}_{F_{f}},\quad\iota_{F_{f}}(b)=b,\quad\iota_{F_{f}}(a)=-a.

By the construction, we immediately have the
semidirect product expression

\mathrm{Aut}(R)=\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)\rtimes\langle\iota_{F_{f}}\rangle,

where

\langle\iota_{F_{f}}\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
is the group generated by

\iota_{F_{f}}\in\mathrm{Aut(R)}
, and this induces a semidirect
expression

\Gamma(R)=\Gamma^{+}(R)\rtimes\iota_{F_{f}}|_{\operatorname{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F})}\cong\Gamma^{+}(R)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, the study of
\mathrm{Aut}(R)

is reduced to one of
\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)
.

Let us study the group
\Gamma^{+}(R)
.
Recall some notations from the theory of congruence subgroups
(see
[
Kob93
]
, for example). For
a positive integer
N
, set

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}(N)=

\displaystyle\left\{\left.\begin{pmatrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{pmatrix}\in SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\,\right|\,r\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,N)\right\},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{0}(N)=

\displaystyle\left\{\left.\begin{pmatrix}p&q\\
r&s\end{pmatrix}\in SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})\,\right|\,q\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,N)\right\}.

Define a subgroup
\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)
of
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
by

\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)=\begin{cases}SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})&\text{for}\ X_{l}^{(t,t)}\ \text{and}\ X_{l}^{(t,t)\ast}\,(t=1,2,3),\\
\Gamma_{0}(2)&\text{for}\ X_{l}^{(1,2)},\ BC_{l}^{(2,4)},\ BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(1)\ \text{and}\ BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(2),\\
\Gamma^{0}(2)&\text{for}\ X_{l}^{(2,1)}\ \text{and}\ BC_{l}^{(2,1)},\\
\Gamma_{0}(3)&\text{for}\ G_{2}^{(1,3)},\\
\Gamma^{0}(3)&\text{for}\ G_{2}^{(3,1)}.\end{cases}

The first step in studying the elliptic modular group
\Gamma^{+}(R)

is the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

Under the identification (
31
), we regard
\Gamma^{+}(R)
as
a subgroup of
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Then,
\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)
is a subgroup of

\Gamma^{+}(R)
.

Before proving the above lemma, we need certain preparations.

1
. First, let us introduce an element
\rho\in F_{f}
by

\rho=\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{l})&\mbox{for $BC_{l}^{(2,4)}$ and $C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}$},\\
\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon&\mbox{for $A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}$},\\
\varepsilon_{1}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{l}&\mbox{for $B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}$},\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Here,
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{l}
is an orthonormal basis of
F_{f}

(see (
16
)), and

\varepsilon
is an element of
F_{f}
such that
I_{F}(\varepsilon,\varepsilon)=2

(see Appendix
C
).

Remark 3.6
.

For later convenience, we denote
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon
in the case of

A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}
.

Define an element
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{(R,G)}\in O(F,I_{F})
by

\mathcal{T}=\begin{cases}E_{F}(b\otimes\rho)&\mbox{if $(R,G)$ is of type $BC_{l}^{(2,4)}$},\\
E_{F}((b+a)\otimes\rho)&\!\!\!\hskip 0.56905pt\begin{array}[]{l}\mbox{if $(R,G)$ is either of type}\\
\qquad\mbox{$A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}$, $B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}$ or $C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}$,}\end{array}\\
\mathrm{id}_{F}&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Note that the operator
\mathcal{T}
does not belong to
\mathrm{Aut}(R)
in general.

Set
F_{f}^{sp}=\mathcal{T}(F_{f})
. Then, we have a decomposition

(35)

F=F_{f}^{sp}\oplus\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}).

(A) If
(R,G)
is an mERS except for of type
BC_{l}^{(2,4)}
,
A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}
,

B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}
and
C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}
, the operator
\mathcal{T}
is
\mathrm{id}_{F}
.
Therefore, we have
F_{f}^{sp}=F_{f}
and the direct sum decomposition (
35
)
of
F
coincides with (
33
).

(B) Assume
(R,G)
is of type
BC_{l}^{(2,4)}
,
A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}
,

B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}
or
C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}
. Recalling the explicit description of

\alpha_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq l)
in Appendix
C
, we have

(36)

F_{f}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{R}\varepsilon_{i}

for such an
(R,G)
.
Set
\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}=-\mathcal{T}(\varepsilon_{i})\ (1\leq i\leq l)
.
The explicit form of
\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}
is given as

(37)

\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}-\varepsilon_{i}+\tfrac{1}{2}b&\mbox{if }(R,G)=BC_{l}^{(2,4)},\\
-\varepsilon_{i}+b+a&\mbox{if }(R,G)=A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast},B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast},\\
-\varepsilon_{i}+\tfrac{1}{2}b+\tfrac{1}{2}a&\mbox{if }(R,G)=C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}.\end{array}\right.

By (
36
), it follows immediately that

F_{f}^{sp}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{R}\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}.

Note that
F_{f}^{sp}\neq F_{f}
in these cases.

2
. Second,
let
(R,G)
be a mERS of type
BC_{l}^{(2,4)}
,
A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}
,

B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}
or
C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}
. By the explicit description of
the set
R
in Appendix
C
for such a mERS and
the formula (
37
), we have a description of
R
in terms of a basis

\varepsilon_{1}^{sp},\ldots,\varepsilon_{l}^{sp},b,a
of
F
as follows:

(38)

\begin{array}[]{lll}R&\!\!=\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{ll}\pm\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}+\left(r+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)b+sa&(1\leq i\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}),\\
\pm 2\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}+2rb+4sa&(1\leq i\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}),\\
\pm\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}\pm\check{\varepsilon}_{j}+rb+2sa&(1\leq i<j\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z})\end{array}\right\}&\!\!\mbox{for }BC_{l}^{(2,4)},\\[20.0pt]
R&\!\!=\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{ll}\pm\varepsilon_{1}^{sp}+rb+sa\ \ \left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}1\leq i\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}\mbox{ such that}\\
(r-1)(s-1)\equiv 0\mbox{ mod }2\end{array}\!\!\right)\end{array}\!\!\right\}&\!\!\mbox{for }A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast},\\[12.0pt]
R&\!\!=\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{ll}\pm\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}+rb+sa\ \ \left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}1\leq i\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}\mbox{ such that}\\
(r-1)(s-1)\equiv 0\mbox{ mod }2\end{array}\!\!\right),\\[10.0pt]
\pm\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}\pm\varepsilon_{j}^{sp}+2rb+2sa\ \ (1\leq i<j\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z})\end{array}\!\!\right\}&\!\!\mbox{for }B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast},\\[20.0pt]
R&\!\!=\left\{\!\!\begin{array}[]{ll}\pm 2\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}+rb+sa\ \ \left(\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}1\leq i\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}\mbox{ such that}\\
(r-1)(s-1)\equiv 0\mbox{ mod }2\end{array}\!\!\right),\\[10.0pt]
\pm\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}\pm\varepsilon_{j}^{sp}+rb+sa\ \ (1\leq i<j\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z})\end{array}\!\!\right\}&\!\!\mbox{for }C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}.\end{array}

These descriptions are used in the proof of Lemma
3.5
.

3
. Third, introduce two subgroups
O_{F_{f}}(F,I_{F})

and
O_{F_{f}^{sp}}(F,I_{F})
of
O(F,I_{F})
by

O_{F_{f}^{\natural}}(F,I_{F})=\{\psi\in O(F,I_{F})\ |\ \psi(F_{f}^{\natural})\subset F_{f}^{\natural}\}\quad(\natural=\emptyset\,\mbox{or}\,sp).

Then, we have a group isomorphism

Ad_{\mathcal{T}}\ :\ O_{F_{f}}(F,I_{F})\xrightarrow{\sim}O_{F_{f}^{sp}}(F,I_{F}),\quad\psi\ \longmapsto\ \mathcal{T}\psi\mathcal{T}^{-1}.

Consider an injective group homomorphism

(39)

\chi_{F_{f}}\ :\ GL(\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F}))\longrightarrow O_{F_{f}}(F,I_{F}),\quad g\ \longmapsto\ \chi_{F_{f}}(g)

where

\chi_{F_{f}}(g)|_{L_{f}}=\mathrm{id}_{F_{f}},\chi_{F_{f}}(g)|_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}=g
, a
nd define

\chi_{F_{f}^{sp}}\ :\ GL(\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F}))\longrightarrow O_{F_{f}^{sp}}(F,I_{F}),\quad g\ \longmapsto\ Ad_{\mathcal{T}}\big(\chi_{F_{f}}(g)\big).

Since the restriction
\mathcal{T}|_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}
is equal to

\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}
, we have

\chi_{F_{f}^{sp}}(g)|_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}=g

for every
g\in GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Remark 3.7
.

(1) Restricting these homomorphisms, we have group homomorphisms

\chi_{F_{f}^{\natural}}|_{SL(\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F}))}\ :\ SL(\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F}))\longrightarrow O_{F_{f}^{\natural}}(F,I_{F})\quad\mbox{for }\natural=\emptyset\ \mbox{or}\ sp.

Unless there is a risk of confusion, we denote them by

\chi_{F_{f}^{\natural}}=\chi_{F_{f}^{\natural}}|_{SL(\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I_{F}))}
, for
simplicity.

(2) By the definition, the operator
\iota_{F_{f}}
introduced in (
34
)
coincides with
\chi_{F_{f}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\right)
.

Now, let us prove Lemma
3.5
. It is enough to prove the following
claim.

Claim
.

For every
g\in\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)
, the element
\chi_{L_{f}^{sp}}(g)
belongs to

\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)
. That is,
\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)
is a subgroup of

\Gamma^{+}(R)
.

Proof.

Assume
F_{f}^{sp}=F_{f}
. Then, the statement follows from case-by-case
brute-force computation by using the explicit description of the set
R
in
Appendix
C
. Since it is straightforward,
we leave the proof to the reader.

If
F_{f}^{sp}\neq F_{f}
, we use the description (
38
) of
R
. In the following,
we give a proof only for of type
B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}
. For the other case, the lemma is
obtained by similar method.

Recalling the description (
38
) of the set
R
for
B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}
,
it is divided into two pieces
R=R_{s}\sqcup R_{l}
where

\begin{array}[]{ll}R_{s}:=\left\{\pm\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}+rb+sa\,\left|\begin{array}[]{c}1\leq i\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}\mbox{ such that}\\
(r-1)(s-1)\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,2)\end{array}\right.\!\!\right\},\\[10.0pt]
R_{l}:=\{\pm\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}\pm\varepsilon_{j}^{sp}+2rb+2sa\,|\,1\leq i<j\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}\}.\end{array}

Since
R_{l}
is obviously preserved by
\chi_{F_{f}^{sp}}(g)
for every

g\in{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, it is enough to show

(40)

\chi_{F_{f}^{sp}}(g)(R_{s})\subset R_{s}\quad\mbox{for every }g=\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
z&w\end{pmatrix}\in{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

For
\alpha_{s}=\pm\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}+rb+sa\in R_{s}
, we have

\displaystyle\chi_{F_{f}^{sp}}(g)(\alpha_{s})=\pm\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}+(rx+sy)b+(rz+sw)a.

By congruent expressions

xw+yz\equiv 1\ \mathrm{mod}\,2
,
rs+1\equiv r+s\ \mathrm{mod}\,2
,

r^{2}\equiv r\ \mathrm{mod}\,2
and
s^{2}\equiv s\ \mathrm{mod}\,2
which
are verified in this setting, we have

\displaystyle(rx+sy-1)(rz+sw-1)

\displaystyle\equiv r(x-1)(z-1)+s(y-1)(w-1)\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2.

Since
xw-yz=1
, at least one of
x-1
and
z-1
(
resp
.
y-1
and
w-1
) is
an even integer. Therefore, we have

\mbox{the right hand side}\equiv 0\ \mathrm{mod}\ 2.

Thus, (
40
) is obtained, and we have

\chi_{F_{f}^{sp}}(g)\in\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)
.
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

(41)

\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)=\Gamma^{+}(R).

Proof.

By the well-known fact in the theory of congruence subgroups
(see
[
Kob93
]
, for example)
, we have

[{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)]=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if }\,\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)={SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}),\\
3&\mbox{if }\,\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)=\Gamma_{0}(2),\Gamma^{0}(2),\\
4&\mbox{if }\,\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)=\Gamma_{0}(3),\Gamma^{0}(3).\end{cases}

1) If
\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)={SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
the assertion is obviously obtained by Lemma
3.5
.

2) Assume
\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)
is either
\Gamma_{0}(2)
or
\Gamma^{0}(2)
.
Namely,
(R,G)
is assumed to be of type
X_{l}^{(1,2)},X_{l}^{(2,1)},BC_{l}^{(2,4)},BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(1)
or
BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(2)
.
Since the index
[{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)]=3
is a prime number
in this case, the group
\Gamma^{+}(R)
is equal to either
\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)
or

{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Therefore, for proving the statement,
it is enough to show that
\Gamma^{+}(R)\neq{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.
More precisely, since the element

S:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})

belongs to neither
\Gamma_{0}(2)
nor
\Gamma^{0}(2)
, it is enough to prove that
there is no element
\psi\in\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)
such that
\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{rad}(I),F}(\psi)=S
.

In the following, we will show the assertion only for of type
BC_{l}^{(2,1)}
.
For the other cases, the statement is obtained by a similar method.
Assume there exists an element
\psi\in\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)
such that

\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),F}(\psi)=S
. That is, we assume

\psi(b)=a\quad\mbox{and}\quad\psi(a)=-b.

Recall the explicit description of the sets
R_{s}
of short roots, and
R_{l}
of long roots
for of type
BC_{l}^{(2,1)}
:

\displaystyle R_{s}

\displaystyle=\{\pm\varepsilon_{i}+rb+sa\,|\,1\leq i\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}\},

\displaystyle R_{l}

\displaystyle=\{\pm 2\varepsilon_{i}+(2r+1)b+sa\,|\,1\leq i\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}\},

in Appendix
C
.
Since
\varepsilon_{i}\in R_{s}
and
\psi
preserves the
set
R_{s}
, the element
\psi(\varepsilon_{i})\in R_{s}
has the following form:

\psi(\varepsilon_{i})=\kappa_{i}\varepsilon_{i}+r_{i}b+s_{i}a\quad(\kappa_{i}\in\{\pm 1\},\ r_{i},s_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\psi(2\varepsilon_{i}+b)

\displaystyle=2\psi(\varepsilon_{i})+\psi(b)=2\kappa_{i}\varepsilon_{i}+2r_{i}b+(2s_{i}+1)a.

On the other hand , since
2\varepsilon_{i}+b
is a long root of type
BC_{l}^{(2,1)}
,
the image
\psi(2\varepsilon_{i}+b)
should belong to
R_{l}
. Therefore, it has the
following form:

\psi(2\varepsilon_{i}+b)=2\kappa^{\prime}_{i}\varepsilon_{i}+(2r+1)b+sa\quad\mbox{for some }\kappa^{\prime}_{i}\in\{\pm 1\},\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}.

Therefore, we have

2r_{i}=2r+1.

But, this is a contradiction. Thus, the group

\Gamma^{+}(R)
does not coincide with
{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, and it should be
equal to
\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)=\Gamma_{0}(2)
, as desired.

3) Assume
(R,G)
is of type
G_{2}^{(1,3)}
. In this case, the group

\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)
coinsides with
\Gamma_{0}(3)
, and

[{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}),\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)]=4
. A complete set of coset
representatives of
{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z})/\Gamma_{0}^{+}(R)
is given by

\left\{E:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\,S,\,U:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-1&1\end{pmatrix},\,U^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-2&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}.

For each
g=S,U,U^{2}
, one can check that there is no element

\psi\in\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)
such that
\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),F}(\psi)=g

by a similar method as in the previous case. Thus, we have

\Gamma^{+}(R)=\Gamma_{0}(3)=\Gamma^{+}_{0}(R).

In the case of
(R,G)
being of type
G_{2}^{(3,1)}
, the statement can be proved by
a similar method. Thus, the proposition is obtained.
∎

Theorem 3.9
.

Both the upper and lower horizontal short exact sequences in
(
32
) split. That is, there are semidirect product
expressions

(42)

\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)\cong\mathrm{Aut}(R,\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))\rtimes\Gamma^{+}(R),

(43)

\mathrm{Aut}(R)\cong\mathrm{Aut}(R,\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))\rtimes\big(\Gamma^{+}(R)\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\big).

Proof.

First, we prove the upper horizontal short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))\longrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)\ \xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),F}}\ \Gamma^{+}(R)\longrightarrow 1

in (
32
) splits. By Lemma
3.5
and
Proposition
3.8
, it follows that the restriction

\chi_{F_{f}^{sp}}|_{\Gamma^{+}(R)}
induces an injective
homomorphism:
\Gamma^{+}(R)\to\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)
such that

\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),F}\circ\chi_{F_{f}^{sp}}=\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma^{+}(R)}
,
as desired.

Next, let us prove the lower horizontal short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))\longrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(R)\ \xrightarrow{\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),F}}\ \Gamma(R)\longrightarrow 1

in (
32
) splits. Recall the involutive automorphism

\iota_{L_{f}}=\chi_{L_{f}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\right)\in\mathrm{Aut}(R)

introduced in (
34
) (see Remark
3.7
(2), also), and set

(44)

\iota_{F_{f}^{sp}}=Ad_{\mathcal{T}}(\iota_{F_{f}}).

Lemma 3.10
.

The involution
\iota_{F_{f}^{sp}}
belongs to
\mathrm{Aut}(R)
.

Proof of the lemma
.
If
F_{f}^{sp}=F_{f}
, there is nothing to prove. Assume
F_{f}^{sp}\neq F_{f}
. That is,

(R,G)
is assumed to be of type
BC_{l}^{(2,4)}
,
A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}
,
B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}

or
C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}
. By the definition (
3.3
) of
\mathcal{T}
, we have

\iota_{F_{f}^{sp}}\ :\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{lllllll}\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}&\!\!\!\!\longmapsto&\!\!\!\varepsilon_{i}^{sp}&\mbox{for }1\leq i\leq l,\\
b&\!\!\!\!\longmapsto&\!\!\!b,\\
a&\!\!\!\!\longmapsto&\!\!\!-a.\end{array}\right.

By the explicit description (
38
) of
R
, it is easy to see that
the set
R
is preserved by
\iota_{F_{f}^{sp}}
. Thus, we have the lemma.

\square

Consider the semidirect product
\Gamma^{+}(R)\rtimes\langle\iota_{F_{f}^{sp}}\rangle

in
GL(F)
. By the above lemma, it follows that this group coincides with
\Gamma(R)

and the restriction
\chi_{F_{f}^{sp}}|_{\Gamma(R)}
induces an injective
homomorphism:
\Gamma(R)\to\mathrm{Aut}(R)
such that

\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),F}\circ\chi_{F_{f}^{sp}}=\mathrm{id}_{\Gamma(R)}
.
Thus, the theorem is proved.
∎

3.4.
The group
\mathrm{Aut}(R,\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))

Let us study the group
\mathrm{Aut}(R,\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))
.
The canonical projection
\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}:F\to F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})

induces a
morphism

(\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}:\mathrm{Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))\to\mathrm{Aut}\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big),

where

\displaystyle\mbox{Aut}\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)

\displaystyle\quad:=\bigl\{\phi\in O\bigl(F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),I_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}\bigr)\,\bigl|\,\phi\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)=R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\bigr\}.

Proposition 3.11
.

(1)

The morphism
(\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}:\mathrm{Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))\to\mathrm{Aut}\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)
is surjective. Therefore, there exists the canonical exact
sequence

(45)

1\to\mathrm{Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)\to\mathrm{Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))\xrightarrow{(\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}}\mathrm{Aut}\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)\to 1.

(2)

The above exact sequence splits. The group

\mathrm{Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))
is isomorphic to the
semidirect product

\mathrm{Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)
.

Proof.

(1) Let
\phi\in\mathrm{Aut}\big(R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})\big)
.
For
1\leq i\leq l
, there exist integers

m_{j,i}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

\phi(\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}(\alpha_{i}))=\sum_{j=1}^{l}m_{j,i}\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}(\alpha_{j}).

Define
\psi_{\phi}\in{GL}(F)
by

\psi_{\phi}(\alpha_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{l}m_{j,i}\alpha_{j}\quad(1\leq i\leq l),\qquad\psi_{\phi}(\delta_{\sharp}):=\delta_{\sharp}\quad(\sharp=b,a).

Then, it is easy to see that the map
\psi_{\phi}
belongs to

\mbox{\rm Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))
and

(\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}(\psi_{\phi})=\phi
.

(2) It is immediate to see that the correspondence

\phi\mapsto\psi_{\phi}
defines a group homomorphism

\zeta:\mathrm{Aut}\big(R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})\big)\to\mathrm{Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))

such that the condition

(\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\circ\zeta=\mathrm{id}

is satisfied. That is, it gives a section for the exact sequence (
45
),
and the assertion (2) is verified.
∎

Let
\Gamma\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)
be the Dynkin diagram of the finite root system

R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
and denote the group of diagram automorphisms of

\Gamma\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)
by

\mathrm{Aut}\big(\Gamma\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)\big)
.
It is known that the group
\mathrm{Aut}\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)
of automorphisms of
the finite root system
R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
is isomorphic to
the semidirect product
W\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}\big(\Gamma\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)\big)
.

Combining these results, we have the following corollary:

Corollary 3.12
.

\begin{split}&\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)\\
\cong&\bigg(\mathrm{Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)\rtimes\Big(W\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}\big(\Gamma\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)\big)\Big)\bigg)\rtimes\Gamma^{+}(R).\end{split}

Note that the structure of the finite Weyl group
W\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)
and the group

\mathrm{Aut}\big(\Gamma\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)\big)
of diagram automorphisms are
known. In addition, the explicit form of the elliptic modular group
\Gamma^{+}(R)
of

R
is already studied in the previous subsection. Therefore,
the study of
\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)
reduces to one of the kernel

\mathrm{Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)
.

The dual weight lattice
P\big((R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)

of the finite quotient root system
R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
is defined by

\displaystyle P\big((R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\{\mu\in F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})\,\big|\,I_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(\mu,\gamma)\in\mathbb{Z}\mbox{ for every }\gamma\in R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big\}.

Let
\Pi=\{\alpha_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq l}
be a simple system of
(R,G)
in the sense of
Definition
2.1
. Set

\Pi_{f}=\Pi\setminus\{\alpha_{0}\}.

Remark 3.13
.

The image
\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(\Pi_{f})=\big\{\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(\alpha_{i})\big\}_{1\leq i\leq l}
is a simple system of the finite
quotient root system
R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
, even when
R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
is non-reduced.

Since the restriction
I_{F_{f}}

:=I\big|_{F_{f}\times F_{f}}
is non-degenerate, one can take the
dual basis
(\varpi_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,\varpi_{l}^{\vee})
of
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l})

with respect to
I_{F_{f}}
. That is,
\{\varpi_{i}^{\vee}\}_{1\leq i\leq l}
is a subset of
F_{f}

so that

I_{F_{f}}(\varpi_{i}^{\vee},\alpha_{j})=\delta_{i,j}\quad\text{for every }1\leq i,j\leq l.

Then the image
\big(\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F_{f}})}(\varpi_{1}^{\vee}),\ldots,\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F_{f}})}(\varpi_{l}^{\vee})\big)
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of the lattice

P\big((R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)
:

P\big((R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{Z}\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F_{f}})}(\varpi_{i}^{\vee}).

Set

P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{\sharp}={\sharp}\otimes P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)\quad\mbox{for }\sharp=b,a.

The following lemma describes the structure of the group

\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)
.

Lemma 3.14
.

We have

(46)

\displaystyle\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\psi\in E_{F}\bigl(P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{b}\oplus P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{a}\bigr)\,\bigl|\,\psi(R)=R\bigr\}.

Proof.

Assume that
\psi
is an element of the right hand side of
(\ref{eqn:Ker1})
.
Then, by direct computation, one can easily check that

\psi\in\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)
.

Conversely, let
\psi\in\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)
. Then,
by the definition, we have

\psi|_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}=\mbox{id},\quad\psi(\alpha_{i})\equiv\alpha_{i}\mbox{ mod rad}(I_{F})\quad\mbox{for every }1\leq i\leq l.

Since
\psi
preserves the root lattice

Q(R)=\bigl(\oplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}\bigr)\oplus\mathbb{Z}b\oplus\mathbb{Z}a
, there exist
\mu_{b},\mu_{a}\in\mbox{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}\big(Q(R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})),\mathbb{Z}\big)
such that

\psi(\beta)=\beta-\mu_{b}(\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}(\beta))b-\mu_{a}(\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}(\beta))a\quad\mbox{for every }\beta\in Q(R).

As
I_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}
is non-degenerate, there exist

u_{b},u_{a}\in P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)
such that

\mu_{\sharp}(\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}(\beta))=I_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}(\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}(\beta),u_{\sharp})\quad\mbox{for }\sharp=b,a.

Therefore, we have

\psi=E_{F}(b\otimes u_{b}+a\otimes u_{a})\in E_{F}\big(P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{b}\oplus P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{a}\big).

Thus, we have the lemma.
∎

For
\psi=E_{F}(b\otimes u_{b}+a\otimes u_{a})
, set

\psi_{\sharp}:=E_{F}({\sharp}\otimes u_{\sharp})\in E_{F}\bigr(P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{\sharp}\bigr)\quad\mbox{for }\sharp=b,a.

Then, by the definition of the Eichler-Siegel map
E_{F}
, it is easy to see

(47)

\psi=\psi_{a}\psi_{b}=\psi_{b}\psi_{a}.

In other words, the group
E_{F}\bigl(P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{b}\oplus P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{a}\bigr)

decomposes into the (internal) direct sum of

E_{F}\bigl(P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{\sharp}\bigr)

(\sharp=b,a)
:

(48)

\displaystyle E_{F}\bigl(P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{b}\oplus P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{a}\bigr)

\displaystyle=E_{F}\bigl(P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{b}\bigr)\oplus E_{F}\bigl(P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{a}\bigr).

Lemma 3.15
.

For
\psi=E_{F}(b\otimes u_{b}+a\otimes u_{a})
, the following conditions are
equivalent
:

(a)

It belongs to
\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr).

(b)

For every
\sharp=b,a
,
\psi_{\sharp}(R)=R.

Proof.

Note that, by Lemma
3.14
, the condition (a) is equivalent to
\psi(R)=R
.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a):  This part follows obviously by (
47
).

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b):  Using an explicit description for the set
R
in Appendix

C
,
one can show the statement by case by case analysis. In the following, we give a
explicit proof only for the case of
B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}
. For other cases, the statement
is proved by a similar way to this case with an easier argument.

Assume
(R,G)
is of type
B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}
. Recall the explicit description
of the set
R=R_{s}\sqcup R_{l}
in Appendix
C
:

(49)

\begin{array}[]{ll}R_{s}=\{\pm\varepsilon_{i}+rb+sa\,|\,1\leq i\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}\mbox{ such that }rs\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,2)\},\\[3.0pt]
R_{l}=\{\pm\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}+2rb+2sa\,|\,1\leq i<j\leq l,\,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}\}.\end{array}.

Take
\psi=E_{F}(b\otimes u_{b}+a\otimes u_{a})\in\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)
. Since
\psi(R_{s})=R_{s}
,
an element

\displaystyle\psi(\pm\varepsilon_{i}+rb+sa)

\displaystyle=\pm\varepsilon_{i}+(r\mp I_{F}(u_{b},\varepsilon_{i}))b+(s\mp I_{F}(u_{a},\varepsilon_{i}))a

should belong to
R_{s}
for every pair
r,s
such that

rs\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,2)
. That is,

\displaystyle(r\mp I_{F}(u_{b},\varepsilon_{i}))(s\mp I_{F}(u_{a},\varepsilon_{i}))\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\,2).

Therefore, we have

(50)

I_{F}(u_{b},\varepsilon_{i})\in 2\mathbb{Z}\quad\mbox{and}\quad I_{F}(u_{a},\varepsilon_{i})\in 2\mathbb{Z}.

By the condition (
50
), it follows that

\displaystyle\psi_{b}(\pm\varepsilon_{i}+rb+sa)

\displaystyle=\pm\varepsilon_{i}+(r\mp I_{F}(u_{b},\varepsilon_{i}))b+sa

and

(r\mp I_{F}(u_{b},\varepsilon_{i}))s\equiv 0\ (\mathrm{mod}\,2).

Therefore,
\psi_{b}
preserves the set
R_{s}
of short roots.

On the other hand, for every
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}
, an element

\displaystyle\psi_{b}(\pm\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}+2rb+2sa)

\displaystyle=\pm\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}+(\mp I_{F}(u_{b},\varepsilon_{i})\mp I_{F}(u_{b},\varepsilon_{j})+2r)b+2sa

belongs to
R_{l}
under the condition (
50
). That is,

\psi_{b}
preserves the set
R_{l}
of long roots. Combining these two results,
we have
\psi_{b}(R)=R
.

By a similar way, we have
\psi_{a}(R)=R
. Thus, the condition (b) is satisfied, and
the statement is proved in this case.
∎

For
\sharp=b,a
, define

\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)_{\sharp}=\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)\cap E_{F}\bigl(P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{\sharp}\bigr).

The following corollary is immediately verified.

Corollary 3.16
.

(1)
For each
\sharp=b,a
, we have

\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)_{\sharp}=\bigl\{\psi_{\sharp}\in E_{F}\bigl(P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{\sharp}\bigr)\,\bigl|\,\psi_{\sharp}(R)=R\bigr\}.

(2)
There is an induced direct sum decomposition of

\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)
:

\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)=\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)_{b}\bigoplus\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)_{a}.

Since
E_{F}\big(P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)_{\sharp}\big)=E_{F}\big(\sharp\otimes P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)\big)
is a free abelian
group and
\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)_{\sharp}
is a
subgroup of it for each
\sharp=b,a
, there exist a lattice

M_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),\sharp}\subset P\big((R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)

such that

\mbox{\rm Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)_{\sharp}=E_{F}(\delta_{\sharp}\otimes M_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),\sharp})\quad\mbox{for }\sharp=b,a.

Thus, we have the following proposition:

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
(R,G)
be a mERS in the K. Saito’s list. There
exist sublattices
M_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),a},M_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),b}\subset P\big((R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)

such that

\mathrm{Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)=E_{F}\big(a\otimes M_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),a}\big)\bigoplus E_{F}\big(b\otimes M_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),b}\bigr).

Let us determine the lattice
M_{F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}),\sharp}\ (\sharp=b,a)
in explicit way. Since the results depend on specific calculations for each
case, we only describe the strategy here.

Recall the dual basis

(\varpi_{1}^{\vee},\ldots,\varpi_{l}^{\vee})
of
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l})
with respect
to
I_{F}
which was introduced after Remark
3.13
.
Under the identification

\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})}:F_{f}\overset{\sim}{\to}F/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})
,
they are regarded as the fundamental coweights of
R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})
, and
the coweight lattice
P\big(R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)
is identified with

\oplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{Z}\varpi_{i}^{\vee}
.
Recall
\psi_{\sharp}=E_{F}(\delta_{\sharp}\otimes u_{\sharp})
with

u_{\sharp}\in P(R_{f}^{\vee})
for
\sharp=b,a
(see (
3.4
)). By using
an
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
\varpi_{i}^{\vee}\ (1\leq i\leq l)
, the condition (b) of Lemma

3.15
is described in an explicit way.

We give an explicit description of the lattice

M_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),\sharp}\ (\sharp=a,b)
for each
(R,G)
in the following table.

Here,

is the sub-lattice of

P\big((R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)
defined by

\widetilde{P}\big((R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))^{\vee}\big)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\frac{I_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}\big(\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(\alpha_{i}),\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(\alpha_{i})\big)}{I_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(\gamma_{s},\gamma_{s})}\mathbb{Z}\pi_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(\varpi_{i}^{\vee}),

where
\gamma_{s}\in R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})
is a short root.

3.5.
The outer automorphisms

It is easy to see that the Weyl group
W(R)
is a normal subgroup of

\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)
. Set

\mathrm{Out}^{+}(R)=\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R)/W(R).

Call an element of
\mathrm{Out}^{+}(R)
(orientation preserving)
outer
automorphism
of
R
. Since an element
w\in W(R)
acts on the radical

\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})
trivially,
W(R)
is a (normal) subgroup of

\mathrm{Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))
. Dividing the upper horizontal exact sequence
in (
32
) by
W(R)
, we have a new exact sequence

(51)

1\longrightarrow\Omega(R)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Out}^{+}(R)\ \longrightarrow\Gamma^{+}(R)\longrightarrow 1,

where

\Omega(R):=\mathrm{Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}({I_{F}}))/W(R),

and call it the
\Omega
-part
of
\mathrm{Out}^{+}(R)
.
Furthermore, as an immediate
consequence of Theorem
3.9
, we get the following corollary.

Corollary 3.18
.

The exact sequence (
51
) also splits. That is, the group

\mathrm{Out}^{+}(R)
is isomorphic to the
semidirect product
\Omega(R)\rtimes\Gamma^{+}(R)
.

In the rest of this subsection, we study the group
\Omega(R)
.
By Proposition
3.3
and Proposition
3.17
, we have

\mathrm{Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)\cap W(R)=E_{F}(T_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(R)).

Therefore, by exact sequences (
29
) and (
45
),
we have the following commutative diagram in which all vertical and horizontal
sequences are exact:

(52)

378.99904pt\raise-99.4625pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-131.54999pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 88.13043pt\raise-131.54999pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 211.3736pt\raise-131.54999pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 310.08644pt\raise-131.54999pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 381.31152pt\raise-131.54999pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array},

where
\overline{(\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}}:\Omega(R)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}\big(\Gamma(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}))\big)
is the induced group homomorphism.
As a corollary of Proposition
3.11
, we have the following result.

Corollary 3.19
.

The lower horizontal exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\dfrac{\mathrm{Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)}{E_{F}(T_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(R))}\longrightarrow\Omega(R)\xrightarrow{\overline{(\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})}_{*}}\mathrm{Aut}\big(\Gamma\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)\big)\longrightarrow 1

of
(
52
)
splits. In other words, the group
\Omega(R)
is
isomorphic to a semidirect product

\dfrac{\mathrm{Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)}{E_{F}(T_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(R))}\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}\big(\Gamma\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)\big)
.

Proof.

Recall the section
\zeta:\mathrm{Aut}(R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))\longrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))

of the exact sequence (
45
) which is constructed in the proof of
Proposition
3.11
(2). Consider a composition

\mathrm{Aut}(R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))\overset{\zeta}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))\longrightarrow\dfrac{\mathrm{Aut}(R,\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))}{W(R)}\ (=\Omega(R)).

Since
\zeta\big(W(R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))\big)\subset W(R)
, a group homomorphism

\overline{\zeta}:\dfrac{\mathrm{Aut}(R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))}{W(R/\mathrm{rad}(I_{F}))}\longrightarrow\Omega(R)

is naturally induced. Furthermore, by the definition, the condition

\overline{(\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})}_{*}\circ\overline{\zeta}=\mathrm{id}

is satisfied. That is, the lemma is obtained.
∎

Combining the above results, we have

\begin{split}\Omega(R)\cong&\dfrac{\mathrm{Ker}\bigl((\pi_{\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})})_{*}\bigr)}{E_{F}(T_{\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})}(R))}\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}\big(\Gamma\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)\big)\\
\cong&\left(\dfrac{M_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),a}}{Q_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),a}}\bigoplus\dfrac{M_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),b}}{Q_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),b}}\right)\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}\big(\Gamma\big(R/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F})\big)\big).\end{split}

In the following table, we give an explicit description of the group
\Omega(R)
.

Table 3.
An explicit description of
\Omega(R)

(R,G)

\frac{M_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),a}}{Q_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),a}}

\frac{M_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),b}}{Q_{F/\operatorname{rad}(I_{F}),b}}

Part II
New marked elliptic root systems

In this chapter, we construct all possible marked elliptic root systems
(R,G)
with non-reduced affine quotient
R/G
.
Let
R_{s},R_{m}
and
R_{l}
be the subsets of the short (resp. middle length and long) roots in
R
. Since the subsets

R_{s}\cup R_{m}
and
R_{m}\cup R_{l}
are reduced elliptic root systems, this can be achieved
by “gluing” two reduced elliptic root systems.

In Section
4
, we give all possible marked elliptic root systems explicitly and state the weak classification (Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
); we do
not
prove that two marked elliptic root systems in the above list are non-isomorphic as marked root systems, in this chapter. Section
5
is devoted to the proof of the last two theorems. Here, after explaining what we mean by “gluing root systems”, we prove these two theorems by performing gluing all possible pairs of elliptic root systems of type
B_{l}
and of type
C_{l}
. In Section
6
, we
study some properties of marked elliptic root systems
(R,G)
with non-reduced affine quotient
R/G
. In
\lx@sectionsign

6.1
, we classify the isomorphism classes of the marked elliptic root systems listed above, as root systems (Theorems
6.1
and
6.2
). In
\lx@sectionsign

6.2
, we study affine quotients of a given elliptic root system.

4.
MERSs with non-reduced quotient

Let
(F,I)
be an
\mathbb{R}
-vector space with a quadratic form whose signature is
(l,2,0)
.
Here we introduce several mERSs
(R,G)
with non-reduced affine quotient
R/G
. It should be noticed that the finite quotient of any such root system is of type
BC_{l}
. In this section, we fix the marking
G=\mathbb{R}a
.

4.1.
Reduced mERSs

First consider the reduced
R
’s. Since there are only 6 such cases, we call each type of the mERS by making reference to
BC_{l}
.

4.1.1.
Types
BC_{l}^{(1,2)},BC_{l}^{(4,2)},BC_{l}^{(2,2)\sigma}(1),BC_{l}^{(2,2)\sigma}(2)

Each root system
R
of the above type is defined by

R=(R(X_{l})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a)\cup(R(X_{l})_{m}+\mathbb{Z}a)\cup(R(X_{l})_{l}+(1+2\mathbb{Z})a),

where
R(X_{l})
, for each
R
, is the affine root system given by the following table:

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{m}+2\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{l}+2\mathbb{Z}b),

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{m}+2\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{l}+2\mathbb{Z}b),

and the root systems of type

There are two mERS of

\displaystyle R(BC_{l}^{(1,1)\ast})=

and

Notice that they had been discovered by S. Azam
[

]
in 2002.
Hence, there is essentially no newly found reduced mERS with non-reduced affine quotient.

4.2.
Non-reduced mERSs

Now consider the non-reduced
(R,G)
’s. Since there are many such mERSs, we call each type of the mERS by making reference to its affine quotient

, which is one of the types
BCC_{l},C^{\vee}BC_{l},BB_{l}^{\vee}
and
C^{\vee}C_{l}
. See Appendix
B
for detailed information on non-reduced affine root systems.

4.2.3.
\diamond
-type

There is only one such root system which we may call of type
C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\diamond}
. This root system
R
is defined as follows:

R=(R(C^{\vee}C_{l})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a)\cup(R(C^{\vee}C_{l})_{m}+\mathbb{Z}a)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{l}+\{\,ma+2nb\,|\,m-n\equiv 0[2]\,\}).

For detailed information about this root system, see §
10.5.11
.

4.3.
Weak classification

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
(R,G)
be a reduced marked elliptic root system belonging to the real vector space
with symmetric bilinear form of signature
(l,2,0)
. Assume that the quotient root system
R/G
is non-reduced. Then it is isomorphic to one of the following:

BC_{l}^{(1,2)},\qquad BC_{l}^{(1,1)\ast},\qquad BC_{l}^{(4,2)},\qquad BC_{l}^{(4,4)\ast},\qquad BC_{l}^{(2,2)\sigma}(2)

for
l\geq 1
and for
l\geq 2
, there is also

BC_{l}^{(2,2)\sigma}(1).

Here, by the same way as we have stated after Theorem
2.9
, we see that all of these mERSs are non-isomorphic. Thus, this classification gives the classification of the isomorphism classes of reduced mERSs with non-reduced affine quotient.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
(R,G)
be a non-reduced marked elliptic root system belonging to the real vector space
with symmetric bilinear form of signature
(l,2,0)
. Then,
as marked elliptic root system, it is isomorphic to one of the following:

1.
R/G
of type
BCC_{l}

(l\geq 1)
:

\displaystyle BCC_{l}^{(1)},\;BCC_{l}^{(1)\ast_{0}},\;BCC_{l}^{(1)\ast_{0^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle BCC_{l}^{(2)}(1)\,(l>1),\;BCC_{l}^{(2)}(2),\;BCC_{l}^{(2)\ast_{p}}\,(p\in\{0,1\}),

\displaystyle BCC_{l}^{(4)}.

2.
R/G
of type
C^{\vee}BC_{l}

(l\geq 1)
:

\displaystyle C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(1)},

\displaystyle C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(2)}(1)\,(l>1),\;C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(2)}(2),\;C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(2)\ast_{p}}\,(p\in\{0,1\}),

\displaystyle C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(4)},\;C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(4)\ast_{0}},\;C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(4)\ast_{0^{\prime}}}.

3.
R/G
of type
BB_{l}^{\vee}

(l\geq 2)
:

\displaystyle BB_{l}^{\vee\,(1)},\;BB_{l}^{\vee\,(2)}(1)\,(l>1),\;BB_{l}^{\vee\,(2)}(2),\;BB_{l}^{\vee\,(4)},

\displaystyle BB_{2}^{\vee(2)\ast}.

4.
R/G
of type
C^{\vee}C_{l}

(l\geq 1)
:

\displaystyle C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(1)},\;C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(1)\ast_{p}}\,(p\in\{0,1\}),

\displaystyle C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)}(1)\,(l>1),\;C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)}(2),\;C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{s}},\;C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{l}},

\displaystyle C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{0}},\;C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1}},\;C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1^{\prime}}},\;C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\diamond},

\displaystyle C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(4)},\;C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(4)\ast_{p}}\,(p\in\{0,1\}).

5.
Proof of Theorems
4.1
and
4.2

Even if the obtained root systems are always reduced elliptic root systems, in view of the reduced mERSs, but still, Saito’s classification is not complete.

We classify now the reduced mERSs
(R,G)
where the quotient
R/G
is non-reduced.

5.1.
Preliminary

Here, we fix the following convention which will be used throughout this section.

Let
(F,I)
be the real vector space
F=\mathbb{R}^{l+2}
equipped with a positive semi-definite symmetric bilinear form
I
with
\dim\operatorname{rad}(I)=2
. Fix a basis
(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\cdots,\varepsilon_{l},b,a)
of
F
(cf. (
31
)) satisfying (
16
), i.e.,

I(\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j})=\delta_{i,j},\qquad\operatorname{rad}(I)=\mathbb{R}a\oplus\mathbb{R}b.

We realize the root systems of type
B_{l},C_{l}
and
D_{l}
in
F_{f}:=\bigoplus_{1\leq i\leq l}\mathbb{R}\varepsilon_{i}
as in Appendix
A
, i.e.,

\displaystyle R(D_{l})=\{\,\pm(\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j})\,|\,1\leq i<j\leq l\,\},

\displaystyle R(B_{l})=R(D_{l})\cup\{\,\pm\varepsilon_{i}\,\}_{1\leq i\leq l},\quad R(C_{l})=R(D_{l})\cup\{\,\pm 2\varepsilon_{i}\ \}_{1\leq i\leq l}.

As the group of diagram automorphisms of type
B_{l}
and
C_{l}
are trivial, it follows that

\mathrm{Aut}(R_{f})=W(R_{f}),\qquad R_{f}=R(B_{l})\;\text{or}\;R(C_{l}).

For type
D_{l}
, the automorphism group of its Dynkin diagram is

\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma(D_{l}))\cong\begin{cases}\;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\quad&l\geq 2\;\text{s.t.}\;l\neq 4,\\
\;\mathfrak{S}_{3}\quad&l=4.\end{cases}

An outer automorphism of
R(D_{l})
is given by

\sigma:\;F_{f}\longrightarrow F_{f};\quad\varepsilon_{i}\mapsto\begin{cases}\;\varepsilon_{i}\;&1\leq i<l\\
\;-\varepsilon_{l}\;&i=l\end{cases},

and this is realized as

r_{\varepsilon_{l}}=r_{2\varepsilon_{l}}\in W(B_{l})=W(C_{l}).

In particular,

W(B_{l})=W(C_{l})\cong W(D_{l})\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle.

For later use, set

\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R(D_{l}))=W(D_{l})\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle.

Remark that, with this notation,

\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R(D_{l}))=\mathrm{Aut}(R(B_{l}))=\mathrm{Aut}(R(C_{l}))\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{l}\rtimes\mathfrak{S}_{l}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0},}

and gives exactly the automorphism group
\mathrm{Aut}(R(D_{l}))
for
l\neq 4
(including the cases
l=2,3
). Nevertheless,

\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R(D_{4}))
is not even a normal subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(R(D_{4}))=W(D_{4})\rtimes\mathfrak{S}_{3}\cong W(F_{4})
.

5.2.
Key idea

Let
(R,G)
be a mERS whose quotient
R_{\mathrm{aff}}:=R/G
is non-reduced,
R_{l},\,R_{m}
and
R_{s}
be the subset of
R
consisting of the long, middle length and short roots, respectively. Both of the subsets
R_{+}:=R_{l}\cup R_{m}
and
R_{-}:=R_{m}\cup R_{s}
are reduced elliptic root systems. In particular, as we assume that
R_{\mathrm{aff}}
is a non-reduced affine root system, their quotients
by the full radical
\operatorname{rad}(I)
are of type
C_{l}
and
B_{l}
, respectively. By the classification of mERSs
(R,G)
whose quotient
R/G
is reduced due to K. Saito
[
Sai85
]
(cf. Appendix
C
), the possible type of the mERSs
(R_{-},G)
and
(R_{+},G)
are given in the next table:

Type of
(R_{-},G)

B_{l}^{(1,1)}

B_{l}^{(1,2)}

C_{2}^{(1,1)}

C_{2}^{(1,2)}

B_{l}^{(2,1)}

B_{l}^{(2,2)}

B_{l}^{(2,2)\ast}

C_{2}^{(1,1)\ast}

A_{1}^{(1,1)}

Type of
(R_{+},G)

C_{l}^{(2,2)}

C_{l}^{(2,1)}

B_{2}^{(2,2)}

B_{2}^{(2,1)}

C_{l}^{(1,2)}

C_{l}^{(1,1)}

C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}

B_{2}^{(2,2)\ast}

A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}

Range of
l

l>2

l=2

l\geq 2

l=2

l=1

For
t\in\{1,2\}
, set

\displaystyle R(B_{2}^{(1,t)})=

\displaystyle R(B_{2})+t\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b,

\displaystyle R(C_{2}^{(2,t)})=

\displaystyle(R(C_{2})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(C_{2})_{l}+t\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b),

to simplify further discussion.

Thus, to classify the mERSs
(R,G)
of rank
>1
, we just have to glue two mERSs, the one
(R_{-},G)
in the first line and the other
(R_{+},G)
in the second line, in the above table. Here, by the term “glue”, we mean the pushout

(R_{+},G)\bigcup_{(R_{m},G)}(R_{-},G)

where
R_{m}=R_{+}\cap R_{-}
is isomorphic to the elliptic root system of type
D_{l}^{(1,1)}
, except for the case where
R_{\pm}
are of type
C_{2}^{(1,1)\ast}
and
B_{2}^{(1,1)\ast}
, respectively. In the latter case,
R_{m}
is given by
R(D_{2}^{(1,1)\ast}):=R(D_{2})+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\}
.

The
gluing procedure
can be understood as follows. Let
R_{+}
and
R_{-}
be elliptic root systems realised in
(F,I)
as in Appendix
C
, whose full quotients are of type
C_{l}
and
B_{l}
respectively, such that
R_{m}:=R_{+}\cap R_{-}=(R_{+})_{s}=(R_{-})_{l}
is either of type
D_{l}^{(1,1)}
or
D_{2}^{(1,1)\ast}
. As any automorphism
\varphi
of
(R_{m},G)
is the restriction of a linear isomorphism of
(F,I)
, we can define a subset
R
of
(F,I)
by

R=R_{-}\amalg_{\varphi}R_{+}:=R_{-}\cup\varphi(R_{+}).

Remark 5.1
.

One can also consider the opposite gluing, i.e.,
R_{+}\amalg_{\varphi}R_{-}=R_{+}\cup\varphi(R_{-})
.
As the group of automorphisms of
C_{l}
-type elliptic root systems realized in

\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R(D_{l}^{(1,1)}),G):=\{\,\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R(D_{l}^{(1,1)}))\,|\,\varphi|_{G}=\mathrm{id}_{G}\}

has a smaller index (
1,2
or
4
) than that of
B_{l}
-type, we have chosen the gluing procedure as above for rank
>2
. For rank
2
cases, a convenient one will be chosen.

Since the full quotient
R/\operatorname{rad}(I)
is a root system of type
BC_{l}
, we may consider only those
\varphi
which belong to the subgroup
\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R(D_{l}^{(1,1)}),G)
of
\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R(D_{l}^{(1,1)}),G)
, isomorphic to

\left(E_{F}(a\otimes P(D_{l}^{\vee})+b\otimes P(D_{l}^{\vee}))\rtimes\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(D_{l})\right)\rtimes U_{-}

under the isomorphism in Corollary
3.12
, where we set
U_{-}
being the subgroup of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
generated by
{}^{t}T
. As we are interested in the classification of mERSs, we shall concentrate on
\Gamma^{+}(R)\cap U_{-}
. For a positive integer
n>0
, let
U_{-}(n)
be the subgroup of
U_{-}
generated by
({}^{t}T)^{n}
.
By Proposition
3.8
, we have the next list:

\Gamma^{+}(R)\cap U_{-}=\begin{cases}\;U_{-}\;&R\,\text{of type}\;X_{l}^{(t,t)},\,X_{l}^{(t,t)\ast},\,X_{l}^{(2,1)},\,BC_{l}^{(2,1)},\,G_{2}^{(3,1)},\\
\;U_{-}(2)\;&R\,\text{of type}\;X_{l}^{(1,2)},\,BC_{l}^{(2,4)},\,BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(1),\,BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(2),\\
\;U_{-}(3)\;&R\,\text{of type}\;G_{2}^{(1,3)}.\end{cases}

Lemma 5.2
.

The set
R=R_{-}\amalg_{\varphi}R_{+}
is a root system, i.e., it is stable under the action of the reflection group of
R
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that

(1)

R_{s}
is stable by
r_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in R_{l}\cong R(BC_{l})_{l}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b
, and

(2)

\varphi(R_{l})
is stable by
r_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in R_{s}\cong R(BC_{l})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b
,

since any such set is a subset of
R_{-}\amalg_{\mathrm{id}}R(C_{l}^{(1,1)})
and
R(B_{l}^{(r,r)})\amalg_{\varphi}R_{+}
with
r\in\{1,2\}
.
∎

Let
\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R_{\pm},G)=\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R_{\pm})\cap\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R_{m},G)
. For
\varphi_{\pm}\in\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R_{\pm},G)
and
\varphi_{0}\in\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R_{m},G)
, setting
\varphi=\varphi_{-}\circ\varphi_{0}\circ\varphi_{+}
, it follows that

\displaystyle R_{-}\amalg_{\varphi}R_{+}=

\displaystyle\varphi_{-}(R_{-})\cup(\varphi_{-}\circ\varphi_{0})(\varphi_{+}(R_{+}))=\varphi_{-}(R_{-}\cup\varphi_{0}(R_{+}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi_{-}(R_{-}\amalg_{\varphi_{0}}R_{+}),

namely,
R_{-}\amalg_{\varphi}R_{+}
is isomorphic to
R_{-}\amalg_{\varphi_{0}}R_{+}
. Hence,
it is enough to analyse
R_{-}\amalg_{\varphi}R_{+}
for each coset in
\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R_{-},G)\backslash\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R_{m},G)/\penalty\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R_{+},G)
.

For later use, for
\delta\in\operatorname{rad}(I)
, set

(53)

\tau_{\delta}=E_{F}\left((-\delta)\otimes(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}+\cdots+\varepsilon_{l})\right).

First classify non-reduced mERSs for
l\geq 2
. The cases when
l=1
will be treated separately.

Some cases require detailed analysis, and for each type of the subtleties we will indicate a way to handle them when it first appears; §
5.3
.(2)(I) and
5.6
.(2)(I).

5.3.
{R/G}
of type
BCC_{l}\;(l>1)

In this case, since

R_{+}/G\cong R(C_{l}^{(1)})\qquad\text{and}\qquad R_{-}/G\cong\begin{cases}\;R(B_{l}^{(1)})\;&l>2,\\
\;R(C_{2}^{(1)})\;&l=2,\end{cases}

the mERS
(R_{+},G)
is either of type
C_{l}^{(1,1)},\,C_{l}^{(1,2)}
or
C_{l}^{(1,1)\ast}
, and

(R_{-},G)
is either of type

(1)

B_{l}^{(1,1)}
or
B_{l}^{(1,2)}
for

and

(2)

C_{2}^{(1,1)}
or
C_{2}^{(1,2)}
for
l=2
.

(1).
The case
R_{+}=R(C_{l}^{(1,1)})
(
l>1
)

In this case,
\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R_{+},G)=\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R_{m},G)
and it is enough to consider the gluing via trivial automorphism.

(I)

R_{-}=\begin{cases}\;R(B_{l}^{(1,1)})\;&l>2,\\
\;R(C_{2}^{(1,1)})\;&l=2.\end{cases}
Here, we obtain the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{m}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{l}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{BCC_{l}^{(1)}}).

(II)

R_{-}=\begin{cases}\;R(B_{l}^{(1,2)})\;&l>2,\\
\;R(C_{2}^{(1,2)})\;&l=2.\end{cases}
Here, we obtain the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{m}+2\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{l}+2\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{BCC_{l}^{(2)}(2)}).

(2).
The case
R_{+}=R(C_{l}^{(1,2)})
(
l>1
)

In this case, the index
[\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R_{m},G):\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R(C_{l}^{(1,2)}),G)]
is
4
. Notice that the set

U_{-}/U_{-}(2)
is represented by
\{1,{}^{t}T\}
.

(I)

R_{-}=\begin{cases}\;R(B_{l}^{(1,1)})\;&l>2,\\
\;R(C_{2}^{(1,1)})\;&l=2.\end{cases}

Here, the coset
\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R_{m},G)/\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R(C_{l}^{(1,2)}),G)
is represented by

\{1,{}^{t}T\}\circ\{\mathrm{id},\tau_{\frac{1}{2}a}\}
.
4
4

4

Set
M=E_{F}(a\otimes\tilde{P}(C_{l}^{\vee})+b\otimes P(C_{l}^{\vee}))
. Then, since
M\cup\tau_{\frac{1}{2}a}M=E_{F}(P(D_{l}^{\vee})\otimes a+P(D_{l}^{\vee})\otimes b)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R_{m})/\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(D_{l})=

\displaystyle(M\cup\tau_{\frac{1}{2}a}M)\cdot(\chi(1)U_{-}(2)\cup\chi({}^{t}T)U_{-}(2))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi(1)(M\cup\tau_{\frac{1}{2}a}M)U_{-}(2)\cup\chi({}^{t}T)(M\cup\tau_{\frac{1}{2}a}M)U_{-}(2),

whence
\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R_{m})/\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R(C_{l}^{(1,2)}))
is represented by
\{1,{}^{t}T\}\circ\{\mathrm{id},\tau_{\frac{1}{2}a}\}
as
\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R(C_{l}^{(1,2)}))/\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(D_{l})\cong MU_{-}(2)
.

Only

and

extend to automorphisms of
R_{-}
.

(i)

For

, we obtain the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{m}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{l}+2\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{BCC_{l}^{(2)}(1)}).

(ii)

For

, we obtain the root system

(II)

R_{-}=\begin{cases}\;R(B_{l}^{(1,2)})\;&l>2,\\
\;R(C_{2}^{(1,2)})\;&l=2.\end{cases}

Here, the coset

For

, we obtain the root system

\displaystyle R=

(II)

R_{-}=\begin{cases}\;R(B_{l}^{(1,2)})\;&l>2,\\
\;R(C_{2}^{(1,2)})\;&l=2.\end{cases}

l>1

\mathrm{Aut}^{\prime}(R_{m},G)/\mathrm{Aut}^{+}(R_{+},G)
is represented by the next
4
elements:
\tau_{\delta}
with
\delta\in\{0,a,b,a+b\}

.

(i)

For
\varphi=\mathrm{id}
, we obtain the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{m}+2\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{l}+4\mathbb{Z}a+4\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(4)\ast_{0}}}).

\varphi=\tau_{a}

\tau_{b}

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(4)\ast_{0^{\prime}}}})=

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,m(n-1)\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{m}+2\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{l}+4\mathbb{Z}a+4\mathbb{Z}b).

(iv)

\varphi=\tau_{a+b}
, we have

via
\tau_{a+b}
, where

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{BC_{l}^{(4,4)\ast}})=

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,(m-1)(n-1)\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{m}+2\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(R(BC_{l})_{l}+4\mathbb{Z}a+4\mathbb{Z}b).

of type

In this case, since

\varphi=\mathrm{id}
, we get the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{m}+2\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{l}+\{\,2ma+2nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{0}}}).

(ii)

For
\varphi=\tau_{a}
, we obtain the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{m}+2\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{l}+\{\,2ma+2nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,(m-1)n\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1^{\prime}}}}).

(iii)

For
\varphi=\tau_{a+b}
, we get the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{m}+2\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(R(BC_{l})_{l}+\{\,2ma+2nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,(m-1)(n-1)\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1}}}).

(iv)

For
\varphi=\tau_{b}
, we have
R\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1}})
via
\tau_{a}\circ\chi(({}^{t}T)^{-1})
.

Remark 5.3
.

(1)

The isomorphism classes of the root systems

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle\big(R(BC_{l})_{s}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z},\;(m-p^{\prime})(n-q^{\prime})\equiv 0\,[2]\,\}\big)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\big(R(BC_{l})_{m}+2\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b\big)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\big(R(BC_{l})_{l}+\{\,2ma+2nb\,|\,1\leq i\leq l,\;m,n\in\mathbb{Z},

\displaystyle\phantom{\big(R(BC_{l})_{l}+\{\,2ma+2nb\,|\,}(m-p-p^{\prime})(n-q-q^{\prime})\equiv 0\,[2]\,\}\big)

for any
p,q,p^{\prime},q^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}

are independent of
p^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}
; we shall denote such root systems by
\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{p,q}}}
.

(2)

The name in the above list corresponds as follows:

C^{\vee}C^{(2)\ast_{0}}

C^{\vee}C^{(2)\ast_{1}}

C^{\vee}C^{(2)\ast_{1^{\prime}}}

C^{\vee}C^{(2)\ast_{0,0}}

C^{\vee}C^{(2)\ast_{1,1}}

C^{\vee}C^{(2)\ast_{1,0}}

(3)

The mERSs
(C^{\vee}C^{(2)\ast_{0,1}},\mathbb{R}a)
and
(C^{\vee}C^{(2)\ast_{1,1}},\mathbb{R}a)
are isomorphic. As a corollary, one sees that the three root systems of type
C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{p,q}}
with
(p,q)\neq(0,0)
are all isomorphic, since
C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1,0}}
and
C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{0,1}}
are isomorphic via
a\leftrightarrow b
.

5.7.
Rank
1
cases

Here, we briefly explain how one can classify the mERSs
(R,G)
of rank
1
whose quotient
R/G
are non-reduced. We shall realise such root systems in the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
F=\mathbb{R}\varepsilon\oplus\mathbb{R}a\oplus\mathbb{R}b
equipped with the symmetric bilinear form
I
that satisfies
I(\varepsilon,\varepsilon)=1
and
\mathbb{R}a\oplus\mathbb{R}b
coincides with the radical of
I
.

A reduced mERS of rank
1
is either of type
A_{1}^{(1,1)}
or
A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}
. Contrary to higher rank cases, we cannot proceed the classification
of the mERSs of rank
1
by “gluing” two root systems, since there is no middle length root in this case. Nevertheless, we shall classify these root systems with an analogous idea. First fix a realisation of
R_{s}
in
(F,I)
as follows:

R_{s}=\{\pm\varepsilon\}+L
where

L:=\begin{cases}\;\mathbb{Z}a\oplus\mathbb{Z}b\qquad&R_{s}:\,\text{of type}\,A_{1}^{(1,1)}\\
\;\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\}\qquad&R_{s}:\,\text{of type}\,A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}.\end{cases}

Remark that the lattice
\langle L\rangle
, generated by the elements of
L
by definition, is always
\operatorname{rad}(I)_{\mathbb{Z}}=\mathbb{Z}a\oplus\mathbb{Z}b
. Next, realize
R_{l}
in the form

R_{l}=\{\pm(2\varepsilon+m_{0})\}+M,

where
m_{0}\in\operatorname{rad}(I)
and
M\subset\operatorname{rad}(I)
is a subset containing
0
, assuming that
R_{l}
is a root system of type
A_{1}^{(1,1)}
or
A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}
. Set
\gamma=2\varepsilon+m_{0}
. Now, we give several properties of these data.

(1)

For
m,m^{\prime}\in M
, we have

r_{\gamma+m^{\prime}}(\gamma+m)=-\gamma+m-2m^{\prime}

which implies that
M+\langle 2M\rangle\subset M
. In particular,

\langle 2M\rangle\subset M
as
M
contains
0
and
M=-M
by setting
m^{\prime}=m
in the above formula.

(2)

Assume that
R=R_{s}\cup R_{l}
is a root system. By assumption,

r_{\varepsilon}(\gamma)=\gamma-4\varepsilon=-\gamma+2m_{0}\in-\gamma+M

which implies that
2m_{0}\in M
. Now, for any
m\in M
and
l\in L
,

r_{\gamma+m}(-\varepsilon+l)=\varepsilon+m_{0}+m+l\in\varepsilon+L,

that is,
m_{0}+M+L\subset L
. As both
M
and
L
contains
0
,
m_{0}\in L
. In particular, it follows that
M+L_{0}\subset L_{0}
as
L_{0}=\langle L\rangle
which implies
M\subset L_{0}
.

(3)

Now, for any
l\in L
and
m\in M
,

r_{\varepsilon+l}\circ r_{\varepsilon}(\gamma+m)=\gamma+m+4l,

which implies that
M+4L\subset M
, i.e.,
M+4L_{0}\subset M
. In particular, as
M
contains
0
, it follows that
M\supset 4L_{0}
.

Summarising the above arguments, we have

Lemma 5.4
.

(1)

M
is a subset of
L_{0}
satisfying

(i)

M+4L_{0}\subset M
, in particular,
4L_{0}\subset M
, and

(ii)

M+\langle 2M\rangle\subset M
.

(2)

m_{0}\in L
satisfies

(i)

2m_{0}\in M
and

(ii)

m_{0}+M+L\subset L
.

With this technical lemma, we shall analyse each case separately.

5.7.1.
\mathbf{R_{s}}

\&

of type
\mathbf{A_{1}^{(1,1)}}

In this case,
M
is a sub-lattice of
L=L_{0}
of full rank containing
4L
.

. In this case,

, the leftmost and lowest circle indicates

.

Let us now classify the possible lattices
M
, together with possible
m_{0}
’s, up to
SL(\operatorname{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I))
-action on
L
fixing
\mathbb{Z}a
. Fix a basis
(b,a)
of
\operatorname{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I)
to identify
SL(\operatorname{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I))
with
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

It is clear that
M_{2,3}=\chi({}^{t}T)(M_{2,1})
,
M_{4,4}=\chi({}^{t}T)(M_{4,1})
,
M_{4,5}=\chi(({}^{t}T)^{-1})(M_{4,1})
,
M_{4,6}=\chi(({}^{t}T)^{2})(M_{4,1})
, and
M_{8,3}=\chi({}^{t}T)(M_{8,1})
. This implies the following classification:

(1)

The case
[L:M]=1
. In this case,
M=M_{1}=L
, and
we obtain the root system

R=(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{BCC_{1}^{(1)}}).

(2)

The case
[L:M]=2
. In this case, we have

(I)

M=M_{2,1}=2\mathbb{Z}a\oplus\mathbb{Z}b
.

(i)

For
m_{0}=0
,

R=(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+2\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{BCC_{1}^{(2)}(2)}).

(ii)

For
m_{0}=a
,

R=(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+(1+2\mathbb{Z})a+\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{BC_{1}^{(1,2)}}).

(II))

M=M_{2,2}=\mathbb{Z}a\oplus 2\mathbb{Z}b
.

(i)

For
m_{0}=0
,

R=(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(1)}}).

(ii)

For
m_{0}=b
, we obtain the root system of type
\mathbf{BC_{1}^{(2,1)}}
.

(3)

The case
[L:M]=4
. In this case, we have

(I)

M=M_{4,1}=4\mathbb{Z}a\oplus\mathbb{Z}b
. In this case,

R=(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+4\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{BCC_{1}^{(4)}}).

(II)

M=M_{4,2}=\mathbb{Z}a\oplus 4\mathbb{Z}b
. In this case,

R=(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+4\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}BC_{1}^{(1)}}).

(III)

M=M_{4,3}=2\mathbb{Z}a\oplus 2\mathbb{Z}b=2L
.

(i)

For
m_{0}=0
,

R=(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+2\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(2)}(2)}).

(ii)

For
m_{0}=a
,

R=(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+(1+2\mathbb{Z})a+2\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{BC_{1}^{(2,2)\sigma}(2)}).

(iii)

For
m_{0}=b
, we obtain the root system of type

\mathbf{BC_{1}^{(2,2)}(2)}
.

(iv)

For
m_{0}=b+a
, we have

\chi({}^{t}T)(R(BC_{1}^{(2,2)}(2)))
.

(IV)

M=M_{4,7}=\mathbb{Z}(a+2b)\oplus 4\mathbb{Z}b=2\mathbb{Z}a\oplus\mathbb{Z}(2b+a)
.

(i)

For
m_{0}=0
,

R=(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+2\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}(2b+a))=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(2)\diamond}}).

(ii)

For
m_{0}=a
, we have

\tau_{b}(R(C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(2)\diamond}))
.

(4)

The case
[L:M]=8
.
In this case, we have

(I)

M=M_{8,1}=4\mathbb{Z}a\oplus 2\mathbb{Z}b
.

(i)

For
m_{0}=0
,

R=(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+4\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(4)}}).

(ii)

For
m_{0}=b
, we obtain the root system of type
\mathbf{BC_{1}^{(2,4)}}
.

(II)

M=M_{8,2}=2\mathbb{Z}a\oplus 4\mathbb{Z}b
.

(i)

For
m_{0}=0
,

R=(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)\cup(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+2\mathbb{Z}a+4\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}BC_{1}^{(2)}(2)}).

(ii)

For
m_{0}=a
,

The case
[L:M]=16
. In this case, we have

and

Remark 5.5
.

If
m_{0}\in 2L
, by the translation
\tau_{\frac{1}{2}m_{0}}

(
cf. (
53
)
)
, we see that it is isomorphic to the case
m_{0}=0
. Hence, the above choice of
m_{0}
is given up to the translation by
2L
.

5.7.2.
\mathbf{R_{s}}
of type
\mathbf{A_{1}^{(1,1)}}
and
\mathbf{R_{l}}
of type
\mathbf{A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}}

In this case, as
M
is contained in
L_{0}
, it is clear that
\langle 2M\rangle\subset 2L_{0}
. By Lemma
5.4
, it follows that the set
M
is stable under the translation of
\langle 2M\rangle+4L_{0}
and the latter is a lattice satisfying

2L_{0}\supset\langle 2M\rangle+4L_{0}\supset 4L_{0}.

Hence, the index of the sub-lattice
M^{\prime}:=\langle 2M\rangle+4L_{0}
of
2L_{0}
is either
1,2
or
4
. Thus, up to the
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})
-action on
L
fixing
\mathbb{Z}a
, it is equivalent either to

2L_{0},2\mathbb{Z}a+4\mathbb{Z}b,4\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b
or
4L_{0}
. We classify
R
in each case:

(1)

The case
M^{\prime}=2L_{0}
. We have

M=\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\}.

(I)

For
m_{0}=0
,

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})=R(\mathbf{BCC_{1}^{(1)\ast_{0}}}).

(II)

For
m_{0}=a
, we have
\chi({}^{t}T)(R(BCC_{1}^{(1)\ast_{0}}))
.

(III)

For
m_{0}=b
,

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,m(n-1)\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})=R(\mathbf{BCC_{1}^{(1)\ast_{0^{\prime}}}}).

(IV)

For
m_{0}=a+b
,

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,(m-1)(n-1)\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{BC_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}}).

(2)

The case
M^{\prime}=2\mathbb{Z}a+4\mathbb{Z}b
. In this case, we have

M=\{\,ma+2nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\}.

(I)

For
m_{0}=0
,

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\{\,ma+2nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(1)\ast_{0}}}).

(II)

For
m_{0}=a+2b
,

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\{\,ma+2nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,(m-1)(n-1)\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(1)\ast_{1}}}).

(3)

The case
M^{\prime}=4\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b
. In this case, we have

M=\{\,2ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\}.

(I)

For
m_{0}=0
,

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\{\,2ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})=R(\mathbf{BCC_{1}^{(2)\ast_{0}}}).

(II)

For
m_{0}=2a+b
,

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\{\,2ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,(m-1)(n-1)\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{BCC_{1}^{(2)\ast_{1}}}).

(4)

The case
M^{\prime}=4L_{0}
. In this case, we have

M=\{\,2ma+2nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\}

and

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\mathbb{Z}a+\mathbb{Z}b)

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\{\,2ma+2nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(2)\ast_{l}}}).

5.7.3.
\mathbf{R_{s}}
of type
\mathbf{A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}}
and
\mathbf{R_{l}}
of type
\mathbf{A_{1}^{(1,1)}}

This case is dual to the previous section, so let us just compute their duals and give the results. We write only those cases where we obtain new root systems, and keep the same numbering system as in the previous section.

(1)

(I)

We get the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+4\mathbb{Z}a+4\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}BC_{1}^{(4)\ast_{0}}}).

(III)

We obtain the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,m(n-1)\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+4\mathbb{Z}a+4\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}BC_{1}^{(4)\ast_{0^{\prime}}}}).

(IV)

For
m_{0}=a+b
, we get the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,(m-1)(n-1)\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+4\mathbb{Z}a+4\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{BC_{1}^{(4,4)\ast}}).

(2)

(I)

We obtain the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+4\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(4)\ast_{0}}}).

(II)

We get the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+4\mathbb{Z}a+2\mathbb{Z}b)=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(4)\ast_{1}}}).

(I)

\displaystyle\cup

(II)

We get the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle\cup

We obtain the root system

5.7.4.
\mathbf{R_{s}}

\&

\mathbf{R_{l}}
of type
\mathbf{A_{1}^{(1,1)\ast}}

In this case, fix

L=\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\}

as in the head of §
5.7
. By Lemma
5.4
(2) (ii), both
m_{0}
and
M
are contained in
2L_{0}
. Hence the only possibility for
M
is
2L
, that is,

M=\{\,2ma+2nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\}.

Thus, we have the next classification:

(1)

For
m_{0}=0
, we have the root system

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\{\,2ma+2nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(2)\ast_{0}}}).

(2)

For
m_{0}=2a
,

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\{\,2ma+2nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,(m-1)n\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})=R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(2)\ast_{1^{\prime}}}}).

(3)

For
m_{0}=2b
, we get
R=\tau_{a}\circ\chi({}^{t}T)((R(C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(2)\ast_{1}})))
.

(4)

For
m_{0}=2(a+b)
, we obtain

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle(\{\pm\varepsilon\}+\{\,ma+nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,mn\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle(\{\pm 2\varepsilon\}+\{\,2ma+2nb\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\text{s.t.}\,(m-1)(n-1)\equiv 0\,[2]\,\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{C^{\vee}C_{1}^{(2)\ast_{1}}}).

6.
More on new mERSs

6.1.
Isomorphic root systems

Among mERSs, there exist several pairs, say
(R_{1},G_{1})
and
(R_{2},G_{2})
who are not isomorphic as mERS but are isomorphic as ERS. In this subsection, we provide those where at least one of the two mERSs has a non-reduced affine quotient.

Theorem 6.1
.

Among the reduced
BC_{l}
-type marked elliptic root systems with non-reduced affine quotient, we have the following isomorphisms

(
as root systems
):

\displaystyle R(BC_{l}^{(1,2)})\cong R(BC_{l}^{(2,1)}),\qquad R(BC_{l}^{(4,2)})\cong R(BC_{l}^{(2,4)}),

\displaystyle R(BC_{l}^{(2,2)\sigma}(i))\cong R(BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(i))\qquad i\in\{1,2\}.

Proof.

They follow from the isomorphism
a\leftrightarrow b
.
∎

Therefore, this theorem implies that there exist
6
infinite series of non-isomorphic reduced mERSs whose full quotient is of type
BC_{l}
:

R(BC_{l}^{(1,2)})\cong R(BC_{l}^{(2,1)})
,

R(BC_{l}^{(2,2)\sigma}(1))\cong R(BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(1))
,
R(BC_{l}^{(2,2)\sigma}(2))\cong R(BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(2))
,
R(BC_{l}^{(4,2)})\cong R(BC_{l}^{(2,4)})
,
R(BC_{l}^{(1,1)\ast})
and
R(BC_{l}^{(4,4)\ast})
.

Theorem 6.2
.

Among the above
34
infinite series and
1
exceptional type of non-reduced marked elliptic root systems, we have the next isomorphisms
(
as root systems
):

(1)

Via the isomorphism
a\leftrightarrow b:

\displaystyle R(BCC_{l}^{(2)}(1))\cong R(BB_{l}^{\vee(1)}),\qquad R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(2)}(1))\cong R(BB_{l}^{\vee(4)}),

\displaystyle R(BCC_{l}^{(2)}(2))\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(1)}),\qquad R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(2)}(2))\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(4)}),

\displaystyle R(BCC_{l}^{(2)\ast_{p}})\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(1)\ast_{p}}),\quad R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(2)\ast_{p}})\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(4)\ast_{p}})\quad(p\in\{0,1\}),

\displaystyle\phantom{ABCDEFGHI}R(BB_{l}^{\vee(2)}(2))\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)}(1)).

(2)

Via the isomorphism
a\mapsto a+b,\;b\mapsto b:

\displaystyle R(BCC_{l}^{(1)\ast_{0^{\prime}}})\cong R(BCC_{l}^{(1)\ast_{0}}),\qquad R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(4)\ast_{0^{\prime}}})\cong R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(4)\ast_{0}}),

\displaystyle\phantom{ABCDEFGHI}R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1^{\prime}}})\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1}}).

(3)

Via an exotic isomorphism
\&

a\leftrightarrow b:

R(BCC_{l}^{(4)})\cong R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(1)})\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\diamond}).

As a consequence, there are only
20
infinite series and
1
exceptional type of non-isomorphic elliptic root systems.

Proof.

To see the isomorphism of elliptic root systems induced from the switching
a\leftrightarrow b
, it is convenient to look at their gluing data.

be a mERS with non-reduced affine quotient

. Denote the linear isomorphism of

(

) and
a\mapsto b,\;b\mapsto a
by
\Phi
. By definition, it follows that
\Phi(R)=\Phi(R_{-})\amalg_{\Phi\circ\varphi\circ\Phi^{-1}}\Phi(R_{+})
. Here are the tables of such data:

.

Remark 6.3
.

In the above tables, the duality
R\leftrightarrow R^{\vee}
of root systems can be seen via the symmetry with respect to the diagonal.

From the above tables, we obtain the isomorphisms for reduced root systems as follows :

\displaystyle R(BC_{l}^{(1,2})\cong R(BC_{l}^{(2,1)}),\qquad R(BC_{l}^{(4,2)})\cong R(BC_{l}^{(2,4)}),

\displaystyle R(BC_{l}^{(2,2)\sigma}(1))\cong R(BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(1)),\qquad R(BC_{l}^{(2,2)\sigma}(2))\cong R(BC_{l}^{(2,2)}(2)).

The quotient root systems of the left hand side give
BCC_{l},C^{\vee}BC_{l},BB_{l}^{\vee}
and
C^{\vee}C_{l}
respectively, whereas those of the right hand side are always
BC_{l}^{(2)}
, which is a reduced affine root system.
Similarly, for non-reduced root systems, we obtain

\displaystyle R(BCC_{l}^{(2)}(1))\cong R(BB_{l}^{\vee\,(1)}),\qquad R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(2)}(1))\cong R(BB_{l}^{\vee(4)}),

\displaystyle R(BCC_{l}^{(2)}(2))\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(1)}),\qquad R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(2)}(2))\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(4)}),

\displaystyle R(BCC_{l}^{(2)\ast_{p}})\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(1)\ast_{p}}),\qquad R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(2)\ast_{p}})\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(4)\ast_{p}}),

\displaystyle R(BCC_{l}^{(4)})\cong R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(1)}),\qquad R(BB_{l}^{\vee\,(2)}(2))\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)}(1)).

Remark 6.4
.

Strictly speaking, the above argument using the construction of a root system via gluing procedure cannot be applied to the rank
1
case, but we can show that any of the above isomorphisms are valid for the rank
1
root systems by case by case checking.

The next isomorphisms explain the symbol
\ast_{i^{\prime}}
(
i=0,1
):

\displaystyle R(BCC_{l}^{(1)\ast_{0^{\prime}}})\cong R(BCC_{l}^{(1)\ast_{0}}),\qquad R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(4)\ast_{0^{\prime}}})\cong R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(4)\ast_{0}}),

\displaystyle R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1^{\prime}}})\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1}}).

Indeed, these isomorphisms are essentially given by
a\mapsto a+b,\;b\mapsto b
.
Let us show that the root systems
R(BCC_{l}^{(2)\ast_{0}})
and
R(BCC_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1}})
are not isomorphic. If they were, there would exist an isometry
\Phi
of
(F,I)
, to which they belong, such that
\Phi(R(BCC_{l}^{(2)\ast_{0}}))=R(BCC_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1}}))
. The restriction of the linear map
\Phi
to their subsets of short roots and long roots impose an incompatible condition which shows that such a linear map cannot exist. Likewise, one can show that the root systems
R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(2)\ast_{0}})
and
R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(2)\ast_{1}})
are not isomorphic. Details are left to the reader.

Finally, there is one exotic case:

\displaystyle R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\diamond})=

\displaystyle\left(\{\pm\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}\,|\,1\leq i<j\leq l\,\}+\mathbb{Z}(a+2b)+2\mathbb{Z}b\right)

\displaystyle\cup\left(\{\pm\varepsilon_{i}\,|\,1\leq i\leq l\,\}+\mathbb{Z}(a+2b)+\mathbb{Z}b\right)

\displaystyle\cup\left(\{\pm 2\varepsilon_{i}\,|\,1\leq i\leq l\,\}+\mathbb{Z}(a+2b)+4\mathbb{Z}b\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(1)})\cong R(BCC_{l}^{(4)}),

thus, we have the next isomorphisms (as root systems):

\displaystyle R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\diamond})\cong R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(1)})\cong R(BCC_{l}^{(4)}).

∎

Let us now describe the dual root system
(\sharp)
of

\displaystyle R((C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\diamond})^{\vee}):=R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\diamond})^{\vee}=
