and
b^{\prime}=b
. Note that
R((C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\diamond})^{\vee})/\mathbb{R}b^{\prime}\cong R(C^{\vee}BC_{l})
.
Since we have

\displaystyle\{\,ma^{\prime}+nb^{\prime}\,|\,m-n\equiv 0\,[2]\,\}=

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{Z}a^{\prime}+\mathbb{Z}(b^{\prime}+a^{\prime}),

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{Z}a^{\prime}+2\mathbb{Z}b^{\prime}=

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{Z}a^{\prime}+2\mathbb{Z}(b^{\prime}+a^{\prime}),

and

\displaystyle 4\mathbb{Z}a^{\prime}+2\mathbb{Z}b^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\{\,2(2m-n)a^{\prime}+2n(b^{\prime}+a^{\prime})\,|\,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\,m^{\prime}(2a^{\prime})+2n^{\prime}(b^{\prime}+a^{\prime})\,|\,m^{\prime}-n^{\prime}\equiv 0\,[2]\,\},

it follows that the marked root system
C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(2)\diamond}
is self-dual.

Remark 6.6
.

Given an elliptic root system
R
, one may ask what can be the possible affine quotients.
It can be shown that

(1)

if
R
is isomorphic to
R(BCC_{l}^{(4)})\cong R(C^{\vee}BC_{l}^{(1)})\cong R(C^{\vee}C_{l}^{((2)\diamond})
, there are
3
non-isomorphic affine quotients,

(2)

otherwise there are at most
2
affine quotients.

These facts can be shown using the automorphism groups of
R
, which will be treated in our forthcoming paper.

Remark that the table also reflects nicely the duality
R\leftrightarrow R^{\vee}
, as
R^{\vee}/G_{a}
and
R^{\vee}/G_{b}
are isomorphic to
(R/G_{a})^{\vee}
and
(R/G_{b})^{\vee}
, respectively. For the reader’s convenience, here is the duality of non-reduced affine root systems (cf. §
B.2
):

R

Type of

\displaystyle B_{2}^{(1,t)}=C_{2}^{(1,t)}\quad\text{and}\quad C_{2}^{(2,t)}=B_{2}^{(2,t)}\qquad(t=1,2),

for simplicity.

We close this chapter summarizing what is achieved so far and what is still necessary to work out to complete the classification of mERSs with non-reduced
affine quotient.
At this stage, one can not tell whether two given non-reduced mERSs in the above theorems are non-isomorphic.
In Section
8
, we discuss this question.
By Proposition
8.2
, Theorem
8.12
and Corollary
8.13
,
it turns out that the above theorem gives a complete list of isomorphism classes of non-reduced mERSs (Theorem
8.15
).

Part III
Classification

Let
(R,G)
be a mERS with the non-reduced affine quotient
R/G
.
In
\lx@sectionsign

4.3
, we already showed that
(R,G)
is isomorphic to
one of the 6 infinite series
if
R
is reduced
(Theorem
4.1
), and
is isomorphic to one of the 34 infinite series
or 1 exceptional type if
R
is
non-reduced (Theorem

4.2
). However, we have not discussed
the following fundamental question:

Are these
mERSs really non-isomorphic to each other?

In this section, we give an affirmative
answer to
this question, by using the
“elliptic diagram”
\Gamma(R,G)
of a mERS
(R,G)
with the non-reduced
affine quotient. The elliptic diagram
\Gamma(R,G)
of
(R,G)
is a
colored graph consisting of finite number of nodes, and edges connecting them, which
depends only on an isomorphism class of mERSs. One has that

•

if two mERSs
(R_{1},G_{1})
and
(R_{2},G_{2})
are isomorphic, their elliptic diagrams

\Gamma(R_{1},G_{1})
and
\Gamma(R_{2},G_{2})
are isomorphic to each other
(Proposition
8.2
), and

•

if
(R_{1},G_{1})
and
(R_{2},G_{2})
are two different mERSs in the sense of
Theorem
4.1
and Theorem

4.2
), then
\Gamma(R_{1},G_{1})

is not isomorphic to
\Gamma(R_{2},G_{2})
(see Theorem
8.12

and
\lx@sectionsign

8.5
(i)).

That is, the results in
\lx@sectionsign

4.3
give the complete list of the
isomorphism classes of mERSs with non-reduced affine quotients.

Let us briefly summarize the results of this section.
In
\lx@sectionsign

7.1
, we give an answer for
Question 1 in
\lx@sectionsign

2.1
, that is,
we describe some relationships between the six reduced affine quotient root
systems:
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
,
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
,
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
,
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
,

(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
, appeared in
\lx@sectionsign

2.1
.
§
7.2
to
7.7
are devoted to
preparations for defining elliptic diagrams. More precisely, we introduce the technical
tools: the
prime map
(see
\lx@sectionsign

7.2
),

paired reduced simple systems
(see
\lx@sectionsign

7.2
),

non-reduced counting numbers
(see
\lx@sectionsign

7.5
),
and study their
basic properties. The goals in these preparations are Proposition

7.20
in
\lx@sectionsign

7.6
and
Proposition
7.23
in
\lx@sectionsign

7.7
.
The former expresses the root system
R
in terms of its affine quotient
R/G
and
the countings. This is a “non-reduced analogue” of
the formula (
12
) for mERSs with reduced affine quotients.
The later says that a paired simple system is uniquely determined (up to
isomorphism) for a given
(R,G)
. This result plays one of the central roles for classifying
isomorphism classes of mERSs with non-reduced affine quotients in Section

8
.

In
\lx@sectionsign

8.1
, we define the elliptic diagram
\Gamma(R,G)

for a mERS
(R,G)
with the non-reduced affine quotient (Definition

8.2
), and show that this diagram depends only on the
isomorphism classes of mERSs (Proposition
8.2
).
Section
8
is devoted to proving Theorem

8.12
which we call the classification theorem of mERSs with
non-reduced affine quotient. In the proof of this theorem, we need case-by-case detailed
analysis. We note that this theorem does not give us a complete list of such mERSs.
Indeed, it only lists all possibilities, but does not state
whether a root
system with such a diagram really exists. In other words, we need to solve the
“existence problem” of
a mERS with a given elliptic diagram. However, comparing
the results of Chapter 2, we show that a mERS really exists for every possible elliptic
diagrams in Theorem
8.12
. Thus, we have a complete list of
isomorphism classes of mERS with non-reduced affine quotients (see
\lx@sectionsign

8.5
, in detail).
In Section
9
, we give detailed description of
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and

R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.

7.
Reduced subsystems of affine quotients

We define the elliptic diagram
\Gamma(R,G)
for a mERS

(R,G)
with non-reduced affine quotient and show that the isomorphism classes of
such mERSs are classified by these diagrams. These are generalizations of
the results in
[
Sai85
]
for mERSs with reduced quotients.
In this section, we simplify the symbols as far as possible, e.g., omitting
(R,G)
in the notations of the elliptic diagrams, the subspace
F
of
I_{F}
, etc.

7.1.
On reduced affine subquotients

First of all, we give an answer to
Question 1 in
\lx@sectionsign

2.1
.
Here and after, we assume that
(R,G)
is an arbitrary mERS, unless
otherwise specified. Recall the canonical projection
\pi_{G}:F\to F/G
.
For
\alpha\in R
, we denote

\overline{\alpha}:=\pi_{G}(\alpha)
for simplicity.

Let
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
be a reduced pair of
R
.
For a reduced
R
, we regard
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=R=R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.
Similarly, for the quotient affine root system
R/G
, let

((R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
be its reduced pair.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\alpha\in R
.

(1)
If
\overline{\alpha}\in(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
,
\alpha
belongs to
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.

(2)
If
\overline{\alpha}\in(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
,
\alpha
belongs to
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.

Proof.

Let us prove statement (1). Assume
\alpha\not\in R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.
By definition, it means

\alpha/2\in R
. Therefore, we have

\overline{\alpha/2}=\overline{\alpha}/2\in R/G
, i.e.,

\overline{\alpha}
does not belongs to
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.
Statement (2) is obtained by a similar method.
∎

Let us recall the setting of Question 1. Assume that
R/G
is non-reduced.
We note that both of
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},G)
and
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}},G)
are reduced mERSs. Since the
corresponding affine quotients
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G
and
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G
are not necessarily
reduced, four reduced affine root systems
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
,
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
,

(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
,
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}

may be distinct. In addition, there are two affine
root systems
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
. Question 1 asks
the relationships between these
six root systems. The following lemma gives a (partial) answer for this question.

Lemma 7.2
.

(1)

(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.

(2)

(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}=(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.

Proof.

Since statement (2) can be proved in a similar way as statement (1), we only prove (1).

First, we prove the inclusion

(54)

(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\subset(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}.

By Lemma
7.1
(1), we have

(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\subset R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G.

Let
\overline{\alpha}
be an element of
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
, i.e.,
\overline{\alpha}/2\not\in R/G
.
It follows from the inclusion
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G\subset R/G
that
\overline{\alpha}/2\not\in R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G
,
i.e.,
\overline{\alpha}\in(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
, which implies (
54
).

Second,
assume that the inclusion (
54
) is strict, i.e., there exists
\overline{\alpha}\in(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\setminus(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.
By the condition
\overline{\alpha}\not\in(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
, we have

\overline{\alpha}/2\in R/G
.
In other words, there exists
\beta\in R
such that

\overline{\beta}=\overline{\alpha}/2
.
In particular,
\beta
is a short root.
On the other hand, by the assumption
\overline{\alpha}\in(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
,
one has
2\overline{\beta}\in(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
. Therefore, we have

\overline{\beta}\not\in R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G
. Since
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
contains the set
R_{s}
of short roots
of
R
, the condition
\overline{\beta}\not\in R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G

implies that
\beta
is not
a short root. This is a contradiction. Thus, we have statement (1).
∎

Remark 7.3
.

In the following, we use only
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}=(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
, and
neither
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
nor
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
will be used. So, we omit to give explicit descriptions
of them.

7.2.
Paired simple system

Let
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
be the reduced pair of a mERS
(R,G)
and

\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
be a simple system of
the reduced mERS
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},G)
in the sense of Definition
2.1
. It is immediate
to see that
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
also gives a simple system of
(R,G)
.
However, because of
non-reducedness, the information on
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
is
not
sufficient for describing
the structure of the set
R
of all roots, that is, Lemma

2.7
(4) holds in general,
which plays an important role
in the detailed study for a mERS
(R,G)
with reduced
R/G
.

To fill the missing information,
we introduce the notion of a “paired simple system”
(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
for
the reduced pair
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
of a mERS
(R,G)
(see Definition

7.2
below). Here,
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}

is a linearly independent
subset of
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
, which satisfies certain properties.
With this additional information, we generalize the description
(
12
) of
R
for an arbitrary case in Proposition

7.20
below.

Let
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
be a simple system of the reduced mERS
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},G)
.
As we mentioned above, it gives a simple system of
(R,G)
also.
Furthermore, its image

\pi_{G}(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})=\pi_{G}(\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}})
is a simple system of

(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
(see Lemma
7.2
(1)).
Motivated by Lemma
2.2
, we want to
take a simple system
\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}
of
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}},G)
such that

i)

\pi_{G}(\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}})
is a simple system of
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
, and

ii)

\pi_{G}(\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}})\setminus\pi_{G}(\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}})
contains only 1 root for

R/G
being of type
BCC_{l}
,
C^{\vee}BC_{l}
and
BB^{\vee}_{l}
, and 2 roots
of type
C^{\vee}C_{l}
.

But it is
impossible
.
Indeed, let
\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}
be a simple system of
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}},G)
.
Then, its image
\pi_{G}(\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}})
is a simple system of the affine root system

(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
. On the other hand, by Lemma
7.2
(2),
we already know
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}=(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
. Therefore, the set

\pi_{G}(\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}})
is not necessary included in
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
, and there is
no

simple system
\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}
of
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}},G)
which satisfies condition i) in general.

Example 7.1
.

Let
(R,G)
be a mERS of type
C^{\vee}C_{l}^{(4)}
with
l\geq 2

(see
\lx@sectionsign

5.6
(2) (II) or
\lx@sectionsign

10.5.12
).
As in the list in
\lx@sectionsign

6.3
(4),
the reduced mERS
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}},G)
is of type

BC_{l}^{(2,2)\sigma}(2)
. Furthermore, as in the list in
\lx@sectionsign

6.2
,

R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G
is a non-reduced affine root system of type
C^{\vee}C_{l}
.
In other words,
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
is of type
B_{l}^{(2)}
and

(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}=(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
is of type
C_{l}^{(1)}
, respectively. Especially,

(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
(resp.
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
)
consists of short and middle (resp. middle and long) roots. Therefore, any simple
system
\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}
of
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}},G)
consists of short and middle roots also, and
the image
\pi_{G}(\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}})
is not a subset of
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.

Instead, we take a linearly independent subset
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
of
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
such that both
the conditions i) and ii) are satisfied. However, if we take it unconditionally, we will
lose control. Therefore, we have to select a reasonable
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}

in accordance with the first
choice of
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\Pi_{R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
. For this purpose, we introduce the “prime map”

(\ \cdot\ )^{pr}:R\to R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.

For each
\alpha\in R
, we introduce a root

\alpha^{\prime}\in R
by
the next rules:

i)

If
2\overline{\alpha}\not\in R/G
, set

\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha.

It is obvious that
\alpha^{\prime}\in R
and
2\alpha^{\prime}\not\in R
.

ii)

Otherwise, one can take a
representative
\beta\in\pi_{G}^{-1}(2\overline{\alpha})\cap R
of

2\overline{\alpha}
. Since

\overline{\beta}=2\overline{\alpha}
, we have

\beta-2\alpha\in Q(R)\cap G=\mathbb{Z}a
.
Hence,

\pi_{G}^{-1}(2\overline{\alpha})\cap R\subset 2\alpha+\mathbb{Z}a.

Let
\alpha^{\prime}\in\pi_{G}^{-1}(2\overline{\alpha})\cap R
be a unique element satisfying

\alpha^{\prime}\in 2\alpha+\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}a\quad\text{and}\quad(\alpha^{\prime})^{*}=\alpha^{\prime}+k(\alpha^{\prime})a\in 2\alpha+\mathbb{Z}_{>0}a.

Since
\alpha^{\prime}
is a long root, we have
2\alpha^{\prime}\not\in R
,
as in the previous case.

This is the definition of
\alpha^{\prime}\in R
for
\alpha\in R
. We note that the
correspondence
\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{\prime}
defines a map

(\,\cdot\,)^{pr}:R\to R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
which we call the

prime map

for
R
.

Remark 7.4
.

The definition of the map
(\,\cdot\,)^{pr}:R\to R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
depends on the choice of a
generator of the lattice
Q(R)\cap G
of rank 1.

Define a subgroup
\mathrm{Aut}(R,G)
of
\mathrm{Aut}(R)
by

\mathrm{Aut}(R,G)=\{\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(R)\,|\,\varphi|_{G}=\mathrm{id}_{G}\}.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(R,G)
. We have

(55)

(\varphi(\alpha))^{\prime}=\varphi(\alpha^{\prime})\quad\text{for every $\alpha\in R$}.

That is, every automorphism
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(R,G)
commutes with
the map
(\,\cdot\,)^{pr}:R\to R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.

Proof.

Note that, since
\varphi(a)=a
, the induced automorphism

\overline{\varphi}\in\mathrm{Aut}(R/G)
is well-defined.
Since the statement is trivial if
2\overline{\alpha}\not\in R/G
, we may
assume
2\overline{\alpha}\in R/G
.
In this case, we have
2\overline{\varphi(\alpha)}\in R/G
.
By definition, we have

\pi_{G}^{-1}(2\overline{\varphi(\alpha)})\cap R\subset 2\varphi(\alpha)+\mathbb{Z}a
.
Hence,
(\varphi(\alpha))^{\prime}\in R
is determined uniquely by the following conditions:

\displaystyle(\varphi(\alpha))^{\prime}\in

\displaystyle\;2\varphi(\alpha)+\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}a,

\displaystyle((\varphi(\alpha))^{\prime})^{*}=(\varphi(\alpha))^{\prime}+k((\varphi(\alpha))^{\prime})a\in

\displaystyle\;2\varphi(\alpha)+\mathbb{Z}_{>0}a.

Denote
\alpha^{\prime}=2\alpha+pa
with
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
. Recall that

p+k(\alpha^{\prime})>0
. We have

\displaystyle\varphi(\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\varphi(2\alpha+pa)\in 2\varphi(\alpha)+\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}a.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle(\varphi(\alpha^{\prime}))^{*}

\displaystyle=\varphi(\alpha^{\prime})+k(\varphi(\alpha^{\prime}))a

\displaystyle=\varphi(\alpha^{\prime})+k(\alpha^{\prime})a\quad(\text{by Lemma \ref{lemma:countings1} (3)})

\displaystyle=2\varphi(\alpha)+(p+k(\alpha^{\prime}))a

\displaystyle\in 2\varphi(\alpha)+\mathbb{Z}_{>0}a\quad(\because\ p+k(\alpha^{\prime})>0).

Thus, the element
\varphi(\alpha^{\prime})
satisfies the defining
condition of
(\varphi(\alpha))^{\prime}
, and we have (
55
).
∎

Let us introduce a “paired simple system”
(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
for a mERS
(R,G)

as follows.

First, take a simple system

\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\{\alpha_{i}\}_{0\leq i\leq l}

of the reduced
mERS
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},G)
in the sense of Definition
2.1
.
Then, its image
(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})_{G}:=\pi_{G}(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})=\{\overline{\alpha_{i}}\}_{0\leq i\leq l}

is a simple system of the affine root system
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.
For later use, in addition, we assume that

(56)

if
\big(\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})\cap R\big)\setminus\frac{1}{2}R_{l}\neq\emptyset

for
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
,
\alpha
belongs to

\big(\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})\cap R\big)\setminus\frac{1}{2}R_{l}
.

Second, define a linearly independent subset
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\subset R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
by

\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}=(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})^{pr}=\{\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\}_{0\leq i\leq l},

where
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}
is the image of
\alpha_{i}\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
under the prime map

(\,\cdot\,)^{pr}:R\to R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.
By definition, the image
\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{i}}
of
\alpha_{i}
(
0\leq i\leq l
) under
the projection
\pi_{G}:F\rightarrow F/G
satisfies

(57)

\overline{\alpha^{\prime}_{i}}=\begin{cases}\overline{\alpha_{i}}&\text{if }2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\not\in R/G,\\
2\overline{\alpha_{i}}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Therefore, as we already claimed in the proof of Lemma
2.2
,

(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})_{G}:=\pi_{G}(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
is a simple system of
(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.

Definition 7.2
.

Such a pair
(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
is called a

paired simple system
of

(R,G)
.

By the above discussion, we may assume that the pair
((\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})_{G},(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})_{G})

is a paired simple system of
R/G
satisfying Lemma
2.2
. For
this reason, we will write
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}
,

\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{G}
instead of

(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})_{G},(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})_{G}
, respectively, to simplify the notations.

7.3.
Descriptions of
R
attached to
(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})

In this subsection, we introduce two descriptions of the set
R

of roots attached to a paired simple system
(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})

(see (
63
) in Corollary
7.10
and
(
64
) in Corollary
7.11
, below).
These are “non-reduced analogues” of the description (
12
)
which is one of the key formulas for analyzing detailed structures
of mERSs with reduced affine quotients. Similar to the previous cases, they play
central roles for studying mERSs with non-reduced affine quotients.

Remark 7.6
.

In the rest of the article, we mainly use the description (
64
).
The reason why we choose (
64
) is explained in the next subsection.

Define two subgroups
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]
and
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}]
of
W(R)
by

W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]=\langle r_{\alpha}\,|\,\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}]=\langle r_{\alpha^{\prime}}\,|\,\alpha^{\prime}\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\rangle.

Since every element in the Weyl group

W(R)
stabilizes the marking
G
, the canonical projection
\pi_{G}:F\to F/G

induces a surjective group homomorphism

(\pi_{G})_{*}:W(R)\to W(R/G).

For each
\alpha\in R
, one has

(\pi_{G})_{*}(r_{\alpha})=\overline{r}_{\overline{\alpha}}
, where

\overline{r}_{\overline{\alpha}}
is the reflection
in
W(R/G)
attached to the root
\overline{\alpha}\in R/G
.
Let
\overline{W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}]}
(
resp
.
\overline{W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{G}]}
) be the group
generated by
\overline{r}_{\overline{\alpha}}\,(\overline{\alpha}\in\Pi_{G}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})

(
resp
.
\overline{r}_{\overline{\alpha}}\,(\overline{\alpha}\in\Pi_{G}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}))
.

Lemma 7.7
.

(1)

\overline{W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}]}=W(R/G)=\overline{W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{G}]}
.

(2)

\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})=(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and

\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})=(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.

Proof.

(1) Since
\Pi_{G}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
is a simple system of the reduced affine root system of

(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
, we have

W((R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})=\overline{W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}]}
. In addition,
since
W(R/G)=W((R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})
,
we have the first equality. The second equality
follows form the definition of
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{G}
.

(2) As the second equality is obtained by a similar way to the first one,
we only give a proof of the first one.
Since
\overline{W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}]}=W((R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})
, we have

\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})=\overline{W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}]}.\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}=W((R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}).\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}=(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}.

as desired.
∎

Define vector subspaces
L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
and

L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}
of
F
by

(58)

L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{R}\alpha_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{R}\alpha^{\prime}_{i},

respectively. Then, we have two decompositions

(59)

F=L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\bigoplus G=L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}\bigoplus G

of the vector space
F
, and they induce isomorphisms of vector spaces

(60)

\pi_{G}|_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}}:L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\xrightarrow{\sim}F/G\quad\text{and}\quad\pi_{G}|_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}}:L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}\xrightarrow{\sim}F/G,

respectively.

It is easy to see that the subset
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}

(
resp
.
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
)
is a root system belonging to

(L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}},I_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}})
(
resp
.
(L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}},I_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}})
).
Furthermore,
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\{\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{l}\}
(
resp
.

\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}=\{\alpha_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,\alpha_{l}^{\prime}\}
) is a simple system of
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}

(
resp
.
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
).
Especially, we have

(61)

Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{Z}\alpha^{\prime}_{i},

where
Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})
(
resp
.
Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
) is the root lattice of

W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
(
resp
.
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
).
Furthermore, the isomorphisms in (
60
) induce bijections

(62)

W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\xrightarrow{\sim}(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\quad\text{and}\quad W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\xrightarrow{\sim}(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}.

Proposition 7.8
.

(1)
The sets
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
and
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}
are reduced affine root
systems belonging to
(L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}},I_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}})
and
(L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}},I_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}})
, respectively.

(2)
One has
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}=W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and

R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}=W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.

Proof.

(1) Since the statements for
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
are obtained in a similar way to those for

R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
, we only prove the later case. First, we prove
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}

is a root system belonging to
(L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}},I_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}})
.
Recall a result due to K. Saito
[
Sai85
]
.
Let
R
be a root system belonging to
(F,I)
in the sense of Definition

1.1
,
G
be a subspace of
\operatorname{rad}{I}
defined over
\mathbb{Z}
and

\pi_{G}:F\longrightarrow F/G
be the canonical projection. Denote
W(R)

(resp.
W(R/G)
) the Weyl group of
R
(resp. the quotient root system
R/G
).

Lemma 7.9

(
[
Sai85
]
(1.15) Note)
.

Let
L
be a subspace of
F
such that

(a)

F=L\bigoplus G
,

(b)

(\pi_{G})_{*}|_{W(R\cap L)}:W(R\cap L)\longrightarrow W(R/G)
is surjective,

where
(\pi_{G})_{*}:W(R)\longrightarrow W(R/G)
is the group homomorphism induced
form
\pi_{G}:F\longrightarrow F/G
, and
W(R\cap L)
is the subgroup of
W(R)

generated by reflections
r_{\alpha}\ (\alpha\in R\cap L)
. Then, we have

(i)

R\cap L
is a root system belonging to
(L,I|_{L})
,

(ii)

the linear isomorphism
\pi_{G}|_{L}:L\xrightarrow{\sim}F/G
induces an injective
map
R\cap L\longrightarrow R/G
which induces an isomorphism
W(R\cap L)\xrightarrow{\sim}W(R/G)
.

Apply this lemma for the case
R=R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and
L=L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
.
Condition (a) is satisfied by (
59
). Since
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\subset R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
and Lemma
7.7
(1),
condition (b) is also satisfied.
Therefore,
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}

is a root system belonging to
(L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}},I_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}})
by the above lemma.

Second, let us prove
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
is reduced. However, this is obvious by
the reducibility of
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.

(2) Let us prove the first equality.
If
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G
is reduced, there is nothing to prove.
Otherwise, it is of type
BCC_{l}
,
C^{\vee}BC_{l}
,

BB^{\vee}_{l}
or
C^{\vee}C_{l}
. The reduced root system
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}

is embedded into
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G
by the isomorphism

\pi_{G}|_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}}:L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\xrightarrow{\sim}F/G
.
Furthermore, since
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
is a subset of
R\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
, the
image
\pi_{G}(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}})
is a reduced subsystem of
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G
which
contains
\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]\cdot\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})=(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}

by Lemma
7.7
(2).
That is, we have a sequence of root systems

(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\subset\pi_{G}(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}})\subset R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G

such that both
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and
\pi_{G}(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}})
are reduced.

Assume
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\subsetneq\pi_{G}(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}})
. Since both

(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and
\pi_{G}(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}})
are reduced, the root lattice

Q((R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})
is a proper sublattice of
Q(\pi_{G}(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}))
.
On the other hand, by Lemma
7.1
(1), we have

Q((R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})=Q(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)
.
This is a contradiction. Thus

\pi_{G}(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}})=(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}).

Since
\pi_{G}|_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}}:L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\xrightarrow{\sim}F/G
is an isomorphism,
we have
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}=W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
as desired.

For the second equality, we have

(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\subset\pi_{G}(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}})\subset R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G

by a similar way to the first case. In addition, we note that both

(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}=\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
and

\pi_{G}(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}})
are reduced.
Hence, the statement is reduced to the following claim.

Claim
.
There is no reduced subsystem
R_{1}
such that

(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\subsetneq R_{1}\subset R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G
.

Proof.

This claim follows from case-by-case detailed analysis. In the following, we give
a proof for the case when
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G
is of type
C^{\vee}C_{l}
, for example. Since
the other cases are obtained by a similar way, we leave the detailed proof to
the reader.

Recall an explicit description of the set
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G
of roots of type
C^{\vee}C_{l}

(see
\lx@sectionsign

B.2.4
in Appendix
B
):

R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\pm\varepsilon_{i}+nb\ (1\leq i\leq l,\,n\in\mathbb{Z}),\\
\pm\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}+2nb\ (1\leq i<j\leq l,\,n\in\mathbb{Z}),\\
\pm 2\varepsilon_{i}+2nb\ (1\leq i\leq l,\,n\in\mathbb{Z})\end{array}\right\}.

Here, we normalize a symmetric bilinear form
I_{F/G}
on
F/G
by

I_{F/G}(\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j})=\delta_{i,j}
,
and
b
is a fixed generator of the lattice
\operatorname{rad}(I_{F/G})\cap Q(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)
of rank
1
.
Note that in this case
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
consists of all middle and long roots of
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G
.

Assume there exists a reduced subsystem
R_{1}
of
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G
such that

(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\\
\subsetneq R_{1}
. Then
R_{1}
must contain a short root
\alpha
. Since
R_{1}

is a root system,
-\alpha
is an element of
R_{1}
. So, we may assume

\alpha=\varepsilon_{i}+mb
for some
1\leq i\leq l
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
is a
subsystem of
R_{1}
, the root system
R_{1}
contains all middle and long roots of

R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G
. Therefore,
2\alpha=2\varepsilon_{i}+2mb
is an element of
R_{1}
.
This contradicts the reducedness of
R_{1}
. Thus, there is no such an
R_{1}
.
∎

By the above claim,
\pi_{G}(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}})
should be equal to

\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}]\cdot\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})=(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
. By the same reason as in the first case, we have

R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}=W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
, which completes the proof of statement (2).
∎

Corollary 7.10
.

We have the following description of the set
R
of roots
:

(63)

R=\Big(\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)\Big)\bigcup\Big(\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)\Big).

Proof.

By Lemma
7.7
(2) and Proposition

7.8
(2), it follows that

\displaystyle\pi_{G}(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}})\cup\pi_{G}(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})\cup\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cup(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}=R/G.

Hence, the statement is an immediate consequence of Lemma

2.7
(1).
∎

On the other hand, by replacing
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}=R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}
with
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
,
we have another description of
R
.

Corollary 7.11
.

(1)
The canonical projection
\pi_{G}:F\to F/G
induces a bijection

\pi_{G}|_{W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}:W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\xrightarrow{\sim}(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.

(2)
There is another description of the set
R
of roots
:

(64)

R=\Big(\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)\Big)\bigcup\Big(\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)\Big).

Proof.

(1) It is easy to see that the map
\pi_{G}|_{W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}
is surjective. Indeed, by Lemma

7.7
(1), (2), we have

\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})=\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})\\
=(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
. Therefore, it is enough to show that the map is injective.

Recall the definition
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}:=(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})^{pr}
. More precisely, for
0\leq i\leq l
,

\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
is defined by

\alpha_{i}^{\prime}:=\begin{cases}\alpha_{i}&\text{if $2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\not\in R/G$},\\
2\alpha_{i}+p_{i}a\text{ for some $p_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}$}&\text{if $2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\in R/G$}.\end{cases}

Recall also that the set
\Pi_{G}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}:=\{\overline{\alpha_{i}}\}_{0\leq i\leq l}

(
resp
.

\Pi_{G}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}:=\big\{\overline{\alpha_{i}^{\prime}}\big\}_{0\leq i\leq l}
)
is a simple
system of the affine root system
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
(
resp
.
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}=(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
).

Let
\gamma_{1}=w_{1}(\alpha_{j_{1}}^{\prime}),\gamma_{2}=w_{2}(\alpha_{j_{2}}^{\prime})

be elements in
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
(
w_{1},w_{2}\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]
)
such that

(65)

\overline{\gamma_{1}}=\overline{\gamma_{2}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\overline{w_{1}}(\overline{\alpha_{j_{1}}^{\prime}})=\overline{w_{2}}(\overline{\alpha_{j_{2}}^{\prime}}).

Here,
\overline{w_{q}}\in\overline{W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]}=W(R/G)
for each
q=1,2
.
Our goal is to show
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
.

Note that (
65
) is equivalent to the condition that

\overline{\alpha_{i_{1}}^{\prime}}
and
\overline{\alpha_{i_{2}}^{\prime}}
are included in the same

W(R/G)
-orbit. Let us partition
the set
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
into the following two pieces:

\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}=(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})\amalg(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\setminus\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}).

Taking the image under
\pi_{G}
, we have

\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{G}=(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}\cap\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{G})\amalg(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{G}\setminus\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}).

Assume
\overline{\alpha_{j_{1}}^{\prime}}\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{G}\setminus\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}
. Then,
in the theory of non-reduced affine root systems, it is known that the
W(R/G)
-orbit
passing through
\overline{\alpha_{j_{1}}^{\prime}}
does not intersect with other

W(R/G)
-orbits. Therefore, it is enough to consider the following two cases:

(a)

\overline{\alpha_{j_{1}}^{\prime}}=\overline{\alpha_{j_{2}}^{\prime}}\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{G}\setminus\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}
;

(b)

both
\overline{\alpha_{j_{1}}^{\prime}}
and
\overline{\alpha_{j_{2}}^{\prime}}
belong to

\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}\cap\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{G}
.

(a) The condition
\overline{\alpha_{j_{1}}^{\prime}}=\overline{\alpha_{j_{2}}^{\prime}}
implies

\alpha_{j_{1}}^{\prime}=\alpha_{j_{2}}^{\prime}
.
Therefore,we have
j:=j_{1}=j_{2}
, and
denote the element
\alpha_{j_{1}}^{\prime}=\alpha_{j_{2}}^{\prime}
by
\alpha_{j}^{\prime}=2\alpha_{j}+p_{j}a
.
Hence, we have

(66)

\gamma_{q}=2w_{q}(\alpha_{j})+p_{j}a\quad\text{ for }q=1,2.

Since
2w_{q}(\alpha_{j})\in L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
and

\pi_{G}|_{L_{\Pi}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}:L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\xrightarrow{\sim}F/G
is an isomorphism, condition
(
65
) implies
2w_{1}(\alpha_{j})=2w_{2}(\alpha_{j})
. Thus, we
have

\gamma_{1}=2w_{1}(\alpha_{j})+p_{j}a=2w_{2}(\alpha_{j})+p_{j}a=\gamma_{2}.

(b) In this case,
\alpha_{j_{q}}^{\prime}=\alpha_{j_{q}}
and

\gamma_{p}=w_{p}(\alpha_{j_{q}})\in L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
for
q=1,2
. Furthermore,
condition (
65
) is equivalent to

\overline{w_{1}}(\overline{\alpha_{j_{1}}})=\overline{w_{2}}(\overline{\alpha_{j_{2}}})
.
Hence, by using the isomorphism
\pi_{G}|_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}}:L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\xrightarrow{\sim}F/G
,
the condition

\overline{w_{1}}(\overline{\alpha_{j_{1}}})=\overline{w_{2}}(\overline{\alpha_{j_{2}}})

implies

\gamma_{1}=w_{1}(\alpha_{j_{1}})=w_{2}(\alpha_{j_{2}})=\gamma_{2}.

Thus, we complete the proof of injectivity of the map
\pi_{G}|_{W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}
, and
statement (1) is proved.

(2) By Lemma
7.7
(1), we have

\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})=\pi_{G}(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
. Hence, the statement
follows from the same reason as the previous corollary.
∎

Remark 7.12
.

Since the two groups
W(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})
and
W(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
are different
in general,
the descriptions (
63
) are (
64
) are not same.

7.4.
Choice of the splitting
F=L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\oplus G

By using the method in the proof of the above corollary, one can rewrite the
description (
64
) by

(67)

R=\Big(\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)\Big)\bigcup\Big(\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\setminus\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)\Big).

As we already mentioned above,
in the rest of the article,
we mainly use the expression
(
67
) of
R

(or its modified version (
92
)).
On the other hand,
the expression (
63
) is used only in the proof of Lemma

7.13
below.

Let us briefly explain why we choose the expression (
67
).
Roughly speaking, the reason is that this expression is compatible with
the decomposition
F=L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\oplus G
of the vector space
F
.
Indeed, since
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\subset L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
, the compatibility of the first term

\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)
of
(
67
) is obvious. Let us discuss the second term. By using (
66
), we have

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\setminus\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma=w(\alpha_{j}^{\prime}):\\
w\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}],\,\alpha_{j}^{\prime}\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\setminus\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\end{subarray}}\big(2w(\alpha_{j})+\big(p_{j}+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)\big)a\big).

Since
2w(\alpha_{j})\in L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
, the second term
of (
67
) is also
compatible with the decomposition
F=L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\oplus G
.
In other words, in the expression (
67
), the only
hyperplane

L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
is used. This fact is suitable for studying the detailed structure of the set

R
of roots.

For comparison, let us recall the expression (
63
):

R=\Big(\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)\Big)\bigcup\Big(\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\cap L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)\Big).

The first term of the right hand side is compatible
with the decomposition
F=L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\oplus G
. On the other hand, the second term
is compatible with the decomposition
F=L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}\oplus G
, but not with

F=L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\oplus G
. That is, two distinct hyperplanes
L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}

and
L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}
are used in this description. There is no problem when studying the first
term and the second term individually, but it is complicated to analyze
the whole structure of
R
. This is the reason why the description
(
63
) is
not
suitable for studying the structure of
R
.

Recall that
\Pi_{G}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\pi_{G}(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})
is a simple system of
R/G
.
As is well-known,
there exists a unique set

\{n_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
of positive integers such that

(a)

\delta:=\sum_{\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}n_{\alpha}\overline{\alpha}

is a generator of the lattice
Q(R/G)\cap\mathrm{rad}(I_{F/G})
of
rank
1
;

(b)

there exists a node
\alpha_{0}\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
such that
n_{\alpha_{0}}=1
,

(see
[
Kac90
]
).
Define an element
\delta_{b}\in Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})\cap\mathrm{rad}(I)
by

(68)

\delta_{b}:=\sum_{\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}n_{\alpha}\alpha.

By definition, it is immediate to see that

(c)

\delta_{b}
is a generator
of the lattice
Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})\cap\mathrm{rad}(I)
of rank
1
.

Lemma 7.13
.

The lattice
\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I)=Q(R)\cap\mathrm{rad}(I)
is
generated by
\delta_{b}
and
a
. That is,

(69)

\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I)=\mathbb{Z}\delta_{b}\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a.

Proof.

Since both
\delta_{b}
and
a
belong to
\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I)
, it is enough to
show
\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I)\subset\mathbb{Z}\delta_{b}\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a
.
By Proposition
7.8
(2), we have

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\Big(\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)\Big)\bigcup\Big(\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)\Big)

\displaystyle\subset\Big(Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a\Big)\bigcup\Big(Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a\Big).

It follows from (
61
) that

\displaystyle Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a=\Big(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}\Big)\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a,

\displaystyle Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a=\Big(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\Big)\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a.

By the definition of the prime map, we have

\alpha_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a\quad\text{for every }0\leq i\leq l.

Therefore,
Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a
is a sublattice of

Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a
, and

\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I)=Q(R)\cap\mathrm{rad}(I)\subset\Big(Q(W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a\Big)\cap\mathrm{rad}(I).

Since the right hand side
=\mathbb{Z}\delta_{b}\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a
by (c), we have

\mathrm{rad}_{\mathbb{Z}}(I)\subset\mathbb{Z}\delta_{b}\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}a
, as desired.
∎

7.5.
Non-reduced counting numbers

In this subsection, we introduce a positive number
k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)
for

\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cup(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})^{*}
, called the “
non-reduced counting number
” of

\alpha
, and study its basic properties. This terminology plays a central role for
defining the elliptic diagram for an arbitrary mERS
(see
\lx@sectionsign

8.1
). Furthermore,
we have an explicit formula for the counting number of
(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\in(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})^{\ast}

in terms of the counting number
of
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
(see Proposition
7.17
).
This formula is used for describing the detailed structure of
R
in the next
subsection.

Let us define the non-reduced counting numbers.

Definition 7.3
.

For an element
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
, define a positive integer

k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
a positive half integer

k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
by the next equalities
:

(70)

\displaystyle(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}=

\displaystyle((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}:\alpha)_{G}\,\alpha+k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})a,

(71)

\displaystyle\alpha=

\displaystyle(\alpha:(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})_{G}\,(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}-k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)a,

where

(x:y)_{G}:=(\overline{x}:\overline{y}).

We call
k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)
the

non-reduced counting number

of an element
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cup(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})^{\ast}
.

By definition, one has

((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}:\alpha)_{G}\in\{1,2\}\quad\text{and}\quad((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}:\alpha)_{G}\,(\alpha:(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})_{G}=1.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
\alpha
be an element of
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.

(1)

k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})=((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}:\alpha)_{G}\,k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)
.

(2)

k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}k((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})&\text{if }\alpha^{\prime}\neq 2\alpha\text{ and }\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}=2\overline{\alpha},\\
k((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

(1) By definition, we have

\displaystyle(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}

\displaystyle=((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}:\alpha)_{G}\,\big\{(\alpha:(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})_{G}\,(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}-k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)a\big\}+k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})a

\displaystyle=(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}+\big\{k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})-((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}:\alpha)_{G}\,k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)\big\}a.

Thus, we have the statement.

(2) Assume
((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}:\alpha)_{G}=1
. Since

((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}:\alpha)_{G}=(\alpha^{\prime}:\alpha)_{G}
, this assumption is equivalent to

\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}=\overline{\alpha}
. In this case,
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha
. Therefore,
the statement is obviously verified. Otherwise, we have

((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}:\alpha)_{G}=2
, and

\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}=2\overline{\alpha}
.
By the definition of
\alpha^{\prime}
,
there exists a unique non-positive integer
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
such that

\alpha^{\prime}=2\alpha+pa
and

(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}=\alpha^{\prime}+k(\alpha^{\prime})a=2\alpha+(k(\alpha^{\prime})+p)a\quad\text{with }k(\alpha^{\prime})+p>0.

Comparing this equality with the definition
k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})
, we have

(72)

k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})=k(\alpha^{\prime})+p.

If
p=0\ (\Longleftrightarrow\,\alpha^{\prime}=2\alpha)
, we have

k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})=k(\alpha^{\prime})=k((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})

by (
14
), as desired. Otherwise, consider the image of the roots
\alpha^{\prime}

and
(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}
by the automorphism
(-\mathrm{Id})\circ r_{\alpha}
, where

r_{\alpha}
signifies the reflection with respect to the root
\alpha
.
By direct computation, we see that both

2\alpha-k^{nr}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})a
and
2\alpha-pa
are roots.
Thus, the only possible case is
p=-k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})
.
Substituting this result to (
72
), we have

2k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})=k(\alpha^{\prime})=k((\alpha^{\prime})^{*}),

as desired.
∎

By Lemma
7.14
and (
14
),
the following formula is verified immediately: for
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
, we have

(73)

k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)=(\alpha\,:\,(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})_{G}\,k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{*})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{4}k(\alpha^{\prime})&\text{if }\alpha^{\prime}\neq 2\alpha\text{ and }\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}=2\overline{\alpha},\\
\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha^{\prime})&\text{if }\alpha^{\prime}=2\alpha,\\
k(\alpha^{\prime})&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

On the other hand, there is another description of the non-reduced counting
number
k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)
for
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.

Proposition 7.15
.

For every
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
, the next formula is valid
:

(74)

k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha)&\text{if }(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\neq 2\alpha^{\ast}\text{ and }\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}=2\overline{\alpha},\\
k(\alpha)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

If
\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}\neq 2\overline{\alpha}
, one has
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha
as in the
proof of the statement (2) of Lemma
7.14
.
Therefore, the statement is a direct consequence of the formula
(
73
), and it is
enough to show (
74
) under the assumption that

\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}=2\overline{\alpha}
.

Assume
(\alpha^{\prime})^{*}=2\alpha^{*}
. Then,
2\alpha^{*}=2\alpha+2k(\alpha)a

is a root. By the definition (
70
)
of
k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})
, we have

k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})=2k(\alpha)
.
By the statement (1) of Lemma
7.14
,
we have

k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)=\dfrac{1}{2}k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})=k(\alpha),

as desired.

Otherwise, we have
(\alpha^{\prime})^{*}\neq 2\alpha^{\ast}
. By the
assumption
\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}=2\overline{\alpha}
, one has

(75)

\alpha^{\prime}=2\alpha+pa\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0})\quad\text{and}\quad(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}=\alpha^{\prime}+k(\alpha^{\prime})a=2\alpha+(k(\alpha^{\prime})+p)a.

Let us consider the following two cases separately.

Case (i)
:
\big(\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})\cap R\big)\setminus\frac{1}{2}R_{l}=\emptyset
;

Case (ii)
:
\big(\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})\cap R\big)\setminus\frac{1}{2}R_{l}\neq\emptyset
.

Case (i)
.
Assume
\big(\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})\cap R\big)\setminus\frac{1}{2}R_{l}=\emptyset
. It means
that

(76)

\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})\cap R=\alpha+\mathbb{Z}k(\alpha)a\subset\frac{1}{2}R_{l}.

Especially, we have
2\alpha\in R
. This means that
p=0
in (
75
),
and
\alpha^{\prime}=2\alpha
. Thus, we have

(77)

(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}=(2\alpha)^{\ast}=2\alpha+k(2\alpha)a\in R.

Moreover, it follows from (
76
) that

\alpha^{\ast}=\alpha+k(\alpha)a\in\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})\cap R\subset\frac{1}{2}R_{l}
.
Hence, we have

2\alpha^{*}=2\alpha+2k(\alpha)a\in R.

This implies
2k(\alpha)\in k(2\alpha)\mathbb{Z}_{>1}
by the assumption

(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\neq 2\alpha^{\ast}
. As
(-\mathrm{Id})\circ r_{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}}
preserves

R
and
(-\mathrm{Id})\circ r_{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}}(\alpha)=\alpha+k(2\alpha)a
, one sees
that
k(2\alpha)\in k(\alpha)\mathbb{Z}
since
R\cap(\alpha+\mathbb{Z}a)=\alpha+\mathbb{Z}k(\alpha)a
.
Hence, we obtain

(78)

k(\alpha)=k(2\alpha).

As
\alpha^{\prime}=2\alpha\in R
, the formula (
73
) implies

k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}k(2\alpha)=\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha).

Thus, we have shown (
74
) in this case.

Case (ii)
. If
\big(\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})\cap R\big)\setminus\frac{1}{2}R_{l}\neq\emptyset
,
the element
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
belongs to

\big(\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})\cap R\big)\setminus\frac{1}{2}R_{l}
by the
condition (
56
).
Thus, we have
2\alpha\not\in R
, and

(79)

k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{4}k(\alpha^{\prime})

by the formula (
73
).
On the other hand, since
2\alpha\not\in R
, we have
\alpha^{\prime}\neq 2\alpha
.
This means

\alpha^{\prime}=2\alpha+pa\quad\text{with}\quad p\in\mathbb{Z}_{<0}

in (
75
).
By a similar argument to the last part
of the proof Lemma
7.14
(2), it follows that

(80)

p=-\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha^{\prime}).

Note that

(-\mathrm{Id})\circ r_{\alpha^{\prime}}(\alpha)=\alpha+pa

is a root. By (
80
), we have

(81)

p=-\dfrac{1}{2}k(\alpha^{\prime})\in k(\alpha)\mathbb{Z}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad k(\alpha^{\prime})\in 2k(\alpha)\mathbb{Z}.

Similarly, since

(-\mathrm{Id})\circ r_{\alpha^{\ast}}(\alpha^{\prime})=\alpha^{\prime}+(-2p+4k(\alpha))a

is also a root, we have

(82)

-2p+4k(\alpha)=k(\alpha^{\prime})+4k(\alpha)\in k(\alpha^{\prime})\mathbb{Z}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad 4k(\alpha)\in k(\alpha^{\prime})\mathbb{Z}.

By (
81
) and (
82
), it follows that

k(\alpha^{\prime})\in\{2k(\alpha),4k(\alpha)\}.

Suppose that
k(\alpha^{\prime})=4k(\alpha)
. In this case, as
\alpha^{\prime}=2\alpha-2k(\alpha)a
, hence

(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}=2\alpha+2k(\alpha)a=2\alpha^{\ast}
which contradicts to our assumption.
Thus, we have

(83)

k(\alpha^{\prime})=2k(\alpha)

and it follows from (
79
) that

k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha)
, as desired.
∎

Let us rewrite the conditions for
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
in the statement (2) of
Lemma
7.14
and one in Proposition
7.15

, for later use.

Corollary 7.16
.

For
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
, we have

(84)

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}k((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})&\text{if }2\alpha\not\in R\text{ and }2\overline{\alpha}\in R/G,\\
k((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(85)

\displaystyle k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha)&\text{if }\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R\text{ and }\frac{1}{2}\overline{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}}\in R/G,\\
k(\alpha)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Since (
84
) is proved in a similar way to
(
85
) with an easier argument, we only give a proof of
the latter. For
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
, note that the following equivalences
are verified immediately by the definition of the prime map:

(86)

\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}=2\overline{\alpha}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad 2\overline{\alpha}\in R/G\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{1}{2}\overline{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}}\in R/G.

It is enough to show the following claim for proving (
85
):

Claim.

Under the assumption that

\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}=2\overline{\alpha}
,
the next two conditions are equivalent.

(a)

(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}=2\alpha^{\ast}
.

(b)

\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
.

Let us prove this claim. Since (a)
\Longrightarrow
(b) is trivially verified, it is enough
to show condition (b) implies (a). For that purpose, assume

(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\neq 2\alpha^{\ast}
. As

\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}=2\overline{\alpha}
, we have

(87)

\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{*}=\alpha+\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha)a

by the formulas in the proof of the last proposition.
Indeed, if
\big(\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})\cap R\big)\setminus\frac{1}{2}R_{l}=\emptyset
, it follows from (
77
) and (
78
).
Otherwise, it follows from (
80
) that

\alpha^{\prime}=2\alpha-\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha^{\prime})a
. Therefore, we have

(\alpha^{\prime})^{*}=\alpha^{\prime}+k(\alpha^{\prime})a=2\alpha+\dfrac{1}{2}k(\alpha^{\prime})a\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}=\alpha+\frac{1}{4}k(\alpha^{\prime})a.

Substituting (
83
) to the right hand side, we have
(
87
).

Hence, we have

\frac{1}{2}\overline{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}}=\overline{\alpha}.

Since
R\cap\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})=\alpha+\mathbb{Z}k(\alpha)a
, the
element
\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{*}
should not belong to
R
. Thus, we have the
claim, and the proof of the corollary is completed.
∎

Combining the above results, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 7.17
.

Let
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
. If
2\overline{\alpha}\in R/G\ \left(\Longleftrightarrow\ \frac{1}{2}\overline{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}}\in R/G\right)
, we have

(88)

k((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})=\begin{cases}2k(\alpha)&\text{if }2\alpha\not\in R\text{ and }\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R,\\
4k(\alpha)&\text{if }2\alpha\not\in R\text{ and }\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R,\\
k(\alpha)&\text{if }2\alpha\in R\text{ and }\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R,\\
2k(\alpha)&\text{if }2\alpha\in R\text{ and }\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R.\end{cases}

Otherwise,

(89)

k((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})=k(\alpha).

Proof.

The statement follows immediately from Lemma
7.14
(2)
and Corollary
7.16
.
∎

The following lemma is useful for later discussion.

Lemma 7.18
.

For every
\lambda\in F
, we have

(90)

r_{\alpha}r_{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}}(\lambda)=\lambda-I(\lambda,\alpha^{\vee})k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)a.

Proof.

The statement follows from direct computation.
Indeed, by the definition,

\displaystyle r_{\alpha}r_{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=r_{\alpha}\left(\lambda-I(\lambda,((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})^{\vee})(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda-I(\lambda,\alpha^{\vee})\alpha-I(\lambda,((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})^{\vee})\left((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}-I((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast},\alpha^{\vee})\alpha\right).

Since

\displaystyle I(\lambda,((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})^{\vee})=

\displaystyle(\alpha:(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})_{G}\,I(\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}),

\displaystyle I((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast},\alpha^{\vee})=

\displaystyle((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}:\alpha)_{G}\,I(\alpha,\alpha^{\vee})=2((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}:\alpha)_{G},

one has

\displaystyle r_{\alpha}r_{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda-I(\lambda,\alpha^{\vee})\left((\alpha:(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})_{G}\,(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}-\alpha\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda-I(\lambda,\alpha^{\vee})k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha)a.

as desired.
∎

Remark 7.19
.

By Lemma
7.14
(1), the following formula is
verified:

I(\lambda,((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})^{\vee})k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})=I(\lambda,\alpha^{\vee})(\alpha:(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})_{G}k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})=I(\lambda,\alpha^{\vee})k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha).

7.6.
A presentation of
R

In this subsection, we give
an explicit description of the set
R
of roots (see (
92
) in Proposition

7.20
below). In this description, every element of
R
is written
using information on the affine Weyl group
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]
and
counting numbers of the elements of
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
. This fact is essentially used
to define the “elliptic diagram” of a mERS
(R,G)
in
\lx@sectionsign

8.1
.

Proposition 7.20
.

Let
(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
be a paired simple system of a mERS
(R,G)
.

(1)
Let
\gamma=w((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})
for
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and
w\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]
.
Then

(91)

\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a=\begin{cases}2w\alpha+(2\mathbb{Z}+1)k(\alpha)a&\text{if $2\alpha\not\in R$,
$2\overline{\alpha}\in R/G$ and $\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R$},\\
2(w\alpha+(2\mathbb{Z}+1)k(\alpha)a)&\text{if $2\alpha\not\in R$,
$2\overline{\alpha}\in R/G$ and $\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R$},\\
2w\alpha+\mathbb{Z}k(\alpha)a&\text{if $2\alpha\in R$ and
$\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R$},\\
2(w\alpha+\mathbb{Z}k(\alpha)a)&\text{if $2\alpha\in R$, and
$\frac{1}{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R$},\\
w\alpha+\mathbb{Z}k(\alpha)a&\text{if $2\overline{\alpha}\not\in R/G$}.\\
\end{cases}

(2)
There exists the following description of the set
R
of roots
:

(92)

R=\Big(\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)\Big)\bigcup\Big(\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\setminus\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})^{\ast}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)\Big)

Proof.

(1) By the definition (
70
) of

k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})
and (
84
),
we have

\displaystyle w((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})+\mathbb{Z}k(w((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}))a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}:\alpha)_{G}w(\alpha)+k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})a+\mathbb{Z}k((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2w(\alpha)+(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2})k((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})a&\text{if $2\alpha\not\in R$ and $2\overline{\alpha}\in R/G$},\\
2w(\alpha)+\mathbb{Z}k((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})a&\text{if $2\alpha\in R$ and $2\overline{\alpha}\in R/G$},\\
w(\alpha)+\mathbb{Z}k((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})a&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Substituting (
88
) to the equality above, the statement
is obtained.

(2) The description (
92
) is an immediate consequence of
(
67
). Indeed, let
\gamma=w((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\setminus\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})^{\ast}
. For such
\gamma
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})+\mathbb{Z}k\big(w((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})\big)a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w(\alpha^{\prime}+k(\alpha^{\prime})a)+\mathbb{Z}k\big(w(\alpha^{\prime})\big)a\quad(\because\ \eqref{k=kast})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w(\alpha^{\prime})+k(w(\alpha^{\prime}))a+\mathbb{Z}k\big(w(\alpha^{\prime})\big)a\quad(\because\ W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]\subset\mathrm{Aut}(R)\text{ $\&$ Lemma \ref{lemma:countings1} (3)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w(\alpha^{\prime})+\mathbb{Z}k\big(w(\alpha^{\prime})\big)a.

Therefore, we get

\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\setminus\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})^{\ast}}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a)=\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\setminus\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})}(\gamma+\mathbb{Z}k(\gamma)a),

and the description (
92
) coincides with that of
(
67
).
∎

Remark 7.21
.

By definition, we have

(93)

(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\setminus\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})^{\ast}=\{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\,|\,\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\,2\overline{\alpha}\in R/G\}.

Corollary 7.22
.

The number
\mathrm{g.c.d.}\{k(\alpha)\,|\,\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\}
is equal to
1
.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
7.20

that
\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\mathbb{Z}k(\alpha)a

generates the lattice
Q(R)\cap G
of rank
1
. Thus,
the statement is verified immediately.
∎

7.7.
Uniqueness of paired simple system

In this subsection, we give a solution to the “uniqueness problem” of
a simple system of
(R,G)
stated in
\lx@sectionsign

2.1

(see Proposition
7.23

and Proposition
7.24
below).

Proposition 7.23
.

For each
i=1,2
, let
(R_{i},G_{i})
be a mERS, and

(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{i},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{i})
be a paired simple system of
(R_{i},G_{i})
.
Fix a generator
a_{i}
of the lattice
Q(R_{i})\cap G_{i}
of rank
1
.
Assume
(R_{1},G_{1})
and

(R_{2},G_{2})
are isomorphic. Then, there exists an isomorphism

\varphi:(R_{1},G_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}(R_{2},G_{2})
such that

\varphi(a_{1})=a_{2}
,

\varphi(\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})=\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and
\varphi(\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})=\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.

It is enough to show the statement in the case where
(R_{1},G_{1})=(R_{2},G_{2})

and
a_{1}=a_{2}
. Denote them by
(R,G)
and
a
, respectively.
Assume there exists
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(R,G)
such that

\varphi(\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})=\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.
Then, by Lemma
7.5
, we have

\varphi(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{1})=\varphi((\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})^{pr})=(\varphi(\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}))^{pr}=(\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})^{pr}=\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}.

Therefore, the above proposition is reduced to the following.

Proposition 7.24
.

Let
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
be two simple systems of
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},G)
which
satisfy condition (
56
).
Then, there exists an automorphism
\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(R,G)
such that

\varphi(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{1})=\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.

In the case when
R/G
is reduced, this statement was proved by K. Saito
(
[
Sai85
]
, (6.2) Corollary).
Our proof is a generalization of the original one which requires
some adaptation because of the non-reducibility of
R/G
.

Before starting the proof of Proposition
7.24
, we
prepare the following two lemmas.

Lemma 7.25
.

Let
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{1}=\{\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{l}\}
and
\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\{\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{l}\}

be two simple systems of
(R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},G)
which satisfy condition (
56
).
Assume

(94)

\overline{\alpha_{i}}=\overline{\beta_{i}}\quad\text{for every}\quad 0\leq i\leq l.

We have the following.

(1)
An equality
k(\alpha_{i})=k(\beta_{i})
holds for every
0\leq i\leq l
.

(2)
The condition
2\alpha_{i}\in R
is equivalent to
2\beta_{i}\in R
.

(3)
An equality
k((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})=k((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})
holds for every

0\leq i\leq l
.

(4)
Under the assumption
2\overline{\alpha_{i}}=2\overline{\beta_{i}}\in R/G
,
the following conditions are equivalent
:

(a)

\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
,

(b)

\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
.

Proof.

Note that there exist integers

m_{i}\ (0\leq i\leq l)
such that

(95)

\beta_{i}=\alpha_{i}+m_{i}k(\alpha_{i})a

by condition (
94
).

Statement (1) is an easy consequence of
Lemma
2.7
(1).
Statement (2) follows from
(
56
) and (
94
). Indeed,
since
\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha_{i}})=\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\beta_{i}})
by
(
94
), the
condition
\big(\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha_{i}})\cap R\big)\setminus\frac{1}{2}R_{l}\neq\emptyset
is
equivalent to
\big(\pi_{G}^{-1}(\overline{\beta_{i}})\cap R\big)\setminus\frac{1}{2}R_{l}\neq\emptyset
.
Since both
\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and
\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
satisfy condition (
56
),
we have statement (2).

Let us prove statements (3) and (4).
If
2\overline{\alpha_{i}}=2\overline{\beta_{i}}\not\in R/G
, we have

\alpha^{\prime}_{i}=\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}^{\prime}=\beta_{i}
. Thus, statement (3) is a direct
consequence of statement (1), in this case.
Statement (4) is not concerned in this case.

Assume
2\overline{\alpha_{i}}=2\overline{\beta_{i}}\in R/G
. Consider the
following three cases:

(i)

2\alpha_{i}\not\in R
and
\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R
,

(ii)

2\alpha_{i}\not\in R
and
\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
,

(iii)

2\alpha_{i}\in R
.

Case (i): Let us prove
\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R
.
Assume
\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
.
Applying Proposition
7.17
to the paired simple system

(\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
, we have

k(\beta^{\prime}_{i})=k((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})=4k(\beta_{i})=4k(\alpha_{i}).

Note that the last equality follows from statement (1).
Since
2\beta_{i}\not\in R
by statement (2), we have the following description
of
(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
:

(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}=2\beta_{i}+\frac{1}{2}k(\beta_{i}^{\prime})a=2\alpha_{i}+2(m_{i}+1)k(\alpha_{i})a.

Since both
\alpha_{i}
and
(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}
are roots, the element

(r_{\alpha_{i}}r_{(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}})^{p}((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})

is also a root for every
p\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By Lemma
7.18
, one has

(r_{\alpha_{i}}r_{(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}})^{p}((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})=(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}-2pk(\alpha_{i})a=2\alpha_{i}+2(m_{i}+1-p)k(\alpha_{i})a.

Setting
p=m_{i}+1
, the equality above indicates that
2\alpha_{i}
belongs to
R
.
This is a contradiction. Therefore, we have
\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R
,
as desired.

Furthermore, applying Proposition
7.17
to the paired
simple systems
(\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
and
(\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
, we have

k((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})=2k(\alpha_{i})
and
k((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})=2k(\beta_{i})
, respectively.
Hence, statement (1) implies (3), in this case.

Case (ii): Assume
\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R
.
Exchanging the roles of

\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
in the previous case, we have a contradiction.
Thus, we get
\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
, and

k((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})=4k(\beta_{i})=4k(\alpha_{i})=k((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}).

Case (iii): By statement (2), we have
2\beta_{i}\in R
. Thus,

\alpha^{\prime}_{i}=2\alpha_{i}\in R\quad\text{and}\quad\beta^{\prime}_{i}=2\beta_{i}\in R,

in this case. Therefore, one obtains

\overline{\alpha_{i}^{\prime}}=2\overline{\alpha_{i}}=2\overline{\beta_{i}}=\overline{\beta^{\prime}_{i}}\in R/G

and

k((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})=k(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})=k(\beta_{i}^{\prime})=k((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}).

This is nothing but statement (3).
Assume
\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
. By Proposition
7.17

for
(\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
, we have
k(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})=2k(\alpha_{i})
.
Hence, by the above results, we get

\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\beta_{i}^{\prime}+k(\beta_{i}^{\prime})a\right)=\beta_{i}+\frac{1}{2}k((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})a=\alpha_{i}+(m_{i}+1)k(\alpha_{i})a\in R.

Thus, we showed that the condition
\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i})^{\ast}\in R
implies

\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{i})^{\ast}\in R
for every case.
Exchanging
the roles
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
, we have the
converse
statement. Thus, the proof of statement (4) is completed.
∎

Lemma 7.26
.

Under the same setting
as in
the previous lemma,
there exists an automorphism
\psi_{1}\in\mathrm{Aut}(R,G)
such that

(96)

\psi_{1}(\alpha_{i})=\beta_{i}\quad\text{for every}\quad 0\leq i\leq l.

Proof.

Recall a decomposition

(97)

F=L_{\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\oplus G

of
F
, where
L_{\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{R}\alpha_{i}
is a one-codimensional
subspace of
F
spanned by
\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
. Define a linear automorphism

\psi_{1}\in\mathrm{GL}(F)
by

\psi_{1}\ :\ \Big(\sum_{i=0}^{l}\lambda_{i}\alpha_{i}\Big)+\mu a\ \longmapsto\Big(\sum_{i=0}^{l}\lambda_{i}\alpha_{i}\Big)+\Big(\mu+\sum_{i=0}^{l}\lambda_{i}m_{i}k(\alpha_{i})\Big)a.

By (
95
),
condition (
96
) is
satisfied, and
\psi_{1}(a)=a
.

The remaining part is to prove that
\psi_{1}
preserves the set
R
. By the description
(
92
) of
R
, it is enough to show that

(98)

\psi_{1}\big(\gamma+pk(\gamma)a\big)\in R\quad\text{for $p\in\mathbb{Z}$ and $\gamma=w_{1}(\alpha_{i})$ or
$\gamma=w_{1}((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})$},

where
w_{1}\in W([)\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]
and
\alpha_{i}\in\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.

Claim
.

(99)

\psi_{1}((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})=(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}.

Proof.

This equality follows from case-by-case analysis. For example, assume

(100)

2\alpha_{i}\not\in R,\quad 2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\in R/G
and
\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
.

By this assumption and Proposition
7.17
,
we have

(101)

\displaystyle\psi_{1}((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\psi_{1}\left(2\alpha_{i}+\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha^{\prime}_{i})a\right)=2\beta_{i}+2k(\alpha_{i})a

\displaystyle=2\beta_{i}+2k(\beta_{i})a.

Note that we used
k(\alpha_{i})=k(\beta_{i})
in the last equality.
On the other hand, by Lemma
7.25
and
the assumption (
100
), we have

2\beta_{i}\not\in R,\,2\overline{\beta_{i}}\in R/G
and

\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
. Therefore, by Proposition

7.17
, we have

(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}=2\beta_{i}+\frac{1}{2}k(\beta_{i}^{\prime})a=2\beta_{i}+2k(\beta_{i})a.

Combining these two equalities, we get
\psi_{1}((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})=(\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}

as desired. For the other cases, the equality (
99
) holds by a similar
method.
∎

Assume
\gamma=w_{1}((\alpha_{1})^{\ast})
.
Since
\psi_{1}\circ r_{\alpha_{j}}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1}=r_{\psi_{1}(\alpha_{j})}=r_{\beta_{j}}\in W([)\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]

for every
0\leq j\leq l
,
w_{2}:=\psi_{1}\circ w_{1}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1}
is an element of

W([)\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]\subset W(R)
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\psi_{1}\big(\gamma+pk(\gamma)a\big)

\displaystyle=\psi_{1}(\gamma)+pk(\gamma)a

\displaystyle=(\psi_{1}\circ w_{1}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1})\big(\psi_{1}((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})\big)+pk(w_{1}((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}))a

\displaystyle=w_{2}((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})+pk((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})a\quad\text{($\because\,w_{1}\in\mathrm{Aut}(R)$)}

\displaystyle=w_{2}((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})+pk((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})a\quad\text{(by Lemma \ref{lemma:nr-counting-Autom3} (3))}

\displaystyle=w_{2}((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})+pk(w_{2}((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}))a\quad\text{($\because\,w_{2}\in\mathrm{Aut}(R)$)}.

Since
w_{2}((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})
is a root, the right hand side belongs to
R
.
Thus, (
98
) is obtained. For the case that

\gamma=w_{1}(\alpha_{i})
, we have (
98
) in a similar way
