with an easier argument. Thus, we completed the proof.
∎

Now, let us prove Proposition
7.24
.

Proof of Proposition
7.24
.
Denote
\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\{\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{l}\}
and

\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\{\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{l}\}
. Since both

\pi_{G}(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{1})=\{\overline{\alpha_{0}},\ldots,\overline{\alpha_{l}}\}
and

\pi_{G}(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{2})=\{\overline{\beta_{0}},\ldots,\overline{\beta_{l}}\}
are simple systems
of the non-reduced affine root system
R/G
, there exists an element

\overline{w}\in W(R/G)
and a sign
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
such that

\{\epsilon\overline{w}(\overline{\alpha_{0}}),\ldots,\epsilon\overline{w}(\overline{\alpha_{l}})\}=\{\overline{\beta_{0}},\ldots,\overline{\beta_{l}}\}.

After suitable change of the numbering of the elements of
\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
, we may
assume that
\epsilon\overline{w}(\overline{\alpha_{i}})=\overline{\beta_{i}}
for every

0\leq i\leq l
. Furthermore, take an element
w\in W(R)
so that

(\pi_{G})_{*}(w)=\overline{w}
. Then,

w\in\mathrm{Aut}(R,G)
, and it is easy to see that
w(\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})
is a simple system of

(R,G)
such that condition (
56
) is satisfied.
Thus, it is enough to prove the proposition
under the assumption

\epsilon(\overline{\alpha_{i}})=\overline{\beta_{i}}\quad\text{for every }0\leq i\leq l.

Recall the decomposition
F=L_{\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}\oplus G
of
F
, where

L_{\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{R}\alpha_{i}
is a one-codimensional subspace of
F

spanned by
\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
. With respect to this decomposition, write

\lambda=\lambda_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{1}}}+\lambda_{G}
for
\lambda\in F
, where

\lambda_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{1}}}\in L_{\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}}
and
\lambda_{G}\in G
.
Define a linear map
\psi_{2}\in\mathrm{GL}(F)
by

\psi_{2}\ :\ \lambda=\lambda_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{1}}}+\lambda_{G}\ \longmapsto\ \epsilon\lambda_{L_{\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{1}}}+\lambda_{G}.

Let us prove that
\psi_{2}
belongs to
\mathrm{Aut}(R,G)
. Since there is nothing
to prove if
\epsilon=1
, we may assume
\epsilon=-1
. By the definition,
it is obvious that
\psi_{2}(a)=a
. Let
\alpha\in R
.
By the expression (
92
) of
R
in Proposition

7.20
, there exist
w\in W([)\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]
,

\alpha_{i}\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{1}
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

\alpha=\begin{cases}2w(\alpha_{i})+(2m+1)k(\alpha_{i})a&\text{if $2\alpha_{i}\not\in R,\,2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\in R/G$
and $\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R$},\\
2w(\alpha_{i})+(4m+2)k(\alpha_{i})a&\text{if $2\alpha_{i}\not\in R,\,2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\in R/G$
and $\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R$},\\
2w(\alpha_{i})+mk(\alpha_{i})a&\text{if $2\alpha_{i}\in R$ and
$\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R$},\\
2w(\alpha_{i})+2mk(\alpha_{i})a&\text{if $2\alpha_{i}\in R$ and
$\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R$},\\
w(\alpha_{i})+mk(\alpha_{i})a&\text{if $2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\not\in R/G$}.\end{cases}

In any case, as
-w(\alpha_{i})=wr_{\alpha_{i}}(\alpha_{i})
and

wr_{\alpha_{i}}\in W(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})
, we see that
\psi_{2}(\alpha)\in R
.
Thus,
\psi_{2}
is an element of
\mathrm{Aut}(R,G)
.

Now, it is enough to show the proposition under the assumption

\overline{\alpha_{i}}=\overline{\beta_{i}}\quad\text{for every }0\leq i\leq l.

This is exactly treated in Lemma
7.26
.
Thus, we have the proposition.

\square

8.
Main theorem

In this section, we introduce a diagram associated with each mERS
(R,G)
with non-
reduced affine quotient
R/G
which encodes the informations
obtained in the previous section. This is an extension of the notion of elliptic diagram introduced in
\lx@sectionsign

2.4
. Interpreting the results obtained in the previous section in terms of diagrams, the strong classification theorem of mERS
with non-reduced affine quotient is given as an application.

8.1.
Elliptic diagrams II: non-reduced affine quotient

The aim of this subsection is to define the elliptic diagram

\Gamma(R,G)
for a
mERS
(R,G)
whose quotient system
R/G
is
non-reduced
.
Fix a paired simple system
(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
of
(R,G)
.
Let
\Gamma^{\downarrow}(R,G)
and
\Gamma^{\uparrow}(R,G)
be the Dynkin
diagrams of the affine root systems
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cong(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and

W([)\Pi^{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}].\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}\cong(R/G)^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
, respectively.
We regard
\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
(resp.
\alpha^{\prime}\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
) as a node of the graph

\Gamma^{\downarrow}(R,G)
(resp.
\Gamma^{\uparrow}(R,G)
) and denote the set
of nodes by

|\Gamma^{\downarrow}(R,G)|
and
|\Gamma^{\uparrow}(R,G)|
,
respectively. If there is no risk of confusion, we may denote

\Gamma^{\downarrow}=\Gamma^{\downarrow}(R,G)

and

\Gamma^{\uparrow}=\Gamma^{\uparrow}(R,G)

for
simplicity.

Definition 8.1
.

Define the numbers

m_{\alpha}
for

\alpha\in|\Gamma^{\downarrow}(R,G)|
by

(102)

m_{\alpha}=\frac{I_{R}(\alpha,\alpha)}{2k^{nr}(\alpha)}n_{\alpha},

and call it the
exponent
of the root
\alpha
.

Remark 8.1
.

In (
102
), we define the exponent
m_{\alpha}
only
for
\alpha\in|\Gamma^{\downarrow}(R,G)|
. By using

\Gamma^{\uparrow}(R,G)
instead of
\Gamma^{\downarrow}(R,G)
, one can define
the “exponent” for a node in
|\Gamma^{\uparrow}(R,G)|
. The explicit relationship
between these two exponents is given in Section
9

(
see
Proposition
9.8
)
.

Set
m_{\max}=\max\{m_{\alpha}\,|\,\alpha\in|\Gamma^{\downarrow}(R,G)|\,\}
.
Let
\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow}=\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow}(R,G)

be the subdiagram of

\Gamma^{\downarrow}(R,G)
consisting of nodes

|\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow}(R,G)|:=\{\alpha\in|\Gamma^{\downarrow}(R,G)|\,|\,m_{\alpha}=m_{\max}\,\}.

Set

|\Gamma_{m}^{\uparrow}(R,G)^{\ast}|=\{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\,|\,\alpha\in|\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow}(R,G)|\,\}.

Definition 8.2
.

The
elliptic diagram

\Gamma(R,G)
for a marked elliptic root system

(R,G)
is the graph whose set of nodes is

(103)

|\Gamma(R,G)|:=|\Gamma^{\downarrow}(R,G)|\,\amalg\,|\Gamma_{m}^{\uparrow}(R,G)^{\ast}|

and is represented by

(Node1’)

\alpha

if either
2\alpha\in R
or
\frac{1}{2}\alpha\in R
,

(Node2’)

\alpha

if
2\alpha,\frac{1}{2}\alpha\not\in R
but either
2\overline{\alpha}\in R/G
or

\frac{1}{2}\overline{\alpha}\in R/G
,

(Node3’)

\alpha

otherwise

for
\alpha\in|\Gamma(R,G)|
, and any two nodes

\alpha,\beta\in|\Gamma(R,G)|
are connected by the rules

(
Edge0
)
-
(
Edge4
)
, with the additional cases

(Edge5)

\alpha
\beta

if
I(\alpha^{\vee},\beta)=4,I(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})=1
and

\overline{\beta}=2\overline{\alpha}
, for
\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.04544pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#CCCCCC} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.94 0 C 3.94 2.17 2.17 3.94 0 3.94 C -2.17 3.94 -3.94 2.17 -3.94 0 C -3.94 -2.17 -2.17 -3.94 0 -3.94 C 2.17 -3.94 3.94 -2.17 3.94 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84544pt\lower-2.84544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.94 0 C 3.94 2.17 2.17 3.94 0 3.94 C -2.17 3.94 -3.94 2.17 -3.94 0 C -3.94 -2.17 -2.17 -3.94 0 -3.94 C 2.17 -3.94 3.94 -2.17 3.94 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\}
.

A node
which satisfies the condition
(Node1’)

(
resp.
(Node2’), (Node3’))

is called a black
(
resp. gray, white
)
node.

For each
i=1,2
, let
(R_{i},G_{i})
be a mERS belonging to
(F_{i},I_{i})
.
Fix a paired simple system
(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{i},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{i})
of
(R_{i},G_{i})
and let

\Gamma(R_{i},G_{i})
be the corresponding elliptic diagram. We say
that two graphs
\Gamma(R_{1},G_{1})
and
\Gamma(R_{2},G_{2})
are
isomorphic

if there exists a
bijection
\varphi:|\Gamma(R_{1},G_{1})|\xrightarrow{\sim}|\Gamma(R_{2},G_{2})|

such that

(i)

the bijection
\varphi
preserves the splitting (
103
)
of nodes;

(ii)

(I_{1})_{R_{1}}(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})=(I_{2})_{R_{2}}(\varphi(\alpha),\varphi(\beta)^{\vee})
for every

\alpha,\beta\in|\Gamma(R_{1},G_{1})|
;

(iii)

m_{\alpha}=m_{\varphi(\alpha)}
for every
\alpha\in\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
.

Note that the definition of
\Gamma(R,G)
depends on
a choice of a paired simple system
(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
of
(R,G)
.
However, the following proposition holds.

Proposition 8.2
.

Recall the setting in Proposition
7.23
. That is,
let
(R_{i},G_{i})
be a mERS, and
(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{i},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}_{i})
be a paired
simple system of
(R_{i},G_{i})
for
i=1,2
. Fix a generator
a_{i}

of the lattice
Q(R_{i})\cap G_{i}
of rank
1
. Assume that
(R_{1},G_{1})
and

(R_{2},G_{2})
are isomorphic. Then, the graphs

\Gamma(R_{1},G_{1})
and
\Gamma(R_{2},G_{2})
are isomorphic. Especially,
the elliptic diagram
\Gamma(R,G)
does not depend on a choice of a paired
simple system
(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})
of
(R,G)
.

Proof.

Denote
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{1}=\{\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{l}\}\subset R_{1}
and

\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\{\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{l}\}\subset R_{2}
.
By Proposition
7.23
, there exists an isomorphism

\varphi:(R_{1},G_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}(R_{2},G_{2})
such that

\varphi(a_{1})=a_{2}
,
\varphi(\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})=\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
and

\varphi(\Pi_{1}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})=\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
.
After renumbering suitably the elements of
\Pi_{2}^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
,
we may assume

\varphi(\alpha_{i})=\beta_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi((\alpha_{i}^{\prime}))=\beta_{i}^{\prime}\quad\text{for every }0\leq i\leq l.

Lemma 8.3
.

Under the same setting as Proposition
8.2
, we have
the following formulae
:

(1)

k(\varphi(\alpha))=k(\alpha)
for every
\alpha\in R_{1}
;

(2)

\varphi(\alpha)^{\ast}=\varphi(\alpha^{\ast})
for every
\alpha\in R_{1}
.

Proof.

Similar to Proposition
7.23
, it is enough to prove
the statements in the case where
(R_{1},G_{1})=(R_{2},G_{2})
and
\varphi(a_{1})=a_{2}
.
Denote them by
(R,G)
and
a
, respectively. Then,
statement (1) is already proved in
Lemma
2.7
(3). Statement (2) follows immediately from
statement (1).
∎

Let us return to the proof of the proposition. By Lemma
8.3
,
we have

(104)

k(\alpha_{i})=k(\beta_{i})\quad\text{and}\quad k((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})=k((\beta_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})\quad\text{for every }0\leq i\leq l.

In addition, by Lemma
7.25
and the definition of
the non-reduced counting numbers, we have

(105)

k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{i})=k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\beta_{i})\quad\text{for every }0\leq i\leq l.

On the other hand, since
\varphi
is an isomorphism between two root
systems
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
, it is known that there exists a non-zero constant

c
such that

I_{1}(\lambda,\mu)=cI_{2}(\varphi(\lambda),\varphi(\mu))
for every

\lambda,\mu\in F_{1}
.
(see (1.4) Lemma in
[
Sai85
]
). After taking normalizations
(I_{1})_{R_{1}}

(I_{2})_{R_{2}}
of
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
respectively
(cf. (
3
)), we have

(106)

(I_{1})_{R_{1}}(\lambda,\mu)=(I_{2})_{R_{2}}(\varphi(\lambda),\varphi(\mu))\quad\text{for every}\quad\lambda,\mu\in F_{1}.

Especially, it follows that

(I_{1})_{R_{1}}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})=(I_{2})_{R_{2}}(\beta_{i},\beta_{j}^{\vee})

for every
0\leq i,j\leq l
.
As a by-product of this equality, we get

n_{\alpha_{i}}=n_{\beta_{i}}\quad\text{for every}\quad 0\leq i\leq l.

Combining the above results, we have

m_{\alpha_{i}}=\frac{(I_{1})_{R_{1}}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})}{2k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{i})}n_{\alpha_{i}}=\frac{(I_{2})_{R_{2}}(\beta_{i},\beta_{i})}{2k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\beta_{i})}n_{\beta_{i}}=m_{\beta_{i}}.

Thus, the automorphism
\varphi
induces a bijection

\varphi:|\Gamma(R_{1},G_{1})|\xrightarrow{\sim}|\Gamma(R_{2},G_{2})|
which
satisfies the conditions (i), (ii), (iii) above. Therefore, the graphs

\Gamma(R_{1},G_{1})
and
\Gamma(R_{2},G_{2})
are isomorphic.
∎

In the next subsection, we classify the elliptic diagrams of mERSs with non-reduced
affine quotients, up to “existence”.
In other words, we discuss only the possibility of these diagrams.
The results are obtained by detailed case analysis depending on the type of
R/G
,
and are exhibited in Table
4\sim 8
in
\lx@sectionsign

8.4
(see Theorem

8.12
and Corollary
8.13
also).
For the existence of a mERS whose diagram is a given one, see
\lx@sectionsign

8.5
(especially Remark
8.16
below).

Until the end of the subsection, we use symbols omitting
(R,G)
for simplicity:

\Gamma=\Gamma(R,G)
,
\Pi=\Pi(R,G)
, etc.

8.2.
Higher rank cases

Throughout this subsection, we assume
(R,G)
is a mERS
with the non-reduced affine quotient
R/G
.
It is of type

BCC_{l}\,(l\geq 1),\quad C^{\vee}BC_{l}\,(l\geq 1),\quad BB^{\vee}_{l}\,(l\geq 2)\quad\text{or}\quad C^{\vee}C_{l}\,(l\geq 1)

(see
\lx@sectionsign

B.2
for explicit descriptions of such root systems).
In the following, choose a numbering of the set

\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l}\}

so that
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}_{G}=\{\overline{\alpha_{0}},\overline{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,\overline{\alpha_{l}}\}

is enumerated as in the list of
\lx@sectionsign

B.2
.

Let
(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}
and

(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}

be subdiagrams of
\Gamma^{\downarrow}
with
the set of nodes given as follows:

|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}|:=\{\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|\,|\,2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\not\in R/G\}\quad\text{and}\quad|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}|:=|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|\setminus|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}|.

Set

\begin{array}[]{rlllllll}|(\Gamma^{\downarrow}_{m})^{\mathrm{w}}|&\!\!\!\!=|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}|\cap|\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow}|&\!\!\!\!=\big\{\alpha_{i}\in|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}|\,\big|\,m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{max}\big\},\\[3.0pt]
|(\Gamma^{\downarrow}_{m})^{\mathrm{nw}}|&\!\!\!\!=|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}|\cap|\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow}|&\!\!\!\!=\big\{\alpha_{i}\in|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}|\,\big|\,m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{max}\big\}.\end{array}

Then there exists a decomposition of
|\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow}|
:

|\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow}|=|(\Gamma^{\downarrow}_{m})^{\mathrm{w}}|\,\amalg\,|(\Gamma^{\downarrow}_{m})^{\mathrm{nw}}|.

Similarly, set

\displaystyle|((\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{w}}|=\big\{(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in|(\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast}|\,\big|\,\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}}\not\in R/G\big\},

\displaystyle|((\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{nw}}|=|(\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast}|\setminus|((\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{w}}|,

where
(\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast}:=\Gamma_{m}^{\uparrow}(R,G)^{\ast}

(see Definition
8.2
). Then, we have a decomposition of

|(\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast}|
:

(107)

|(\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast}|=|((\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{w}}|\amalg|((\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{nw}}|.

Lemma 8.4
.

(1)
A node
\alpha_{i}\in|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}|
is a
white node in
|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|
.

(2)
A node
\alpha_{i}\in|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}|
is not a white node.

(3)
A node
(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in|((\Gamma_{m}^{\uparrow})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{w}}|
is
a white node.

(4)
A node
(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in|((\Gamma_{m}^{\uparrow})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{nw}}|

is not a white node.

Proof.

For statement (1), it is enough to show that
2\alpha_{i}\not\in R
and

\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{i}\not\in R
.
The first condition is an immediate consequence of the condition

2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\not\in R/G
. Since
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|
is a subset of
R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
,
the second condition is satisfied for every node in
|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|
.

For
\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|\setminus|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}|
, we have

2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\in R/G
. If
2\alpha_{i}\in R
,
\alpha_{i}
is a black node.
Otherwise, it is a gray node. Thus, we have statement (2).

We have statement (3) (resp. (4)) by a similar way to the
proof of statement (1) (resp. (2)). Thus, we completed the proof of the lemma.
∎

There is another decomposition of the set

|(\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast}|
. Set

\displaystyle\big|\big(\big((\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}\big)^{pr}\big)^{\ast}\big|=\big\{(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\,\big|\,\alpha_{i}\in|(\Gamma^{\downarrow}_{m})^{\mathrm{w}}|\big\},

\displaystyle\big|\big(\big((\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}\big)^{pr}\big)^{\ast}\big|=\big\{(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\,\big|\,\alpha_{i}\in|(\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}|\big\},

respectively. Then, we have

(108)

|(\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast}|=\big|\big(\big((\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}\big)^{pr}\big)^{\ast}\big|\amalg\big|\big(\big((\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}\big)^{pr}\big)^{\ast}\big|.

Lemma 8.5
.

The decomposition (
108
) coincides with
the decomposition (
107
). That is, we have

\big|\big(\big((\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}\big)^{pr}\big)^{\ast}\big|=|((\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{w}}|\quad\text{and}\quad[\big|\big(\big((\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}\big)^{pr}\big)^{\ast}\big|=|((\Gamma^{\uparrow}_{m})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{nw}}|.

In other words, for
\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow}|
,

\begin{array}[]{lllllllll}\text{$\alpha_{i}$ is a white node}&\Longleftrightarrow&(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in|(\Gamma_{m}^{\uparrow})^{\ast}|\text{ is a white node},\\[3.0pt]
\text{$\alpha_{i}$ is not a white node}&\Longleftrightarrow&(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in|(\Gamma_{m}^{\uparrow})^{\ast}|\text{ is not a white node}.\end{array}

Proof.

The statement is an immediate consequence of the definition of the map

(\,\cdot\,)^{pr}:R\to R^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
, and the equivalences in
(
86
).
∎

Now, we have a decomposition of the set

|\Gamma(R,G)|
of nodes of
\Gamma(R,G)
:

(109)

|\Gamma(R,G)|=\big(\,|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}|\,\amalg\,|((\Gamma_{m}^{\uparrow})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{w}}|\,\big)\,\amalg\,\big(\,|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}|\,\amalg\,|((\Gamma_{m}^{\uparrow})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{nw}}|\,\big).

The above lemmas imply that

•

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}(R,G)|:=|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}|\,\amalg\,|((\Gamma_{m}^{\uparrow})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{w}}|

is the set of all white nodes in

|\Gamma(R,G)|
, and

•

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}(R,G)|:=|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}|\,\amalg\,|((\Gamma_{m}^{\uparrow})^{\ast})^{\mathrm{nw}}|

is the set of all colored nodes in
|\Gamma(R,G)|
.

Here and after, we denote

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|=|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}|,\quad|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}|=|(\Gamma^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}|,\quad|\Gamma_{m}^{\mathrm{w}}|=|(\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{w}}|,\quad|\Gamma_{m}^{\mathrm{nw}}|=|(\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow})^{\mathrm{nw}}|

for simplicity.

Notice that the subset
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|
of
|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|
is determined by
the information on the affine root system
R/G
.
The explicit form of
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|
is given as follows:

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|=\begin{cases}\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l-1}\}&\text{if $R/G$ is of type $BCC_{l},\,C^{\vee}BC_{l},\,BB^{\vee}_{l}$},\\
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{l-1}\}&\text{if $R/G$ is of type $C^{\vee}C_{l}$}.\end{cases}

Note that
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|=\emptyset
if
R/G
is of type
C^{\vee}C_{1}
.

To classify the diagrams
\Gamma(R,G)
,
we divide the problem into the following
two pieces:

(a)

What is the shape of
\Gamma(R,G)
?

(b)

What is the color of each node of
\Gamma(R,G)
?

Namely, problem (a) is equivalent to determining the subsets

|\Gamma_{m}^{\mathrm{w}}|
of
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|
and
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}_{m}|
of

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}|
, respectively. For problem (b), we already know that

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|
(
resp
.

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}|
)
is the set of all white (
resp
. colored) nodes in
|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|
.
Therefore, the remaining problem is to determine whether the color of the nodes in

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}|
is gray or black.
The goal of this subsection is to solve these problems.

For that purpose, we divide the set
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|
of white nodes in

|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|
into the following two pieces.
A white node
\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|
is called
an
ordinary white node

if
\alpha_{i}
is connected to at least two nodes in
|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|
,
and denote the set of all ordinary white nodes by
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
.

Hence, we have a
decomposition

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|=|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}\amalg\big(|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|\setminus|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}\big).

A node
\alpha_{j}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|\setminus|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
is called a
boundary
white node
.
The explicit form of
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
is given as follows:

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}=\begin{cases}|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|\setminus\{\alpha_{0}\}&\text{if $R/G$ is of type $BCC_{l},\,C^{\vee}BC_{l}$},\\
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|\setminus\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\}&\text{if $R/G$ is of type $BB^{\vee}_{l}$},\\
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|&\text{if $R/G$ is of type $C^{\vee}C_{l}$}.\end{cases}

Note that
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}=\emptyset
if
R/G
is of
type
BB^{\vee}_{2}
, and
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|=|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}=\emptyset

if
R/G
is of type
C^{\vee}C_{1}
.

The following proposition is a first step for the above problem.

Proposition 8.6
.

(1)
Assume both
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{i+1}
are nodes in

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
. One has
m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{\alpha_{i+1}}
.

(2)
Assume
R/G
is not of type
C^{\vee}C_{l}
.
Let
\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
and

\alpha_{j}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|\setminus|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
.
One has
m_{\alpha_{i}}\geq m_{\alpha_{j}}
. Especially, if
R/G
is of type

BB^{\vee}_{l}
, one has
m_{\alpha_{i}}>m_{\alpha_{j}}
.

(3)

Let
\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
and

\alpha_{j}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}|
.
One has
m_{\alpha_{i}}\geq m_{\alpha_{j}}
.

(4)
Let
\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
and

\alpha_{j}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}|
. If

m_{\alpha_{i}}>m_{\alpha_{j}}
, the element
2\alpha_{j}
is a root.

Proof.

(1) Since
2\overline{\alpha_{i}}\not\in R/G
and

2\overline{\alpha_{i+1}}\not\in R/G
,
we have
k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{i})=k(\alpha_{i})
and
k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{i+1})=k(\alpha_{i+1})

by Proposition
7.15
, respectively. Since both
\alpha_{i}
and

\alpha_{i+1}
are middle roots, we have

I(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i+1}^{\vee})=I(\alpha_{i+1},\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=-1
, and

I_{R}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})=I_{R}(\alpha_{i+1},\alpha_{i+1})=4
. It follows from the
first equality that

(110)

k(\alpha_{i})=k(\alpha_{i+1})

by Lemma
2.7
(2).
In addition, we observe that
n_{\alpha_{i}}=n_{\alpha_{i+1}}
for every case
by explicit data in
\lx@sectionsign

B.2
. Thus, we have

m_{\alpha_{i}}=\frac{I_{R}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})}{2k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{i})}n_{\alpha_{i}}=\frac{I_{R}(\alpha_{i+1},\alpha_{i+1})}{2k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{i+1})}n_{\alpha_{i+1}}=m_{\alpha_{i+1}}.

(2) By the conditions
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}\neq\emptyset
and

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|\setminus|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}\neq\emptyset
,
we see that
R/G
should be of type
BCC_{l}\,(l\geq 2),\,C^{\vee}BC_{l}\,(l\geq 2)
or
BB^{\vee}_{l}\,(l\geq 3)
. In each case,
there exists a unique node
\alpha_{i_{0}}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|
such that it connects
to
\alpha_{j}
by a bond in
\Gamma
. Since
m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{\alpha_{i_{0}}}
by
statement (1), it is enough to show the statement under the assumption that

\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i_{0}}
.

By a similar way as in the proof of statement (1), we obtain that

k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{i})=k(\alpha_{i})
and
k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})=k(\alpha_{j})
.
Here, we used the fact that
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{j}
belongs to
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|
.
In the following table, we give the possible list of

\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{j}
. In addition,
n_{\alpha_{i}}
,
n_{\alpha_{j}}
,

I_{R}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})
,
I_{R}(\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j})

I(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})
and
I(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})
are exhibited also.

R/G

\alpha_{i}

n_{\alpha_{i}}

I_{R}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})

\alpha_{j}

n_{\alpha_{j}}

I_{R}(\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j})

I(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})

I(\alpha_{j},\alpha_{i}^{\vee})

Remark 8.7
.

The dual
\alpha^{\vee}=\frac{2\alpha}{I(\alpha,\alpha)}
of
\alpha\in R

depends on a choice of a bilinear form
I
. On the other hand, since there exists
a positive constant
c>0
such that
I=cI_{R}

(
see
[
Sai85
]
(1.11))
,
we have

I(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})=\frac{2I(\alpha,\beta)}{I(\beta,\beta)}=\frac{2cI_{R}(\alpha,\beta)}{cI_{R}(\beta,\beta)}=I_{R}(\alpha,\beta^{\vee_{R}})\quad\text{for every }\alpha,\beta\in R,

where
\beta^{\vee_{R}}:=\frac{2\beta}{I_{R}(\beta,\beta)}
.

By direct computation, we have

m_{\alpha_{i}}=\begin{cases}4/k(\alpha_{i})&\text{if $BCC_{l}\,(l\geq 2)$},\\
2/k(\alpha_{i})&\text{if $C^{\vee}BC_{l}\,(l\geq 2)$},\\
4/k(\alpha_{i})&\text{if $BB^{\vee}_{l}\,(l\geq 3)$},\end{cases}\quad m_{\alpha_{j}}=\begin{cases}4/k(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $BCC_{l}\,(l\geq 2)$},\\
1/k(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $C^{\vee}BC_{l}\,(l\geq 2)$},\\
2/k(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $BB^{\vee}_{l}\,(l\geq 3)$}.\end{cases}

First, assume
R/G
is of type
BCC_{l}\,(l\geq 2)
.
By Lemma
2.7
(2), we have

k(\alpha_{i})|k(\alpha_{j})|2k(\alpha_{i})
.
Therefore,
k(\alpha_{j})=k(\alpha_{i})
or
2k(\alpha_{i})
, and

\begin{cases}m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{\alpha_{j}}&\text{if $k(\alpha_{j})=k(\alpha_{i})$},\\
m_{\alpha_{i}}>m_{\alpha_{j}}&\text{if $k(\alpha_{j})=2k(\alpha_{i})$}.\end{cases}

Second, in the case when
R/G
is of type
C^{\vee}BC_{l}\,(l\geq 2)
, we have

k(\alpha_{i})=2k(\alpha_{j})
or
k(\alpha_{j})
, and

\begin{cases}m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{\alpha_{j}}&\text{if $2k(\alpha_{j})=k(\alpha_{i})$},\\
m_{\alpha_{i}}>m_{\alpha_{j}}&\text{if $k(\alpha_{j})=k(\alpha_{i})$},\end{cases}

by a similar method. Finally, if
R/G
is of type
BB^{\vee}_{l}\,(l\geq 3)
, we have

k(\alpha_{i})=k(\alpha_{j})
by Lemma
2.7
(2).
Therefore, the inequality
m_{\alpha_{i}}>m_{\alpha_{j}}
is obtained, and
we get the desired results.

(3) Note that the condition
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}|\neq\emptyset

is always satisfied. By the condition
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}\neq\emptyset
,
R/G

should be of type
BCC_{l}\,(l\geq 2),\,C^{\vee}BC_{l}\,(l\geq 2),\,BB^{\vee}_{l}\,(l\geq 3)
or
C^{\vee}C_{l}\,(l\geq 2)
. By the same reason as
in the previous case, we may assume that
\alpha_{i}
connects to

\alpha_{j}
by an edge in
\Gamma
, and we obtain that
k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{i})=k(\alpha_{i})
.
In the following table, we give the possible list of

\alpha_{i}
,
\alpha_{j}
, and exhibit
n_{\alpha_{i}}
,
n_{\alpha_{j}}
,

I_{R}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})
,
I_{R}(\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j})

I(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})
and
I(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})
.

R/G

\alpha_{i}

n_{\alpha_{i}}

I_{R}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})

\alpha_{j}

n_{\alpha_{j}}

I_{R}(\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j})

I(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})

I(\alpha_{j},\alpha_{i}^{\vee})

BCC_{l}\,(l\geq 2)

\alpha_{l-1}

2

4

\alpha_{l}

-1

By direct computation, we have

(111)

m_{\alpha_{i}}=\begin{cases}4/k(\alpha_{i})&\text{if $BCC_{l}\,(l\geq 2)$},\\
2/k(\alpha_{i})&\text{if $C^{\vee}BC_{l}\,(l\geq 2)$},\\
4/k(\alpha_{i})&\text{if $BB^{\vee}_{l}\,(l\geq 3)$},\\
2/k(\alpha_{i})&\text{if $C^{\vee}C_{l}\,(l\geq 2)$},\\
\end{cases}\quad m_{\alpha_{j}}=\begin{cases}2/k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $BCC_{l}\,(l\geq 2)$},\\
1/k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $C^{\vee}BC_{l}\,(l\geq 2)$},\\
2/k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $BB^{\vee}_{l}\,(l\geq 3)$},\\
1/k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $C^{\vee}C_{l}\,(l\geq 2)$},\\
\end{cases}

Thus, the desired inequality is equivalent to

(112)

2k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})\geq k(\alpha_{i}).

By the definition of the (elliptic) root system, the element

r_{\alpha_{j}}r_{(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}}(\alpha_{i})
is a root.
By Lemma
7.18
, we have

r_{\alpha_{j}}r_{(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}}(\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{i}-I(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}^{\vee})k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})a=\alpha_{i}+2k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})a\in R.

By Lemma
2.7
(1), we have

k(\alpha_{i})|2k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})
.
This shows (
112
), and we get statement (3).

(4) The assumption
m_{\alpha_{i}}>m_{\alpha_{j}}
is equivalent to

2k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})>k(\alpha_{i}).

By Lemma
7.14
and (
84
), we have

(113)

k(\alpha_{i})<2k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})=k^{\mathrm{nr}}((\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}k((\alpha^{\prime}_{j})^{\ast})=\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha^{\prime}_{j})&\text{if $2\alpha_{j}\not\in R$},\\
k((\alpha^{\prime}_{j})^{\ast})=k(\alpha^{\prime}_{j})&\text{if $2\alpha_{j}\in R$}.\end{cases}

On the other hand, since
\alpha_{j}^{\prime}
is a long root, and
\alpha_{i}
is a middle
root,
I(\alpha^{\prime}_{j},\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=-2
.
By Lemma
2.7
(2), we have

k(\alpha^{\prime}_{j})|I(\alpha^{\prime}_{j},\alpha_{i}^{\vee})k(\alpha_{i})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad k(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})|2k(\alpha_{i}).

That is, there exists a positive integer
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that

mk(\alpha^{\prime}_{j})=2k(\alpha_{i})
.

Assume
2\alpha_{j}\not\in R
. By (
113
) and
the above consequence, we have

mk(\alpha^{\prime}_{j})<k(\alpha^{\prime}_{j})\quad\text{for $m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}$}.

This is a contradiction. Therefore, the element
2\alpha_{j}
should belong to
R
,
and the proof of the proposition is completed.
∎

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
and
\alpha_{j}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}|
.

(1)
If
m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{\alpha_{j}}
, we have

(114)

k(\alpha_{i})=\begin{cases}k(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R$},\\
2k(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R$}.\end{cases}

(2)
If
m_{\alpha_{i}}>m_{\alpha_{j}}
,
we have

(115)

k(\alpha_{i})=k(\alpha_{j})

and
\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}
is a root.

Proof.

(1) As already shown in the proof of the proposition, the condition

m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{\alpha_{j}}
is equivalent to
2k^{nr}(\alpha_{j})=k(\alpha_{i})
.
By this equality and (
85
), statement (1) follows
immediately.

(2) We already proved that the condition
m_{\alpha_{i}}>m_{\alpha_{j}}
implies

2\alpha_{j}\in R
in statement (4) of the above proposition.
Furthermore, by a similar argument to the above proof, we have

mk(\alpha^{\prime}_{j})<2k(\alpha^{\prime}_{j})
for
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
Therefore, the integer
m
should be equal to
1
, and we get

k(\alpha^{\prime}_{j})=2k(\alpha_{i})
.
In addition, by Proposition
7.17
and

2\alpha_{j}\in R
, we have

\displaystyle 2k(\alpha_{i})=k(\alpha^{\prime}_{j})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}k(\alpha_{j})&\text{if }\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R,\\
2k(\alpha_{j})&\text{if }\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R.\end{cases}

On the other hand, by Lemma
2.7
(2), we have

k(\alpha_{j})|k(\alpha_{i})|2k(\alpha_{j})
. From this, we obtain

k(\alpha_{i})=k(\alpha_{j})\,\text{or}\,\,2k(\alpha_{j}).

Therefore, the only possible case is
k(\alpha_{i})=k(\alpha_{j})
and we get

\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
, as desired.
∎

8.3.
Lowest rank cases

(R,G)
is assumed to be

is of type

,

,

. In the following table, we give

.

, both

and

belong to
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}|
and

.

R/G

\alpha_{i}

n_{\alpha_{i}}

(116)

m_{\alpha_{i}}=\begin{cases}4/k(\alpha_{i})&\text{if $BCC_{1}$},\\
1/k(\alpha_{i})&\text{if $C^{\vee}BC_{1}$},\\
2/k(\alpha_{i})&\text{if $BB^{\vee}_{2}$},\end{cases}\quad m_{\alpha_{j}}=\begin{cases}2/k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $BCC_{1}$},\\
1/k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $C^{\vee}BC_{1}$},\\
2/k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $BB^{\vee}_{2}$},\\
1/k^{\mathrm{nr}}(\alpha_{j})&\text{if $C^{\vee}C_{1}$},\\
\end{cases}

Proposition 8.9
.

(1)
Assume
R/G
is of type
BCC_{1}
or
C^{\vee}BC_{1}
.

(a)

If
m_{\alpha_{0}}=m_{\alpha_{1}}
, one has

k(\alpha_{1})/k(\alpha_{0})=-\frac{I(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0}^{\vee})}{t},\quad\text{where }t=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R.\\
2&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R.\\
\end{cases}

(b)

If
m_{\alpha_{0}}<m_{\alpha_{1}}
, then

k(\alpha_{1})/k(\alpha_{0})=-\frac{I(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0}^{\vee})}{t},\quad\text{where }t=\begin{cases}2&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R.\\
4&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R.\\
\end{cases}

(c)

If
m_{\alpha_{0}}>m_{\alpha_{1}}
, one has

2\alpha_{1}\in R,\ \frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
and

k(\alpha_{1})/k(\alpha_{0})=-I(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0}^{\vee}).

(2)
Assume
R/G
is of type
BB_{2}^{\vee}
.

(a)

One has
m_{\alpha_{2}}=m_{max}
.

(b)

If
m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{max}
for
i=0
or
1
, then
one has

2\alpha_{2}\in R,\ \frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
and

k(\alpha_{i})=k(\alpha_{2}).

Furthermore, if
m_{\alpha_{j}}<m_{max}
for
j\in\{0,1\}
such that
j\neq i
, one has

k(\alpha_{j})=2k(\alpha_{2}).

(c)

If both
m_{\alpha_{0}}
and
m_{\alpha_{1}}
are strictly smaller than

m_{max}
, then

k(\alpha_{2})/k(\alpha_{i})=\dfrac{1}{t},\quad\text{where }t=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R,\\
2&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R,\end{cases}\quad\text{for }i=0,1.

(3)
Assume
R/G
is of type
C^{\vee}C_{1}
and let
i,j
be indices such that

\{i,j\}=\{0,1\}
.

(a)

If
m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{\alpha_{j}}
, one has

k(\alpha_{j})/k(\alpha_{i})=\dfrac{2}{t},\quad\text{where }t=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R\text{ and }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R,\\
4&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R\text{ and }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R,\\
2&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(b)

If
m_{\alpha_{i}}<m_{\alpha_{j}}
, then one has

2\alpha_{i}\in R,\ \frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
and

k(\alpha_{j})/k(\alpha_{i})=\dfrac{2}{t},\quad\text{where }t=\begin{cases}2&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R\\
4&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R.\end{cases}

Proof.

(1) Since the statements for
C^{\vee}BC_{1}
are obtained by a similar
way, we give only the proof for
BCC_{1}
. By (
116
) and Corollary

7.16
, we have

(117)

m_{\alpha_{0}}\lesseqqgtr m_{\alpha_{1}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad k(\alpha_{1})/k(\alpha_{0})\lesseqqgtr\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R,\\
\tfrac{1}{2}&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R.\end{cases}

On the other hand, by Lemma
2.7
(2), we have

\begin{array}[]{lllll}k(\alpha_{0})|I(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}^{\vee})k(\alpha_{1})&\Longleftrightarrow&k(\alpha_{0})|4k(\alpha_{1}),\\[3.0pt]
k(\alpha_{1})|I(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0}^{\vee})k(\alpha_{0})&\Longleftrightarrow&k(\alpha_{1})|k(\alpha_{0}).\end{array}

Therefore,

(118)

the number
k(\alpha_{1})/k(\alpha_{0})
should be equal to
1,1/2
or
1/4
.

(a) Assume
m_{\alpha_{0}}=m_{\alpha_{1}}
. As
I(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{0}^{\vee})=-1
,
statement (a) is obtained by (
117
).

(b) Assume
m_{\alpha_{0}}<m_{\alpha_{1}}
.

i) If
\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R
, we have
k(\alpha_{1})/k(\alpha_{0})=1/2
or

1/4
by (
117
) and (
118
).
Assume
k(\alpha_{1})/k(\alpha_{0})=1/4
.
By Lemma
7.18
, we have

\displaystyle r_{\alpha_{1}}r_{(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}}(\alpha_{0})

\displaystyle=\alpha_{0}-I(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}^{\vee})k^{nr}(\alpha_{1})a

\displaystyle=\alpha_{0}+2k(\alpha_{1})a\quad(\because\ \tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{0}+\tfrac{1}{2}k(\alpha_{0})a\quad(\because\ k(\alpha_{1})/k(\alpha_{0})=1/4).

Since
r_{\alpha_{1}}r_{(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}}(\alpha_{0})

is a root, the above formula says that
\tfrac{1}{2}k(\alpha_{0})a
belongs to the
counting set
K_{G}(\alpha_{0})
. This contradicts the minimality of
k(\alpha_{0})
.
Thus, the number
k(\alpha_{1})/k(\alpha_{0})
should be equal to
1/2
.

ii) If
\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
, the number
k(\alpha_{1})/k(\alpha_{0})

should be equal to
1/4
by (
117
) and
(
118
), as desired.

(c) Assume
m_{\alpha_{0}}>m_{\alpha_{1}}
. By (
118
), we have

\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
and
k(\alpha_{1})/k(\alpha_{0})=1
. By Lemma

7.18
, we have

\displaystyle r_{\alpha_{0}}r_{(\alpha_{0}^{\prime})^{\ast}}\big((\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\big)

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}-I\big((\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast},\alpha_{0}^{\vee}\big)k(\alpha_{0})a

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}+2k(\alpha_{0})a\quad(\because\ 2\overline{\alpha_{0}}\not\in R/G)

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}+2k(\alpha_{1})a\quad(\because\ k(\alpha_{1})/k(\alpha_{0})=1).

Assume
2\alpha_{1}\not\in R
. Then, by Proposition
7.17
, we
get
k\big((\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\big)=4k(\alpha_{1})
. Substituting it to the above formula,
it follows that

r_{\alpha_{0}}r_{(\alpha_{0}^{\prime})^{\ast}}\big((\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\big)=(\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}+\tfrac{1}{2}k\big((\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\big)a

is a root. This contradicts to the minimality of the counting number

k\big((\alpha_{1}^{\prime})^{\ast}\big)
. Thus, the element
2\alpha_{1}
should
belong to
R
,
and we completed the proof of statement (1).

(2) Similar to the previous case, we have

(119)

m_{\alpha_{i}}\lesseqqgtr m_{\alpha_{2}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad k(\alpha_{2})/k(\alpha_{i})\lesseqqgtr\begin{cases}2&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R,\\
1&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R,\end{cases}\quad\text{for }i=0,1,

by (
116
) and Corollary
7.16
, and

(120)

the number
k(\alpha_{2})/k(\alpha_{i})
should be equal to
1
or
1/2
,

by Lemma
2.7
(2).

(a) Assume
m_{\alpha_{i}}>m_{\alpha_{2}}
.
By (
119
), we have
k(\alpha_{2})>k(\alpha_{i})
. However, this
contradicts (
120
). Thus, it follows that

m_{\alpha_{i}}\leq m_{\alpha_{2}}
and statement (a) holds.

(b) Assume
m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{max}\ (=m_{\alpha_{2}})
.
If
\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R
,
we have
k(\alpha_{2})=2k(\alpha_{i})
by (
119
). Therefore, it follows
from Lemma
7.18
that

r_{\alpha_{i}}r_{(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}}(\alpha_{2})=\alpha_{2}-I(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{i}^{\vee})k^{nr}(\alpha_{i})a=\alpha_{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}k(\alpha_{2})a.

This contradicts the minimality of
k(\alpha_{2})
. Therefore,

\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
and
k(\alpha_{2})=k(\alpha_{i})
. Hence, we have

r_{\alpha_{i}}r_{(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}}\big((\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\big)=(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}-I\big((\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast},\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\big)k^{nr}(\alpha_{i})a=(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}+2k(\alpha_{2})a.

Assume
2\alpha_{2}\not\in R
.
Then, one has
k\big((\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\big)=4k(\alpha_{2})
by
Proposition
7.17
. Substituting this to the above equality, one has

r_{\alpha_{i}}r_{(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}}\big((\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\big)=(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}+\tfrac{1}{2}k\big((\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\big)a.

This contradicts to the minimality of
k\big((\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\big)
,
and we have
2\alpha_{2}\in R
.

Assume
m_{\alpha_{j}}<m_{max}
for
j\in\{0,1\}
such that
j\neq i
.
Since
\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
, the number
k(\alpha_{2})/k(\alpha_{j})
should
be equal to
\tfrac{1}{2}
by (
119
) and (
120
),
as desired.

(c) Assume
m_{\alpha_{i}}<m_{max}
for
i=0,1
.

i) If
\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R
, we have
k(\alpha_{2})/k(\alpha_{i})=1/2
or

1
by (
119
) and (
120
).
Furthermore, it follows from Corollary
7.16
that

k^{nr}(\alpha_{2})=\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha_{2})
.
Assume
k(\alpha_{2})/k(\alpha_{i})=1/2
. By Lemma
7.18
, we have

\displaystyle r_{\alpha_{2}}r_{(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}}(\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i}-I(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{2}^{\vee})k^{nr}(\alpha_{2})a=\alpha_{i}+k(\alpha_{2})a=\alpha_{i}+\frac{1}{2}k(\alpha_{i})a.

Since
r_{\alpha_{2}}r_{(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}}(\alpha_{i})\in R
, the above formula
contradicts
the minimality of
k(\alpha_{i})
. Therefore, we get
k(\alpha_{2})/k(\alpha_{i})=1
.

ii) If
\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{2}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R
, we have
k(\alpha_{2})/k(\alpha_{i})=1/2
by
(
119
) and (
120
).

Thus, we proved the statement.

(3) We have

(121)

m_{\alpha_{i}}\lesseqqgtr m_{\alpha_{j}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad k(\alpha_{j})/k(\alpha_{i})\lesseqqgtr\begin{cases}2&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R\text{ and }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R,\\
\frac{1}{2}&\text{if }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in R\text{ and }\tfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{j}^{\prime})^{\ast}\in R,\\
1&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

by (
116
) and Corollary
7.16
, and

(122)

the number
k(\alpha_{j})/k(\alpha_{i})
should be equal to
2,1
or
1/2
,

by Lemma
2.7
(2). By using (
121
) and
(
122
), one has the statements by a similar argument to
the previous cases. We leave a detailed proof to the reader.
∎

Remark 8.10
.

Since there is a symmetry of exchanging
\alpha_{0}
with
\alpha_{1}
when

R/G
is of type
BB^{\vee}_{2}
or
C^{\vee}C_{1}
, we may assume that

m_{\alpha_{0}}\leq m_{\alpha_{1}}
for these cases.

8.4.
Classification in terms of diagrams

Let us translate the results of Proposition
8.6
and
Proposition
8.9
into the
language of the diagram
\Gamma^{\downarrow}
.
We say
\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|
is a

collapsed node
if

(\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast}\not\in|\Gamma(R,G)|
.
We note that this condition is equivalent to
m_{\alpha_{i}}<m_{max}
.

Let
(R,G)
be a mERS whose quotient root system
R/G
is of type

BCC_{l}\,(l\geq 1),\,C^{\vee}BC_{l}\,(l\geq 1),\,BB^{\vee}_{l}\,(l\geq 2)
or

C^{\vee}C_{l}\,(l\geq 1)
. A first observation obtained
from these propositions is

(a)

for every
\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
, we have

m_{\alpha_{i}}=m_{max}
. That is,
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
is a subset of

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}_{m}|
.

Since
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}=|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|
for the case when
R/G
is of type

C^{\vee}C_{l}\,(l\geq 2)
, we have
|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}=|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}_{m}|
.
Furthermore, Proposition
8.6
(2) says that, if
R/G
is of
type
BB^{\vee}_{l}\,(l\geq 3)
, a node
\alpha_{j}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|\setminus|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
is a collapsed node. Therefore, we get

|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}=|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}_{m}|
in this case.

The second observation is

(b)

if
\alpha_{j}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}|
is a collapsed node,
it is a black node.

This is nothing but statement (4) of Proposition
8.6
,
statements (1) (c) and (3) (b) of Proposition
8.9
.
when
R/G
is of type
BB^{\vee}_{2}
is exceptional. In this case, we
observe that

(c-1)

a colored node
\alpha_{2}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{nw}}|
is
not a collapsed node for any case, and

(c-2)

if there is a non-collapsed node
\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|
, every
colored node in
|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|
is a black node.

Compiling the above results, we have a “possible list” of
the elliptic diagrams for mERSs with non-reduced affine quotients.
Furthermore, the counting numbers

k(\alpha)\ (\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})

are uniquely determined by the condition

\mathrm{g.c.d.}\{k(\alpha)\,|\,\alpha\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\}=1

(Corollary
7.22
). By Proposition
7.17
,

k((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast})\ ((\alpha^{\prime})^{\ast}\in(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})^{\ast})
are determined also.
The results are exhibited in the following tables.

1. The case when
R/G
is of type
BCC_{l}\,(l\geq 1)
,

C^{\vee}BC_{l}\ (l\geq 1)
or
BB_{l}^{\vee}\ (l\geq 3)
.

In this case,

\displaystyle|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l-1}\}&\text{if $R/G$ is of type
$BCC_{l}\,(l\geq 1)$ or $C^{\vee}BC_{l}\ (l\geq 1)$,}\\
\{\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{l-1}\}&\text{if $R/G$ is of type
$BB_{l}^{\vee}\ (l\geq 3)$,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|

\displaystyle=\{\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{l-1}\}\quad\text{and}\quad|\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow}|=|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}\amalg\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{non-collapsed nodes}\\
\text{in the following tables}\end{array}\right\}.

In the following tables, we exhibit (a) the color of
\alpha_{l}
;
(b) the color of
(\alpha_{l}^{\prime})^{\ast}
;
(c)
k(\alpha_{j})
for
\alpha_{j}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|\setminus|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
;
(d)
k(\alpha_{i})
for
\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|_{o}
;
(e)
k(\alpha_{l})
;
(f)
k((\alpha_{l}^{\prime})^{\ast})
.

Remark 8.11
.

(1)
For every case, the following equality holds
:

k((\alpha_{i}^{\prime})^{\ast})=k(\alpha_{i})\quad\text{for }\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\mathrm{w}}|.

Therefore, for every element of
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}\cup\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}}
, its counting number is given
in the above tables.

(2)
In
|\Gamma^{\downarrow}|
, a non-collapsed node should exist. By this condition,
we need to add the constraint
l\geq 2
in the following three cases
:

No.10 in Table
4
, No. 10 in Table
5
and
No. 15 in Table
8
.

Now, we have one of the main results of this article.

Theorem 8.12
.

Let
(R,G)
be a mERS with the non-reduced quotient
R/G
and

(\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}},\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{m}}^{c}})

a paired simple system of
(R,G)
.
After a suitable choice of a numbering of the set
\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}=\{\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{l}\}
,
its elliptic diagram
\Gamma(R,G)
is isomorphic to one in the above list.

We observe that the diagrams appeared in the list (Tables
4

\sim

8
) above are not isomorphic to
each other. Furthermore, by Proposition

8.2
, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 8.13
.

The isomorphism classes of mERSs with non-reduced affine quotients
are classified by their elliptic diagrams.

However, we note that Theorem
8.12
and
Corollary
8.13

does not

give a complete solution for the
classification problem of mERSs with non-reduced affine quotients. Indeed,
we
have not
proved yet the existence of a mERS whose elliptic diagram
is isomorphic to every diagram in the above list.
For getting the complete list of such mERSs, we need to solve the “existence
problem”. We will discuss this problem in
\lx@sectionsign

8.5
.

Finally, we give some comments on the exponents and
counting numbers.

Lemma 8.14
.

Let
(R,G)
be a mERSs with non-reduced affine quotient.

(1)
One has
m_{\alpha_{i}}=\frac{1}{2}m_{max}
for every
\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma^{\downarrow}\setminus\Gamma_{m}|
.

(2)
One has
g.c.d.\{k(\alpha_{i})\,|\,\alpha_{i}\in|\Gamma_{m}^{\downarrow}|\}=1
.

This lemma is an easy observation following from Tables
4

\sim

8
.

8.5.
Remarks on the classification theorem

As we already mentioned at the end of
\lx@sectionsign

8.4
,
for getting the complete list of mERS with non-reduced affine quotients,
we need to solve the following problem.

Problem 1
.

For every diagram in Tables
4

\sim

8

preceding Theorem
8.12
, does there
exist a mERS whose elliptic diagram is isomorphic to it?

In conclusion, the answer is YES.
That is, we have the main theorem of this article.

Theorem 8.15
.

Tables
4

\sim

8
preceding Theorem

8.12

give the complete list of isomorphism classes of mERSs with non-reduced affine
quotients.

There are at least two ways for proving Theorem
8.15
.

(i)

Method 1: Comparison with the results of Chapter 2;

(ii)

Method 2: Direct computation;

on

F_{a}=\oplus_{i=0}^{l}\mathbb{R}\alpha_{i}
, and let

]

F=F_{a}\oplus\mathbb{R}a
, and let

I_{F}:F\times F\to\mathbb{R}
be a symmetric bilinear form on
F
such that

I_{F}|_{F_{a}\times F_{a}}=I_{F_{a}}
and
\mathbb{R}a\subset\mathrm{rad}(I_{F})
.

2.

Let
r_{\alpha_{i}}:F\to F
be the reflection attached
to
\alpha_{i}\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}
, and
W([)\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}}]
be the group generated by the reflections

r_{\alpha_{i}}\ (\alpha_{i}\in\Pi^{\,\underline{\mathrm{d}}^{c}})
.

From the data in Table
4
, one can construct the

(see

below).

.

), the subsets
